INTRODUCTION 
 
 
I, the Chairperson of the Committee on External Affairs having been authorized by the 
Committee to submit the Report on their behalf, present this Twelfth Report of the Committee 
on External Affairs (2025-26) on Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Ministry of External 
Affairs. 
2.   The Committee heard the views of the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs 
at their Twelfth Sitting held on 23 February, 2026. 
3.  The Committee wish to express their gratitude to the officers of the Ministry of External 
Affairs for placing before them the material and information that the Committee desired and 
also appearing before the Committee for placing their considered views before them in 
connection with the examination of Demands for Grants (2026-27).   
4. The Committee place on record their appreciation for the assistance 
rendered to them by the officials of the Lok Sabha Secretariat attached to the Committee 
5.  The Report was considered and adopted by the Committee at their Sixteenth Sitting held 
on 12 March, 2026. 
6.  The Minutes of the Sitting of the Committee held on 23 February 2026 and 12 March 2026 
are given in Appendix-I and II to the Report. 
7.   For facility of reference, the Observations/Recommendations of the Committee have 
been printed in bold.

CHAPTER 1 
ANALYSIS OF DEMANDS FOR GRANTS (2026-27) 
 The Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) formulates and conducts India’s foreign 
policy within the framework of our values and principles of fostering international 
peace and cooperation with the overall objective of meeting India’s strategic aims 
and aspirations as well as to secure our national interests. In pursuance of its 
mandated objectives, the MEA headquarters in New Delhi is assisted by 231 
Missions and Posts abroad, its Branch Secretariats and 37 Passport Offices in India, 
and autonomous bodies funded by it. The Ministry is also supported by other 
Ministries and State Governments which also contribute to India’s international 
engagement in consultation and coordination with MEA. 
1.2 Under the Constitution of India [Article 113 (2)], estimates of expenditure is 
submitted to Lok Sabha in the form of Demands for Grants. The Demands for Grants 
of the Ministry of External Affairs (Demand No. 29) was laid on the table of the House 
on 12 February 2026.  
I. OVERALL BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
1.3 MEA’s budget allocation in Budget Estimates (BE) 2025-26 was Rs. 20516.61 
crore. The allocation in Revised Estimates (RE) 2025-26 was Rs. 21742.74 crore and 
the actual expenditure of the Ministry in FY 2025-26 (upto December 2025) was Rs. 
14748.99 crore (71.89% of BE 2025-26 & 67.83 % of RE 2025-26). The allocation in 
BE 2026-27 is Rs 22118.97 crore, which is 7.81% more than BE 2025-26 allocation 
and 1.73% more than RE 2025-26 allocation. The total budgetary allocation during 
the last five years along with the BE and RE for the respective years including 
Actuals & shortfall/excess in utilization of RE is as follows:

3 | P a g e 
 
1.6 Regarding the percentage of GDP allocated to MEA during the last ten years, 
the Ministry responded that budget allocation as % of GDP is not maintained in the 
Ministry.  
1.7 Over the issue of MEA’s percentage allocation to GOI’s budget, the Foreign 
Secretary during testimony made the following submission on 23 February, 2026: 
“The Hon’ble Committee has also underlined the need for greater budgetary 
allocation, underscoring the decreasing share of the MEA’s allocation as a 
percentage of the overall GOI Budget. Our Budget for Financial Year 2026-27 
represents roughly 0.41 per cent of the total Union Budget, which is roughly 
the same as 2025-26. We should also note that even as our BE allocation has 
been steadily increasing over the years, so has the Budget of the 
Government. Even though our proportion remains at a constant figure, our 
actual allocations are increasing, and this is what has enabled us to meet our 
international obligations more effectively.” 
 
1.8 When asked to compare with corresponding ratios for select major countries 
with comparable global engagements, the Ministry submitted that MEA’s BE 2026-27 
is Rs 22,118.97 crore, representing roughly 0.41% of the total Union Budget. As a 
percentage of GDP, this allocation is around 0.06% based on the estimated GDP of 
approx. Rs 342 lakh crore for FY 2025-26 (actual figures not yet finalized). Official 
percentage to GDP data of major countries is not available in public domain. 
Secondary data from other sources such as research institutes and think tanks have 
observed that countries like US, UK, Singapore, Germany, France are allocating 
foreign affairs budget in the range 0.15 - 0.30% of their GDPs. It may be noted that 
fiscal years, budget categorizations and data availability vary therefore, this is also 
indicative. In FY 2025-26, the US’s International Affairs Budget is US $ 56.8 billion 
(approx. Rs 4.71 lakh Crore) while UK’s foreign, commonwealth and development 
office’s budget allocation for FY 2025-26 is around US $ 15.5 billion (approx. Rs 1.29 
lakh crore).              
1.9 Looking at the MEA’s standing, the Ministry was specifically asked whether 
foreign affairs have been perceived as requiring fewer resources in comparison to 
other high-priority sectors such as education, defense, etc. The Ministry responded 
that each Ministry makes demands for grants based on its own requirements and 
allocations are accordingly made by Ministry of Finance as per its assessment. The 
demand and allocations of various Ministries may not be comparable as each 

4 | P a g e 
 
Ministry’s requirements would be based on their own ongoing and planned 
programmes.

1.11 The Budget Estimate (BE) for the Ministry of External Affairs for the year 2026-
27 is Rs. 22118.97crores, reflecting an increase of 7.81% compared to the BE for 
2025-26 and 1.73% compared to the RE for 2025-26. On being asked about the key 
initiatives, projects, and schemes proposed to be undertaken within this allocation, 
the Ministry furnished the following response: 
Revenue Section 
(i) For BE 2026-27, an increase of Rs 853.08 crore compared to BE 2025-26 
and Rs 426.05 crore compared to RE 2025-26 under the head “Embassies and

Mission” is due to opening of new Missions and Posts abroad. Funds have been 
allocated after accounting for numerous variables that have an inflationary or 
deflationary impact on the costs associated with establishing embassies, such as 
currency exchange rates. 
(ii) For BE 2026-27, an increase of Rs 199 crore compared to BE 2025-26 
and Rs 186 crore compared to RE 2025-26 under the head “International 
Conference/Meetings” to meet the expenditure on organising BRICS related 
events, including the Summit level meeting to be held this year when India takes 
over BRICS Presidency. 
(iii) For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 125.31 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “International Co-operation”. The increased demand 
caters to the mandatory contribution to the United Nation Organisation, for 
special programmes under Trilateral Development Co-operation, etc. 
(iv) For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 102.64 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “Technical and Economic Co-operation with Other 
Countries”. The increased demand caters to India’s aid and assistance 
programmes in India’s immediate and extended neighbourhood and in countries 
in South East Asia, Central Asia, Africa, Latin America and the Caribbean, and 
the Indo-Pacific region. It also caters for Aid for Disaster Relief and contributions 
to various regional funds. Aid programmes are influenced by the dynamics of 
India’s national interest, diplomatic engagements and bilateral relations. 
Capital Section 
(i) For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 176 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “Capital Outlay on Public Works”. The increased 
demand is due to planned acquisition of properties abroad as well as in India for 
construction of buildings for RPOs.  
(ii) For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 70 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “Capital Outlay on Housing”. The increased demand is 
attributed to ongoing construction of Chancery-cum-residential complexes in 
Phuentsholing (Bhutan), Addis Ababa (Ethiopia), Kuala Lampur (Malaysia), 
Jakarta (Indonesia).   
(iii) For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 144.92 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 and Rs 176/92 crore RE 2025-26 under the head “Loans to Govt of 
Bhutan” on account of expected expenditure towards Puna – I Hydroelectric 
Project and Puna – II Hydroelectric Projects.    
1.12 On the Committee’s specific query about inclusion of Line of Credit (LoC) 
provisioning for Exim Bank in the MEA budget, the Ministry responded that BE 2026-27 
does not include LoC provisioning for EXIM Bank.    

6 | P a g e 
 
1.13 Responding to this query during evidence on 23 February 2026, the Foreign 
Secretary stated as under: 
“Sir, the Hon’ble Committee is already aware that we have, in the past years, 
also had provisioning amounts reflected in our Budget. Last year’s Budget did 
not have a reflection of this. The provisioning amount is an expenditure that is 
charged to the Guaranteed Redemption Fund, which is a notional expenditure 
and an adjustment of accounts between different branches of Government. 
As per the directions of the Reserve Bank of India and the Ministry of Finance, 
the MEA is required to provide from its Budget the payments that are due to 
EXIM Bank of India in lieu of what they are due to receive against the lines of 
credit that they have extended to foreign countries at our recommendation. 
The recommendation of the hon. Committee that the provisioning for non-
performing loans be allocated outside the regular MEA Budget is important. 
We will ensure that our core responsibilities are highlighted and receive 
adequate funding. We remain engaged with the RBI and the Ministry of 
Finance on this issue.” 
1.14 In its Fifth Report on DFG 2025-26, the Committee had recommended a 
budgetary enhancement of ‘at least 20 % in this fiscal year to meet the expanding 
needs and ensure more effective engagement on the global stage’. The Committee 
enquired whether the issue was taken up with the Ministry of Finance. The Ministry 
responded that in line with Hon’ble Committee’s recommendation, the matter was 
formally (vide a letter) brought to the notice of Ministry of Finance by the Foreign 
Secretary before the pre-budget meetings held for deciding RE 2025-26 and BE 
2026-27 allocations. As a result, the Ministry’s BE allocation for FY 2026-27 has been 
increased by 7.81 % over BE 2025-26.   
1.15 As regard to the adequacy of the allocated budget for the financial year to 
meet the expanding mandate and increasing global role of the Ministry of External 
Affairs (MEA), the Ministry stated that anticipation of expenditure progress in the 
fiscal year is regularly assessed, which makes budgetary demand and allocation a 
dynamic process. Instruments for re-appropriating money, such as Supplementary 
requests, permit re-prioritization and re-allocation of funds in order to allocate 
budgetary resources fully and effectively. Therefore, funding provided to the Ministry 
in BE 2026–27 reflects a fair evaluation of our current international obligations, 
predicted spending growth, and realistic assessment of requirements. The quarterly 
budgetary and spending evaluations put in place by the Ministry in the form of 

7 | P a g e 
 
Quarterly Budget Management Meetings reinforce the beneficial reallocation of 
budgetary distribution with a preference towards the Ministry's development 
partnership portfolio. For FY 2026-27, additional allocations, if required will be 
suitably taken up with Ministry of Finance at RE stage. 
1.16 Explicating the key factors that contributed to the upward revision in RE 
allocations during 2025-26, the Ministry stated that the upward revision in RE 
allocations is majorly attributed to Contingency Advance taken to cater to India’s 
contribution towards Chabahar Port (Rs 300 crore), expenditure on opening of new 
Missions and Posts abroad, increase in service charges related to Passport Seva 
Project, India’s committed contributions to UN and other international organizations, 
increase in developmental assistance offered to Mauritius (including budgetary 
support), Maldives and Nepal, increase in establishment expenditure in Headquarters 
during the current Financial Year. MEA’s budgetary expenditure up to December 
2025 is Rs 14,789.99 crore from BE allocation of Rs 20,516.61 crore, which is 72% 
spending at the end of three quarters. By end-January 2026, MEA has spent more 
than 76% of the revised allocation (which is 1226.13 crore more than BE). MEA 
therefore is on track to fully utilize its budget by end of the current Financial Year.

1.19 The Committee wanted to know whether the Ministry’s budget has been 
adapted to address emerging global challenges such as cyber security, climate 
change, public health crises, and advancements in emerging technologies. The 
Ministry stated that its budget includes various components that relate to addressing 
emerging global challenges. For instance, the autonomous policy research bodies 
funded by the Ministry, viz. Indian Council for World Affairs (ICWA) and Research 
and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS), undertake several research 
and academic activities on a broad range of subjects, including cyber security, 
climate change, public health, emerging technologies, migration and mobility. 
Ministry’s Multilateral Economic Relations Programme budget head caters to India’s 
contribution to the South Centre, Geneva for supporting climate change activities. 
The Quad Cooperation budget head supports initiatives in the Indo-Pacific region 
relating to climate change and public health such as off-grid solar projects and the 
Quad Cancer Moonshot for providing HPV sampling kits, detection kits, and cervical 
cancer vaccines. The ITEC programme offers training courses in contemporary

subject matters such as govtech, IT, digital economy, and emerging technologies 
(blockchain, artificial intelligence, etc.). From FY 2024-25, a new head has been 
created labelled “Aid to Tech Infrastructure Project”, to support projects pertaining to 
Digital Public Infrastructure, Small and Medium Enterprises, and green 
technology/solar/ water projects. 
 
1.20 Further, the Ministry has administrative provisions in its budget for actively 
participating in international dialogues and negotiations pertaining to cyber security, 
climate change, public health, and emerging technologies, in close coordination with 
nodal Ministries and agencies in the Government. It may, however, be noted that the 
substantive portfolio relating to these matters reside with the respective line Ministries 
of the Union Government. 
 
1.21 When asked about the main budgetary constraints being faced by the MEA, 
and areas that are underfunded, the latter responded that budgetary demand and 
allocation is a dynamic exercise aided by regular assessment of Ministry’s 
expectation of expenditure progress in a given financial year. Instruments of re-
appropriation of budget, supplementary demands etc., allow re-prioritisation and re-
allocation of funds for full and efficient allocation of budgetary resources. The 
quarterly budgetary and spending evaluations put in place by the Ministry in the form 
of Quarterly Budget Management Meetings reinforce the beneficial reallocation of 
budgetary distribution with a preference towards the Ministry’s development 
partnership portfolio.  
 
1.22 Moreover, Divisions/Spending Units accordingly make all efforts to ensure 
evenly paced expenditure, considering the constraint of Ministry’s spending being in 
foreign countries, where diverse local conditions, inter-alia including geological, 
security, administrative, legal & economic ecosystems and varying project 
development and implementation schedules, posing significant challenge for 
alignment of expenditure to our own budget cycle. Ministry’s budgetary & expenditure 
management thus is unlike any other Ministry in Government, with global diversity of 
various ecosystems, in which our projects are executed & expenditure managed. 
 

11 | P a g e 
 
1.23 As India’s diplomatic presence expands globally with the opening of new 
Missions/Posts, besides the development assistance portfolio, the Ministry’s 
establishment expenditure is expected to rise as will the capital expenditure relating 
to acquisition/construction of suitable properties to house the Chanceries and 
residences.    
 
1.24 In pursuance of the Committee’s persistent recommendation, the Research 
and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS) has examined empirical 
trends in MEA Budget through an inter-Ministerial comparison and cross-country 
comparison of different countries in their Report titled ‘Analysis of Trends of MEA 
Budgetary Allocation”. The Committee were keen to know whether the Ministry has 
utilized the RIS Report to advocate for a higher budgetary allocation in line with 
India’s growing global profile and activities. The Ministry stated that in accordance 
with the findings of the report, the Ministry continues to make efforts to reduce the 
gap between demand and allocation by rationalizing demand to make it more 
realistic, need-based, and aligned to targets, as recommended by the Hon’ble 
Committee, and by working more closely with the Ministry of Finance to facilitate a 
better appreciation by the latter of MEA’s important initiatives and associated funds 
requirements.    
 
1.25 On Committee’s specific incorporation of the dimension of gender budgeting in 
proposing the budgetary demands, the Ministry responded affirmatively and stated 
that it has constituted a gender budget cell, which has been tasked with 
assessing/evaluating gender related contributions of projects/schemes implemented 
by the Ministry and has been making efforts to make development assistance 
projects more gender impactful. Accordingly, Ministry has urged the relevant 
stakeholders, including the Exim Bank, to factor in gender aspects before the 
commencement of a project as well as after the project completion. Accordingly, over 
the last one year, efforts have been made to capture, wherever feasible, gender-
related data in India-funded concessional financing projects abroad during the 
Feasibility Study and the Impact Assessment stages. 
 
1.26 The Ministry has also obtained gender-desegregated data of beneficiaries of 
training programmes under the Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation 

12 | P a g e 
 
Programme (ITEC). It is heartening to inform the esteemed Committee that since 
2015, India has trained nearly 30,000 women from the Global South for various 
courses in India. Overall, women account for nearly 39% of all ITEC trainees every 
year. In addition, since 2015, India has provided nearly 60 gender specific/women-
only courses. 
 
1.27 Additionally, the Ministry has been continuously reviewing service rules and 
Government of India’s directives to improve gender-specific service conditions. 
Officers of the Ministry have also been provided training on gender budgeting at IIM, 
Ahmedabad. The Ministry regularly organises sports and competitions to boost 
morale of women employees. Ministry organized a ‘Swasth Naari’ Health Camp for all 
women in November 2025. Efforts are underway for broader dissemination of gender-
related success stories of the Ministry. The Ministry remains focused on advancing 
inclusive development, and gender equality as well as United Nations Sustainable 
Development Goals.   
 
1.28 In Action Taken Replies to Recommendation No. 10 of the Fifth Report of the 
Committee on External Affairs on DFG 2025-26, the Ministry has submitted that it has 
commissioned a study on Gender Budgeting in other countries so that the best 
practices could be adopted. On being asked about the status of the Report, the 
Ministry informed that it had engaged a think tank to carry out a detailed study to 
understand various aspects of Gender Budgeting in Foreign Ministries in other 
countries. The study is now complete with best practices in 17 countries/international 
institutions across the globe analysed. The think tank has submitted their report and 
made a detailed presentation to the Gender Budget Cell of the Ministry last month. 
The Ministry is currently examining the Report. The Ministry remains committed to 
implementing some of the actionable/practical recommendations in a phased 
manner.

1.32 The Ministry also furnished the reasons for increase in allocation under 
Revenue and Capital during BE 2026-27 when compared with BE and RE 2025-26. 
Revenue Section 
 For BE 2026-27, an increase of Rs 853.08 crore compared to BE 2025-26 and 
Rs 426.05 crore compared to RE 2025-26 under the head “Embassies and 
Mission” is due to opening of new Missions and Posts abroad. Funds have 
been allocated after accounting for numerous variables that have an 
inflationary or deflationary impact on the costs associated with establishing 
embassies, such as currency exchange rates. 
 For BE 2026-27, an increase of Rs 199 crore compared to BE 2025-26 and Rs 
186 cr compared to RE 2025-26 under the head “International 
Conference/Meetings” to meet the expenditure on organising BRICS related 
events, including the Summit level meeting to be held this year when India 
takes over BRICS Presidency. 
 For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 125.31 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “International Co-operation”. The increased demand 

15 | P a g e 
 
caters to the mandatory contribution to the United Nation Organisation, for 
special programmes under Trilateral Development Co-operation, etc. 
 For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 102.54 crore compared to BE 
2025-26 under the head “Technical and Economic Co-operation with Other 
Countries”. The increased demand caters to India’s aid and assistance 
programmes in India’s immediate and extended neighbourhood and in 
countries in South East Asia, Central Asia, Africa, Latin America and the 
Caribbean, and the Indo-Pacific region. It also caters for Aid for Disaster Relief 
and contributions to various regional funds. Aid programmes are influenced by 
the dynamics of India’s national interest, diplomatic engagements and bilateral 
relations. 
Capital Section 
 For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 176 crore compared to BE 2025-26 
under the head “Capital Outlay on Public Works”. The increased demand is 
due to planned acquisition of properties abroad as well as in India for 
construction of buildings for RPOs.  
 For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 70 crore compared to BE 2025-26 
under the head “Capital Outlay on Housing”. The increased demand is 
attributed to ongoing construction of Chancery-cum-residential complexes in 
Phuentsholing (Bhutan), Addis Ababa (Ethiopia), Kuala Lampur (Malaysia), 
Jakarta (Indonesia).   
 For BE 2026-27, there is an increase of Rs 144.92 compared to BE 2025-26 and 
Rs 176.92 in RE 2025-26 under the head “Loans to Govt of Bhutan” on account 
of expected expenditure towards Puna – I Hydroelectric Project and Puna – II 
Hydroelectric Projects.

MEA. In MEA context, Aid heads which focus on India’s developmental partnership is 
considered as ‘Scheme’ expenditure as the outcome is of socio-economic nature in 
partner countries while establishment and other expenditure of the Ministry is 
classified as ‘Non-Scheme’ Expenditure.

Aid to Tech Infrastructure: Funds under this recently created head is intended to meet 
expenditure related to projects in the field of (i) Digital Public Infrastructure (ii) Small 
and Medium Enterprise Programme and (iii) Green Technology/Solar/ Water projects 
and other similar projects. Funds will be utilized for implementation of projects related to 
Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI). 
 
Aid to Pacific Island Countries: Funds under this new head are intended to primarily 
meet expenditure related to projects in Pacific Island Countries under the following 
categories: 
(I) Grant in Aid Projects 
(II) Projects being executed as part of commitments made in the FIPIC (Forum for 
India-Pacific Islands Cooperation) Summits 
(III) Quick Impact Projects      
1.40 With regard to the monitoring mechanism followed for keeping a track on 
expenditure under ‘Scheme’ and ‘Non-Scheme’ Heads, the Ministry responded that all 
expenditure is closely monitored at the level of Division / Spending Units as well as 
under the mechanism of Budget Review Meetings. Especially, there has been intensive 
monitoring and review of the technical aid and development assistance projects – 
‘Scheme’ heads - by Programme Divisions in the Headquarters and by our Missions 
abroad in close coordination with partner countries on one level and also at higher level 
by External Affairs Minister and Foreign Secretary. These reviews have identified the 
way forward for many projects where progress was slow. Further, due to the regular 
review and monitoring of MEA’s scheme section at high levels, several new initiatives 
and projects have been identified in consultation with the concerned beneficiary host 
governments, which would be getting finalized and operationalized in due course.

following submission before the Committee: 
“In pursuit of the Hon’ble Committee's recommendation on the use of zero-

1.43 On being asked about the steps taken to ensure balanced quarterly 
expenditure, the Ministry stated that it scrupulously follows instructions from Ministry 
of Finance towards economy measures, rationalization of expenditure, etc. All 
expenditure is monitored and fund releases are made in line with physical and 
financial progress of projects/schemes to ensure that there is no parking of funds 
with implementing agencies. Ministry’s efforts have been to keep establishment 
expenses in accordance with GOI’s guidelines. 
 
1.44 Furthermore, to ensure optimal utilization of budgetary resources during each 
quarter of the financial year, quarterly budget management & expenditure review 
meetings are held to monitor expenditure by the Ministry in consultation with 
spending units. In these meetings, the expenditure plans of various Divisions and 
spending units are discussed and the importance of ensuring balanced expenditure 
during each quarter of the financial year is emphasized and monitored. 
Divisions/spending units in turn make efforts to ensure evenly paced expenditure, 
within the constraints of majority of the Ministry’s spending being in foreign countries, 
where diverse local conditions, inter-alia including geological, security, 
administrative, legal & economic ecosystems and varying project development and 
implementation schedules posing significant challenge for alignment of expenditure 
to our own budget cycle. 
 
1.45 In Financial Year 2025-26, Ministry made efforts to keep evenly paced 
expenditure in each quarter, to the extent possible. Cumulative expenditure in the 
first three quarters: First quarter – 20.49%, Second quarter – 46.50% and third 
quarter – 71.89% of BE 2025-26. The quarterly expenditure by the Ministry in FY 
2025-26 was in consonance with Cash Management Guidelines of Ministry of 
Finance, last quarter ceiling of 33%.  
       

21 | P a g e 
 
1.46 When asked whether the Ministry has identified any areas within its budgetary 
planning process that require further streamlining, the Ministry stated that budgetary 
demand and allocation is a dynamic exercise. This is aided by regular assessment of 
Ministry’s expected expenditure progress in a given financial year. Instruments of re-
appropriation of budget, supplementary demands etc., allow re-prioritization and re-
allocation of funds for full and efficient allocation of budgetary resources. The 
quarterly budgetary and spending evaluations put in place by the Ministry in the form 
of Quarterly Budget Management Meetings reinforce the beneficial reallocation of 
budgetary distribution with a preference towards the Ministry’s development 
partnership portfolio. In these meetings, the expenditure plans of spending units are 
assessed to ensure balanced expenditure during each quarter. Spending units in turn 
make efforts to ensure evenly paced expenditure. The Ministry’s expenditure is in 
foreign countries, where diverse local conditions, inter-alia including geological, 
security, administrative, legal & economic ecosystems and varying project 
development and implementation schedules pose a challenge for alignment of 
expenditure to our own budget cycle.

while drawing up their future projects. As regards the extent to which the Ministry has 
succeeded in developing a focused and well-defined budgetary planning process, the

Ministry stated that its evaluation of budgetary demand and its dynamic repurposing 
during any Financial Year are guided by the overriding premise of zero-based 
budgeting, aligned with the Hon’ble Standing Committee's advice.  
 
1.49 Further, allocations for Aid heads are decided after conducting a realistic 
spending evaluation based on project implementation cycles, local conditions, 
physical progress of ongoing projects/schemes/programmes; as well as on project 
implementation timeframes and cost projections for planned 
projects/schemes/programmes. For ongoing projects funded by the aid programmes, 
the budgetary needs are estimated based on data collected from the main 
divisions/spending units, compared to work progress, and spending rates. A realistic 
budgetary estimate is created by the Ministry with the help of budget management 
meetings/discussions with various expenditure units at the RE and BE stage. 
Budgetary projections for new projects are prepared in accordance with projections 
based on project implementation timetables and cost estimates in Detailed Project 
Reports. The funds are subsequently allocated within the overall budgetary provision 
established by the Ministry of Finance. The decision for implementation of a particular 
project/s under a specific Aid head is taken by the concerned territorial and 
Development Partnership Administration Division based on bilateral priorities. 
 
1.50 When asked about the benefits and challenges encountered while considering 
the adoption of Zero-Based Budgeting (ZBB) in preparing its budgetary demands for 
FY 2026-27, the Ministry stated that its budget management is continuously 
evaluated and assessed in relation to funds using zero-based budgeting approach. 
The outcome of more precise budgetary planning is shown in RE and BE's demand 
vs. allocation ratios, with RE 2025–26 proposed demand realization at 91.03 % (Rs 
21742.74 crore against demand of Rs 23884.10 crore) and BE 2026-27 ceiling of Rs. 
22118.97 crore (increase of Rs 1602.36 crore i.e. 7.81% from BE 2025-26). The 
Ministry will put more effort into achieving this goal in order to meet budgetary 
demands that are credible and justified by past effective use of funds and unbiased 
assessment of anticipated expenditures based on implementation progress. The 
Ministry's goal is to reduce the difference between budgetary allocations made to 
meet demand and those made to meet allotted resources. This goal is supported by 
reliable assessment and efficient use of allocated resources. In accordance with 

23 | P a g e 
 
Zero-based budgeting, no head, whether Scheme or Non-Scheme heads, receives 
fixed or rigid budgetary resource allocation or management; rather, each head is 
evaluated for respective demands taking a base of Zero into account.  
 
1.51 The Committee further enquired whether the Ministry has introduced any new 
initiatives or actions to address the Committee’s recommendations on improving 
financial management and expenditure patterns, particularly to avoid exceeding the 
33% expenditure ceiling for each quarter and the 15% expenditure ceiling for March. 
In a written response, the Ministry stated that for effective quarterly expenditure 
management, all expenditure is being closely monitored. Fund releases are made in 
line with physical and financial progress of projects to ensure that there is no parking 
of funds with implementing agencies. This is done in consultation with the spending 
units, including through quarterly budget management & expenditure review meetings 
chaired by Foreign Secretary. In these meetings, the expenditure plans of the 
Ministry’s various Divisions and Spending Units are discussed and the importance of 
ensuring balanced expenditure during each quarter of the financial year is 
emphasized and monitored. 
 
1.52 In Financial Year 2025-26, Ministry has ensured smooth and balanced pace of 
expenditure during each quarter, to the extent possible. Cumulative expenditure in 
the first three quarters has First quarter – 20.49%, Second quarter – 46.50% and 
third quarter – 71.89% of BE 2025-26. The quarterly expenditure by the Ministry in FY 
2025-26 was in consonance with Cash Management Guidelines of Ministry of 
Finance, last quarter ceiling of 33%.  
 
1.53 The Committee also wanted to be updated about the status of the linkage 
between IMAS 2.0 and the Public Finance Management System (PFMS. In a written 
reply the Ministry stated that the initial version of IMAS 1.0 (standalone version) was 
gradually migrated to IMAS Version 2.0 (centralised web-based, online) to facilitate 
the monitoring of expenditure in real time basis. Currently, out of 221 Missions, 214 
Missions are on IMAS 2.0 and 07 are on IMAS 1.0 due to technical and security 
reasons. IMAS 2.0 has now been integrated with PFMS through API based server to 
server integration since July 2025. As a result of this, the Heads of Account 
mentioned in Detailed Demand for Grants (DDG) of Ministry are reflected on real-time 

24 | P a g e 
 
basis in IMAS 2.0. In addition, accounts of the Missions is directly captured in PFMS 
through integration with IMAS 2.0 on a monthly basis. Efforts are on to sync 
Mission’s transaction data on daily basis into PFMS via integration route.

CHAPTER 2 
OVERSEAS INDIAN CITIZENS 
India's diaspora, numbering over 32 million, constitutes one of the largest and 
most vibrant diasporic communities in the world, spread across more than 190 
countries and contributing significantly to the social, economic, and cultural fabric of 
their host nations while maintaining deep and enduring ties with the motherland. 
Consequent upon the merger of the erstwhile Ministry of Overseas Indian Affairs 
(MOIA) with the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) in 2016 the following Divisions 
within the MEA handles matters relating to Diaspora, their welfare and emigration” 
(i) Diaspora Engagement (DE) Division: This Division deals with matters 
relating to students and NRI marital disputes issues along with 
programmes related with engagement of Diaspora which includes Know 
India Programme (KIP), Scholarship Programme for Diaspora Children 
(SPDC), Promotion of Cultural Ties with Diaspora (PCTD), celebration of 
Pravasi Bharatiya Divas (PBD) etc.  
(ii) Emigration Policy & Welfare (EP & W) Division: This Division deals with 
matters relating to Migration and Mobility including MMPAs & LMAs, 
Emigration Bill, India-EU CAMM, Global Compact on Migration, ADD, 
IMRF and other Regional Processes along with welfare initiatives for 
Indian workers abroad like PDOT, PBBY, ICWF & PKVY.  
(iii) Overseas Employment & Protector General of Emigrants (OE & PGE) 
Division: This Division deals with matters related to overseas employment 
of Indian workers, appointment of overseas recruitment agents, providing 
assistance through Pravasi Bharatiya  Sahatya Kendra (PBSK), working of 
E-Migrate Portal, Welfare measures to protect the interests of Emigrants 
working abroad etc.  
(iv) Passport Seva Programme (PSP) Division: The PSP Division of the 
Ministry provides passport services through the Central Passport 
Organization (CPO) and its network of Passport Offices, Passport Seva 
Kendras (PSKs); and Post Office Passport Seva Kendras (POPSKs). 
Consular, Passport and Visa services to overseas Indians/Foreign 15 

26 | P a g e 
 
nationals are provided by the PSP Division through the Indian Missions 
and Posts abroad.  
(v) Consular, Passport & Visa (CPV) Division: It handles all policy matters 
related to Consular Grievance, Legalisation/Attestation of documents, 
Extradition Matters, Issuance of Indian Visas at our Missions abroad for 
foreign nationals, Registration of births and deaths abroad and Issuance of 
OCI Card by our Missions/Posts abroad 
2.2 When asked about the manner in which the Ministry proposes to utilize Indian 
Diaspora for our soft power diplomacy, the Ministry responded that it engages the 
Indian diaspora in a structured, long-term manner, leveraging them as cultural 
ambassadors, knowledge partners, and economic stakeholders in India’s 
development. Soft power diplomacy is promoted through cultural and people-to-
people exchanges that highlight India’s heritage, values, and traditions. Programmes 
like the Know India Programme (KIP) and the Bharat Ko Janiye Quiz strengthen 
diaspora connections, particularly among youth, nurturing a sustained link with their 
roots and enabling them to serve as informed representatives of India abroad. The 
PCTD scheme supports Indian Missions/Posts in promoting and showcasing India’s 
cultural heritage, enhancing engagement with diaspora communities. The Ministry 
also deepens diaspora engagement in education, research, innovations, 
entrepreneurship, and knowledge exchange. Initiatives such as the Scholarship 
Programme for Diaspora Children (SPDC) provide financial assistance to eligible 
diaspora students for pursuing higher education in India. The Ministry also 
encourages greater diaspora participation in investments and skill and knowledge-
sharing programmes. Policies facilitating NRI investments, support for startups and 
innovations, and avenues for economic participation enable the diaspora to 
contribute meaningfully to India’s growth and development.  To sustain trust with the 
diaspora, the Ministry leverages mechanisms like the Indian Community Welfare 
Fund (ICWF), which provides financial assistance, legal aid, medical support, and 
repatriation services to Indians in distress abroad. Large-scale evacuations such as 
the Vande Bharat Missions and “Operation Ganga”, “Operation Kaveri”, “Operation 
Ajay”, “Operation Sindhu” etc further highlight India’s commitment to protecting the 
welfare of its citizens overseas, strengthening diaspora goodwill.  
 

27 | P a g e

2.4 On being asked about how does this year’s budget for overseas Indian affairs 
compare with the previous fiscal years in terms of funding levels and priorities, the 
Ministry stated that funds for overseas Indian affairs have remained broadly 
consistent with previous years. In the next financial year 2026-27, slight increases for 
key programmes such as the Promotion of Cultural Ties with Diaspora (PCTD) and 
Scholarship Scheme for Diaspora Children have been made. The overall trend 
reflects a growing focus on engagement and welfare of the Indian diaspora. The 
Scholarship Programme for Diaspora Children (SPDC) is being gradually 
strengthened through incremental budget support. PCTD continues to promote 
Indian culture and traditions abroad through cultural events, festivals, and initiatives 
that maintain strong links with the diaspora, with the 2026-27 allocation aimed to 
support enhanced cultural diplomacy. There is a requirement to further enhance the 
budgetary allocations under the PCTD Scheme of this Ministry in future budgeting 
cycles. 

28 | P a g e 
 
2.5 The Committee wanted to know about funds provided, utilization thereof and 
major achievements under the various schemes/programmes implemented by MEA 
for the welfare of Indian Diaspora. In a written reply, the Ministry stated that it 
implements several programmes for the welfare of the Indian diaspora and has made 
significant progress in engaging and supporting the Indian diaspora through its key 
programmes. The Pravasi Bharatiya Divas (PBD) held biennially, continues to serve 
as the flagship event connecting overseas Indians with the Government of India. The 
Scholarship Programme for Diaspora Children (SPDC) provides opportunities for 
diaspora youth to pursue higher education in India, strengthening their academic and 
cultural ties with the country. The Know India Programme (KIP) engages young 
members of the diaspora, enhancing their understanding of India’s heritage, culture, 
and contemporary socio-economic developments. The Promotion of Cultural Ties 
with Diaspora (PCTD) supports initiatives by Indian Missions/Posts abroad and 
Indian organizations to preserve and promote Indian culture, fostering deeper cultural 
connections with overseas communities. Together, these programmes have 
strengthened diaspora engagement, promoted cultural diplomacy, and reinforced 
India’s bonds with its global community. 
2.6 Replying to the Committee’s query about undertaking specific audits, reviews, 
or reports on the actual utilization of diaspora welfare funds, the Ministry stated that 
utilization of diaspora engagement budget of this Ministry is regularly subjected to 
C&AG Audit. A comprehensive report was presented to Parliament following the last 
(18th) Pravasi Bharatiya Divas Convention held at Bhubaneswar in Odisha in January 
2025. Ministry has also initiated action and responded to the recommendations of the 
Parliamentary Standing Committee on External Affairs contained in its Sixth Report 
on the subject “Indian Diaspora Overseas, including NRIs, PIOs, OCIs and Migrant 
Workers: All Aspects of their Conditions and Welfare, including the Status of the 
Emigration Bill.” 
2.7 In addition, Indian Missions and Posts regularly submit detailed reports of 
outreach activities undertaken by them for engaging with the Indian diaspora, 
through utilization of funds allocated to them under the Promotion of Cultural Ties 
with Diaspora (PCTD) scheme of this Ministry. The PCTD scheme supports 
initiatives aimed at strengthening ties of India with its diaspora and for enabling the 
latter to preserve, maintain and showcase their rich Indian heritage and culture.  

29 | P a g e 
 
2.8 The Committee sought to know how does the MEA assess the needs of the 
diaspora in different regions and the manner in which they are incorporated into the 
budgetary planning process. The Ministry responded that it assesses the needs of 
the Indian diaspora through regular engagement with Indian Missions and Posts, 
which maintain close contact with local communities and organise regular Consular 
Camps and Open Houses. Diaspora members are encouraged to register, join 
Mission WhatsApp groups, and lodge grievances via the MADAD Portal and 
CPGRAMS. Indian Missions/Posts proactively assist distressed Indians, contributing 
to provisioning legal aid, emergency medical care, and boarding or lodging support. 
The Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) offers means-tested assistance, 
including counselling and repatriation of mortal remains, while One Stop Centres 
(OSCs) support distressed Indian women in select countries. Special attention is 
accorded to countries with a large Indian diaspora population, where sustained 
engagement and promotion of Indian culture are particularly important. Under the 
Promotion of Cultural Ties with Diaspora (PCTD) scheme, this Ministry allocates 
budget to Indian Missions/Posts abroad, particularly with large Indian diaspora 
population with the view to support cultural programmes, outreach activities, and 
diaspora welfare initiatives. Indian Missions/Posts abroad also monitor political and 
economic developments in host countries, issue advisories, raise diaspora-related 
issues with local governments and plan for evacuations of Indian nationals during 
emergencies. Such region-specific assessments and priorities are systematically 
incorporated into the MEA’s budgetary planning process, ensuring efficient allocation 
of resources to programmes and initiatives that address the practical needs of the 
diaspora while advancing India’s broader cultural and diplomatic objectives.

Pre-Departure Orientation and Skill Upgradation of Emigrant Worker 
2.10 Under the ‘Surakshit Jaayein Prashikshit Jaayein’ abhiyaan of the 
Government of India, the Pre-Departure Orientation Training Programme was 
launched by the Ministry of External Affairs in January, 2018 with an aim of 
enhancing soft skills of Indian emigrant workers, going abroad specially to Gulf and 
other ECR countries.Under this programme, the emigrant workers are given one day 
free of cost orientation training aiming to help them to understand local rules and 
regulations of the destination country and their culture, tradition, language etc.

31 | P a g e 
 
2.13 The Committee were curious to know the rationale behind this token 
allocation. During the course of oral evidence, the Foreign Secretary responded to 
this query by stating as under: 
“Regarding your other question on why there is a budget item for repatriation, 
the simple answer is that there is a “Destitute Indians” Head in the Ministry’s 
financing scheme. It is a legacy Head that has existed for years. In order to 
keep the Head operational, in case we may need it on a rainy day, we provide 
a token sum of Rs.1 lakh in it, on the advice of the Financial Advisor.As I 
mentioned, its actual functions have already been taken over by other budget 
heads, largely by funding under the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF).” 
2.14 He further added: 
“Sir, I want to mention that distressed nationals are invariably repatriated by 
our Missions using ICWF funds that are available to them. ICWF is not 
reflected in the budget of the Ministry but they do reflect as expenditures, and 
I can assure you that we leave no stone unturned in ensuring that those 
people, who need to return to India, are returned to India, and the finances are 
available for them through our ICWF funding that is available.” 
 
Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) 
2.15 Established in 2009, the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) is aimed at 
assisting overseas Indian nationals in times of distress in the most deserving cases 
on a means tested basis by providing emergency medical assistance, air passage to 
stranded Indians, legal assistance, emergency medical assistance, boarding & 
lodging, assistance to Indian women facing marital issues and transfer of mortal 
remains to India. The scheme has been revised in 2017 and has been set up in all 
Indian Missions and Posts abroad.  
 
2.16 Regarding the allocation and efficacy of the Indian Community Welfare Fund 
(ICWF), the Ministry stated that Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) was set up 
in 2009 to assist Overseas Indian nationals in times of distress and emergency. 
ICWF is a self-sustained fund raised by levying a service charge on consular 
services by India Missions/Posts abroad. Missions/Posts have its administrative 
control locally. Therefore, there is no such mechanism of fund allocation for the 
welfare programs run under ICWF. Every reported distressed Indian citizen in abroad 

32 | P a g e 
 
is being supported from ICWF on mean tested basis as per ICWF guidelines. During 
the period from 2012 to September 2025, ICWF fund has been utilized for assisting 
3,62,882 Indians in distress in foreign countries. 
2.17 Responding to the Committee’s question over provisioning of funds under ICF, 
the Foreign Secretary during testimony submitted: 
“The ICWF amount is collected currently as a separate component as part of 
the Consular, Visa and passport fee charged by our missions and posts 
abroad for every passport issued, every visa issued and every consular 
service rendered. It is credited in a separate account maintained by every 
separate Mission and Post constituting the ICWF. It is three dollars and it is 
standard across the world. It is used for assisting distressed Indian nationals.” 
 
Grievance Redressal Mechanism 
2.18 When asked about an overview of the grievance redressal mechanisms 
available for the Indian diaspora, the Ministry stated that India has one of the largest 
and most geographically dispersed diasporas in the world. With increasing overseas 
mobility for employment, education, business and tourism, Indian Missions and Posts 
abroad regularly receive a large volume of requests for consular assistance, 
particularly in situations of distress. To address these challenges in a structured, 
transparent and time-bound manner, the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) launched 
an online Consular Grievance Management System, MADAD, in February 2015 as 
part of the Government’s good governance and digital service delivery initiatives. 
2.19 The MADAD Portal provides a single, centralized platform for Indian nationals 
abroad to register, track and monitor grievances related to consular services. All 
Indian Missions and Posts abroad, along with MEA Branch Secretariats in Chennai, 
Guwahati, Hyderabad, Kolkata and Mumbai, are integrated with the portal for 
grievance redressal and follow-up. The system allows real-time monitoring, 
automated escalation, priority marking and regular status updates, thereby 
strengthening accountability and responsiveness in consular service delivery. 
2.20 Regarding the effectiveness of the existing grievance redressal systems in 
addressing complaints from the diaspora, the Ministry stated that over the years, the 
MADAD Portal has emerged as a key instrument for extending assistance to Indians 

33 | P a g e 
 
abroad across a wide spectrum of issues including repatriation, transportation of 
mortal remains, salary and employment-related disputes, imprisonment cases, 
passport and OCI-related matters, student issues and other emergency situations. 
The launch of the MADAD Mobile App and Twitter Sewa (@MEAMADAD) has also 
improved accessibility and outreach. Data from the MADAD Portal indicates that from 
2015 to February 2026, over 1.00 lakh grievances were received, of which 
approximately 97% have been resolved, reflecting sustained efforts by 
Missions/Posts and the Ministry to address consular issues expeditiously. 
2.21 From time to time, MEA has expanded the Portal’s functionality and promoting 
its uses in crises. Multilingual Call Centres through Pravasi Bharatiya Sahayta 
Kendras (PBSK), Student Registration Module, Integration with the “eMigrate” 
system, Integration with State Governments in India, Module for assistance through 
Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF), etc. have been added to the scope of the 
portal. The MADAD portal represents a qualitative improvement over existing 
procedures for handling of consular grievances, through online forwarding, tracking 
and escalation until their eventual resolution. It allows direct registration of the 
grievances by the members of the public and effective tracking of the entire 
grievance handling process thereafter. MADAD incorporates several innovative 
features such as a flexible architecture to handle a variety of grievances, online filing 
and linking of similar grievances for easier retrieval and reference, automatic 
escalation and enhancement of priority, colour-coded dashboards for easy 
assessment and monitoring. Recently, MADAD Version 2.0 with updated user 
interface, content layout and enhanced real time notifications has also been 
launched successfully in December 2025. The MADAD portal is presently available 
in English and Hindi. The Ministry is considering integration of the portal in other 
regional languages using BHASHINI language module. 
2.22 On being asked about the role of Indian embassies and consulates in 
resolving grievances related to the diaspora, the Ministry stated that the Government 
has taken several steps to mitigate the problems faced by the Indians travelling and 
working in foreign countries through Indian Missions on priority basis. Grievances are 
responded through various channels like calls, walk-ins, e-mails, social media, 24x7 
Helplines and Open Houses. As required, these issues are taken up with the 

34 | P a g e 
 
employers and the host government as the case may be, for requisite action. Pravasi 
Bharatiya Sahayata Kendras (PBSKs) have been set up at Dubai, Sharjah, Riyadh, 
Jeddah and Kuala Lumpur to provide assistance to Indian workers abroad. Shelter 
homes for distressed Indian nationals have also been set up at some Missions/Posts. 
Mission officials visit immigration offices and labour camps to address the complaints 
of Indian workers. Financial assistance to distressed Indians, if necessary, is 
provided through the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF). During emergency or 
crisis situations, our Missions/Posts abroad pro-actively help distressed/stranded 
Indian nationals in terms of providing food, shelter, medicine and helping their return 
to India. ICWF is used for redressing grievance/complaint of the distressed Indian 
citizen abroad. Latest ICWF guidelines cover assistance by Indian Missions/Posts 
abroad out of ICWF in the following key areas: i) Assisting Overseas Indian nationals 
in distress ii) Improvement in Consular services, iii) Support for Community Welfare 
activities.

2.24 On being asked about new advances and ventures being explored by the = for 
engaging Indian Diaspora, the Ministry stated that it is exploring new avenues to 
make diaspora engagement more structured, technology-driven, and development-
oriented. Digital platforms such as the MADAD portal, online consular services, and 
social media are being leveraged for real-time communication, grievance redressal, 
and easier access to services. Youth and knowledge-based engagement is being 
strengthened through programmes like the Know India Programme (KIP), 
Scholarship Programme for Diaspora Children (SPDC), etc, connecting diaspora 
youth and professionals with India’s academic and innovation ecosystem. Ministry is 
also strengthening institutional engagement and coordination with State 
Governments through initiatives like the Videsh Sampark Programme, addressing 
diaspora-related issues and enhancing the delivery of welfare services. Alongside 
this, there is growing emphasis on leveraging the diaspora’s skills, expertise, and 
investments to support national development, start-ups, and innovations.  
 
Pravasi Bharatiya Divas (PBD) Convention  
2.25 The Pravasi Bharatiya Divas (PBD) is one of the flagship events of the 
Government of India which has been celebrated since 2003 to recognize the 
contribution of Overseas Indian Community in the development of India and to 
celebrate Indian Diaspora’s connection with its roots. The format of the PBD 
Convention was revised in 2015 with the main PBD Convention being held every 
alternate year with the participation of overseas Diaspora experts, policy makers and 
stakeholders. 
2.26 The 18th PBD Convention was held in Bhubaneswar in Odisha from 8-10 
January, 2025. For the last few years, the Government of India has been organizing 
the PBD Convention in partnership with one of the State Governments. The PBD 
Convention hosted in Partner State provides the opportunity to showcase the State’s 
strengths to the Diaspora and helps in attracting investment and technology into that 
State.  
2.27 On being asked about the reasons for revision of the format of PBD 
Convention in 2015, the Ministry in their written reply have submitted that the 
decision was made to primarily enhance the impact of the event by organizing it 

36 | P a g e 
 
every two years instead of on an annual basis. The intention was to create a more 
comprehensive and detailed program for enhancing the depth and reach of India’s 
engagement with its Diaspora and to effectively harness their potential to contribute 
towards India’s growth story. 
 
Videsh Sampark Programme 
2.28 The Videsh Sampark Programme was started in 2017 as an outreach initiative 
of MEA aimed at engaging with relevant stakeholders within the State Governments. 
MEA initiated State Outreach Programmes under the “Videsh Sampark Programme”, 
which is an in-depth dialogue between the Ministry and the State governments. State 
Outreach Events are being organised in partnership with the State Governments to 
seek cooperation in addressing issues pertaining to welfare and protection of our 
citizens abroad and brief the State Government about the MEA’s initiatives on 
engagement and welfare of the overseas Indian community and curbing illegal 
migration. 
2.29 With regard to the Committee’s question about the present status of the 
Videsh Sampark Programme, the Ministry stated that launched in 2017, the 
programme is an ongoing flagship outreach initiative of the Ministry of External 
Affairs with Indian States. It serves as a vital platform for sustained dialogue and 
cooperation between the Ministry and State Governments on diaspora-related 
issues. The Programme continues to be actively implemented, with the most recent 
edition held in October 2025. It remains central to the Ministry’s efforts to build 
enduring partnerships with State Governments to enhance engagement with the 
Indian diaspora, resulting in more efficient service delivery and better coordination in 
welfare initiatives. 
2.30 The Ministry also informed that Twelve (12) outreach events have been 
organized till date in collaboration with the State Governments of Telangana, Kerala 
and Maharashtra (2017); Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh (2018); Karnataka (2020); 
Punjab and Haryana (2023); Bihar, Tripura and Uttarakhand (2024); and Andhra 
Pradesh (2025). 

37 | P a g e 
 
2.31 About the frequency of these outreach activities organised in each State/UT, 
the Ministry stated that the Ministry actively strives to organise Videsh Sampark 
Programme on a regular basis, in coordination with the State Governments, with 
approximately 2–3 editions of the Programme conducted every year.  
2.32 The Committee further enquired about the key outcomes achieved under the 
Programme in terms of awareness about passport services, safe and legal migration, 
and welfare of NRIs and overseas Indian workers. The Ministry responded that the 
Videsh Sampark Programme has led to enhanced awareness among senior State 
officials and key stakeholders about Central Government initiatives concerning the 
Indian diaspora, passport services, and safe and legal migration. It has improved 
understanding of key services and platforms such as passport services, e-Migrate, e-
Sanad, and various welfare and protection measures for Indian citizens abroad. The 
Programme has helped spread knowledge about diaspora engagement initiatives, 
including Pre-Departure Orientation Training (PDOT), the Know India Programme 
(KIP), welfare of Indian students abroad, and mechanisms for resolution of NRI 
marriage and child custody issues. 
2.33 The Programme has further promoted safe and legal migration by 
strengthening awareness initiatives thus preventing illegal migration and exploitation. 
State Governments have been apprised of and are encouraged to support 
campaigns such as ‘Surakshit Jaaye, Prashikshit Jaaye’ (Go Safe, Go Trained), 
conducted in regional languages to effectively reach prospective migrants and 
vulnerable communities. It has strengthened coordination between the Central 
Government and State Governments for effective grievance redressal, stricter 
monitoring of recruiting agents, and implementation of mandatory insurance 
coverage for Emigration Check Required (ECR) category workers. 
2.34 The VSP outreach programmes have also facilitated closer coordination and 
strengthened partnerships between the Centre and State Governments to address 
region-specific emigration and consular requirements. These engagements have 
enabled more efficient service delivery, improved institutional mechanisms, 
enhanced diaspora engagement and redressal of diaspora grievances, particularly 
those concerning the local administration such as property disputes etc, and 
extended stronger welfare support for NRIs and overseas Indian workers. Overall, 

38 | P a g e 
 
the Programme has increased accessibility to services and contributed to sustained 
and structured engagement with the Indian diaspora. 
III. EMIGRATION 
2.35 In recent times, international migration has assumed a pivotal role, shaped by 
demographic shifts and technological progress, significantly impacting labour 
markets worldwide. India is a leading country in the international migration dynamics 
and is advantageously poised due to its demographic potential. The emigration of 
Indian workers, particularly low-skilled and semi-skilled workers proceeding to the 
nineteen notified Emigration Check Required (ECR) countries of the Gulf, South-East 
Asia, and beyond, represents one of the most consequential and most vulnerable 
dimensions of India’s external engagement. The primary legal framework governing 
their emigration is the Emigration Act, 1983. 
Protector of Emigrants 
2.36 With the help of 16 offices of the Protector of Emigrants (POEs) located at 
Mumbai, Chennai, Delhi, Kolkata, Chandigarh, Hyderabad, Kochi, 
Thiruvananthapuram, Jaipur, Lucknow, Patna, Bengaluru, Guwahati, Ranchi, 
Bhubaneswar and Agartala, the Protector General of Emigrants (PGE) enforces the 
Emigration Act, 1983. The Protectors of Emigrants performs functions assigned to 
them by this Act under the general superintendence and control of the Protector 
General of Emigrants. The main function of the Protector of Emigrants includes the 
grant of Emigration Clearance to the emigrants to ECR (Emigration Check Required) 
Countries as per the procedure prescribed under the Emigration Act, 1983, 
monitoring of recruitment process and reporting illegal agents, inspection of office 
premises of prospective Recruiting Agents (RAs) as they apply for the Registration 
Certificate and helping the Protector General of Emigrants in implementation of 
Emigration Act, 1983. As per the Act, the Recruiting Agents (RAs) are required to 
obtain Registration Certificate (RC) from the PGE before they could engage in 
recruitment for overseas employment. Emigration of ECR passport holders is 
regulated by way of grant of Emigration Clearance (EC) by any of the above 16 
officers of the POEs for employment in any of the 19 notified ECR countries, namely, 
Afghanistan, Bahrain, Indonesia, Iraq, Iran, Jordan, Kuwait, Lebanon, Libya, 

39 | P a g e 
 
Malaysia, Oman, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, South Sudan, Sudan, Syria, Thailand, United 
Arab Emirates and Yemen. 
2.37 The mandate and core responsibilities of the Protector of Emigrants are 
defined under the Emigration Act, 1983. These duties of PoEs have been elaborated 
under Section 4 of the ibid act, as "General duties of Protectors of Emigrants—
Subject to the other provisions of this Act, every Protector of Emigrants shall, in 
addition to the special duties assigned to him by or under this Act —  
(a) protect and aid with his advice all intending emigrants and emigrants;  
(b) cause, so far as he can, all the provisions of this Act and of the rules made 
thereunder to be complied with;  
(c) inspect, to such extent and in such manner as may be prescribed— 
(i) any emigrant conveyance, or 
(ii) any other conveyance if he has reason to believe that any intending 
emigrants or emigrants are proceeding from, or returning to, India to or from a 
place outside India by such other conveyance; 
(d) aid and advise, so far as he reasonably can, emigrants who have returned to 
India. 
2.38 Apart from above duties following responsibilities have been entrusted to the 
Protector of Emigrants: 
a. Emigration Clearance: Granting Emigration Clearance (EC) to individuals 
intending to go abroad for employment to notified ECR countries, ensuring that 
applications are thoroughly verified and meet all legal requirements. 
b. Guidance and Protection: Providing comprehensive protection and guidance to 
prospective emigrants and those already deployed abroad. This includes 
rendering timely and effective advice and assistance throughout their journey and 
employment abroad. 
c. Regulatory Compliance: Ensuring all stakeholders, including Recruiting Agents 
(RAs), comply with the Emigration Act, 1983, and relevant regulations, thereby 
maintaining the integrity of the emigration system. 

40 | P a g e 
 
d. Grievance Redressal: Addressing grievances from emigrants regarding 
repatriation, employment disputes, and related issues, ensuring quick resolution 
and support. 
e. Coordination with Authorities: Engaging with the Bureau of Immigration, State 
Police, Indian Embassies, and Consulates, as well as other relevant government 
agencies, to protect emigrant welfare and enforce compliance. 
f. Monitoring and Investigation: Conducting thorough investigations into the 
treatment of emigrants, both during transit and their stay abroad, and taking 
corrective actions if required. 
g. Educational and Outreach Programs: Organizing educational initiatives and 
outreach programs to inform and educate emigrants and stakeholders about safe 
and legal migration practices. Pre-departure Orientation Training for emigrants is 
an important initiative in this direction.  
h. Policy Implementation and Feedback: Implementing policy changes and 
providing feedback to the PGE Division and Ministry of External Affairs to 
continuously enhance emigration processes and safeguard emigrant interests.
