2.40 When asked about the key priority areas where the budget for the PGE is 
being utilized, the Ministry stated that the key priority area for Protector General of 
Emigrants is to safeguard the interest of emigrants and promotion of legal migration 
through eMigrate portal for which the budget under head ‘PGE Information Computer 
and Telecommunication’ is being utilized to pay service provider and technical team 
of eMigrate portal. Budget Head ‘Pravasi Bhartiya Sahayta Kendra’ is utilized for 
setting up and maintaining Pravasi Bhartiya Sahayata Kendra and Kshetriya Pravasi 
Bhartiya Sahayata Kendra. The expenses incurred on awareness campaigns, 
workshops, publicity material to spread awareness on safe and legal migration are 
booked under budget head ‘PGE-Advertising & Publicity’. 
2.41 The Ministry also informed that the budget for publicity and efforts to raise 
awareness for safe and legal migration as well as their rights and available services 
from Government of India is Rs 73 lakhs in the current FY 2025-26 under the budget 
head Advertising and Publicity.   

42 | P a g e 
 
2.42 When asked whether the MEA has any plan to set up offices of Protector of 
Emigrants in migration hot spots in the country, the Ministry submitted that it has 
been exploring the possibility of opening of Protector of Emigrants Offices (PoEs) in 
those states, where such offices currently do not exist. Currently, the proposal of 
opening a POE office in Vijayawada has been approved and procedure to establish 
office is at an advance stage. The proposal for opening offices in Madhya Pradesh is 
also under active consideration.  
2.43 On being asked how PoE offices align with the broader goals of the Ministry of 
External Affairs (MEA), the Ministry stated as under: 
i. The PoE ensures that Indian workers going abroad, especially to the 
notified ECR countries, do so through legal and safe channels. It regulates 
RAs to prevent exploitation, which is part of MEA’s broader commitment 
towards the welfare of Non-Resident Indians (NRIs).  
ii. By ensuring ethical labour migration practices, the PoE helps India 
maintain strong diplomatic relations with labour-receiving countries. It 
collaborates with Indian Missions/Posts abroad to resolve grievances faced by 
Indian workers. 
iii. The PoE plays a key role in implementing the Emigration Act, 1983, to 
regulate emigration process and curb illegal recruitment. This supports MEA’s 
goal of streamlining legal migration processes for better international labour 
mobility.  
iv. PoE ensures Pre-Departure Orientation and Training (pDOT) 
programs, helping emigrants adapt to foreign work environments. It assists in 
resolving disputes related to contracts, wages, and working conditions, 
complementing initiatives taken under Indian Community Welfare Fund 
(ICWF). 
By fulfilling these responsibilities, PoE offices align with the broader goals of the 
MEA, which include promoting safe, orderly and legal migration, protecting Indian 
citizens working abroad, and advancing India's international labour mobility policies. 
2.44 Elaborating the emigration clearance process work in PoE offices, the Ministry 
stated that since 2014, the Ministry of External Affairs has started granting of online 
Emigration Clearance (EC) to the Indian Emigrants through the eMigrate portal 
(www.emigrate.gov.in)  only. The detailed process for grant of EC is as follows: 

43 | P a g e 
 
a. Demand initiation: The Foreign Employer (FE) raises a demand, providing 
essential details such as vacancies, job roles, and employment conditions. 
The RA acknowledges the demand, verifying authenticity and compliance of 
the documents, including the labor quota and contract documentation. 
b. Application submission: The emigrant or the recruiting agent (RA) submits 
an online application through the eMigrate portal after paying relevant fees 
and uploading necessary documents like the passport, visa, employment 
contract, and insurance policy. Document requirements may vary from country 
to country. 
c. Verification by CPC and Passport validation: Passport validation occurs by 
cross-checking with the Passport database of the Ministry of External Affairs 
(MEA). The Central Processing Center (CPC) conducts detailed verification of 
submitted documents, employment contracts, insurance coverage, and 
compliance with prescribed rates and emigration regulations. 
d. File forward and review: Verified files are forwarded by the CPC with 
comments to the concerned Protector of Emigrants (PoE), who reviews the 
documents and either approves or rejects the emigration clearance, providing 
clear justifications in case of rejections. 
e. Update of records: The eMigrate system is updated with the clearance 
status to reflect the outcome of the application process. 
f. Sharing of data and verification of EC at airport: Clearance data is integrated 
and shared with Bureau of Immigration (BOI) check posts to ensure seamless 
processing and monitoring of emigrant travel. At the airport, immigration 
officials verify aspects like Emigration Clearance status, Visa details etc. Exit 
from India is allowed only if the emigrant intending to travel abroad to notified 
ECR countries for employment purposes, has valid clearance. 
2.45 The Committee further enquired about safeguards are in place within PoE 
offices to ensure that the clearance process is not misused or circumvented by illegal 
recruitment agencies or unauthorized intermediaries. In a written reply the Ministry 
stated that various safeguards are in place within PoE offices to ensure that the 
clearance process is not misused or circumvented by illegal recruitment agencies or 
unauthorized intermediaries.  
a. Digital verification: All the Emigration Clearances (ECs) are applied and 
processed through the eMigrate portal only, ensuring that only authorized agents 
registered with the Ministry can apply for EC. The Bureau of Immigration (BOI) will 

44 | P a g e 
 
not allow a passenger to board a flight/vessel if EC clearance is not granted via 
the eMigrate system, preventing unauthorized emigration.  
b. Regular audits: Periodic audits of clearance processes and decisions help 
identify and address any discrepancies or irregularities. 
c. Strict recruiting agent (RA) registration: A rigorous vetting process (compulsory 
police and degree verification) is in place for registering and renewing Recruiting 
Agent licenses. 
d. Initiating actions: Show cause notices are issued to registered RAs involved in 
illegal activities. Those violating provisions of the Emigration Act 1983 are 
suspended as necessary. Additionally, action is taken against illegal/unregistered 
RAs in coordination with local police under various provisions of the Emigration 
Act. 
e. Surprise inspections: Unannounced checks on RAs deter illegal activities and 
ensure compliance. 
f. Coordination with law enforcement: Working closely with police and cyber crime 
units to tackle fraudulent practices reinforces the integrity of the emigration 
process. Similarly regular coordination is maintained with Bureau of Immigration 
(BOI) officials to discuss patterns/trends in emigration and sharing details of 
violations with each other.  
2.46 On the Committee’s query about coordination between PoE offices and Indian 
embassies and consulates abroad to ensure the welfare of emigrants, the Ministry 
stated that the PoE office directly coordinates with the Consular and Labour Wings of 
Indian Missions to ensure the welfare of emigrants. PoE office serves as the nodal 
field office of MEA for matters related to emigration and associated grievances and is 
well-positioned to engage with state police and local administration, acting as a vital 
link between Indian Missions, the emigrant’s family, and local authorities. The PoE 
regularly coordinates with Indian Embassies and Consulates abroad on following 
issues: 

45 | P a g e 
 
a. Regular information exchange: PoE offices maintain regular communication 
with Indian embassies and consulates to stay updated on local labour laws and 
working conditions. 
b. Sharing of emigrant data: Emigrant data is shared with Embassies and 
Consulates to facilitate easy tracking and assistance when needed. 
c. Joint monitoring: Collaboration with our Embassies and Consulates abroad for 
monitoring employers and workplace conditions helps ensure emigrant welfare. 
d. Collaborative grievance handling: PoE offices and Embassies/Consulates work 
together to address grievances and resolve issues faced by emigrants. 
e. Recruiting Agents (RA) and Foreign Employer (FE) compliance: Upon receiving 
grievances, PoE ensures that RAs assist emigrants by fulfilling contractual 
obligations. Similarly, in cases where required, PoE suggests Indian Missions to 
ensure FEs meet their commitments. 
f. Crisis liaison: PoE offices act as a liaison between families in India and 
emigrants abroad during crises situation, facilitating communication and support. 
g. Repatriation coordination: In crisis situations, PoE offices coordinate with the 
Embassies and Consulates for the safe repatriation of emigrants. 
h. Crisis communication: PoE offices help disseminate crisis-related information 
and updates to families and stakeholders. 
i. Handling death cases: In case of death, PoE works closely with Indian Missions 
for the timely dispatch of mortal remains. It acts as the first point of contact 
between the emigrant's family and agencies abroad. POEs further work in 
assisting the family in obtaining insurance claims by coordinating with Missions to 
gather necessary legal and medical documents. 
2.47 Enumerating the role of PoE offices in assisting emigrants who face 
exploitation, abuse, or other forms of distress once they are abroad and how are 
these cases reported to and handled by PoE offices, the Ministry stated that PoE 
offices play a vital role in assisting emigrants who face exploitation, abuse or other 
forms of distress once they are abroad. The PoE offices issue show cause notices to 

46 | P a g e 
 
registered RAs as and when grievances against them are registered. The registration 
certificate (RC) of those violating provisions of the Emigration Act 1983 are 
suspended as deemed necessary. Additionally, actions are taken against 
illegal/unregistered RAs in coordination with local police under various provisions of 
the Emigration Act. The PoE offices work closely with state police and cyber crime 
units to tackle fraudulent practices and to reinforce the integrity of the emigration 
process. Similarly regular coordination is kept with BOI officials to discuss patterns in 
emigration and sharing details of violations with each other. Various activities 
undertaken by the PoE offices to assist the emigrants are listed below: 
a. Helplines operated by PBSK: The helplines are managed by the Pravasi 
Bharatiya Sahayata Kendra (PBSK), which registers grievances from emigrants 
and forwards them to the concerned Protector of Emigrants for further action. 
b. Coordination with Indian Missions and RAs: Upon receiving grievances, the 
PoE ensures that Recruiting Agents (RAs) assist emigrants by fulfilling their 
contractual obligations. Simultaneously, PoE recommends that Indian Missions 
ensure Foreign Employers (FEs) adhere to their commitments. 
c. Facilitating communication: PoE facilitates communication between emigrants 
and their families, keeping them informed about the situation and actions taken. 
d. Legal assistance: PoE provides support in legal matters, including addressing 
contract violations and ensuring fair treatment of emigrants in coordination with the 
concerned Indian Mission.  
e. Resolution through RAs: PoE works with RAs to resolve issues faced by 
emigrants and ensure compliance with contracts and regulations. 
f. Continuous engagement: The PoE maintains direct contact with individual 
emigrants raising grievances and remains engaged until the issue is satisfactorily 
resolved. 
g. Maintaining distress databases: PoE maintains a comprehensive database of 
distress cases, enabling informed follow-up and policy improvement. 

47 | P a g e 
 
h. Action against erring parties: PoE initiates action through issuing Show Cause 
Notices (SCNs) or suspending the licenses of employers or agents involved in 
exploitation, abuse, or other illegal practices. 
i. Recovery of dues: PoE assists in recovering overdue payments and 
compensation for emigrants facing financial exploitation. 
j. Registration and categorization: Upon receiving a complaint, PoE registers and 
categorizes the case, coordinating with relevant stakeholders (Embassy, RA, 
employer) for resolution. 
k. Follow-up and closure: PoE provides regular updates to the family of emigrant 
and ensures proper documentation of the resolution and closure of the case. 
2.48 When asked about the challenges faced by PoE offices in fulfilling their role 
and responsibilities, the Ministry replied that PoE offices play an important role in 
promoting legal and safe migration. In view of the growing trend of outward labour 
mobility from India, this task confronts numerous challenges stemming from the 
breadth and complexity of responsibilities across the spectrum comprised by 
licensing, orientation, grievance redressal and policy making. In addition, issues 
related to availability of technologically competent staff and infrastructure, 
coordination with law enforcement authorities, combating fraud, ensuring 
communication and public awareness and updating the applicable legal framework 
pose additional challenges. The Ministry is working to address these in an integrated 
manner.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

48 | P a g e 
 
CHAPTER 3 
TECHNICAL &ECONOMIC COOPERATION WITH OTHER COUNTRIES 
This budget head caters to India’s aid and assistance programmes not only in 
India’s immediate and extended neighborhood, but also countries in South East 
Asia, Central Asia, Africa, Latin America and the Caribbean, and the Indo-Pacific 
region. It also caters for Aid for Disaster Relief and contributions to various regional 
funds. Aid programmes are influenced by the dynamics of India’s national interest, 
diplomatic engagements and political relations.

3.5  On the primary objectives of India’s financial aid and assistance programmes, 
the Ministry in a written reply has stated that the primary objectives are furtherance of 
bilateral relations along with strengthening bonds of friendship, respect and mutual 
trust between India and partner countries underlies Indian financial aid and 
assistance programmes abroad. These reaffirm the strong ties that exist between 
India and the host country where the programmes are implemented and enhances 
India’s visibility globally, the relationship on ground with an increased people-to-
people connect and overall appreciation of the work rendered by Government of 
India. India’s approach to development is human-centric and is marked by respect, 
diversity, care for the future, and sustainable development. India's approach towards 
developmental partnerships has also been comprehensive, offering assistance while 
sharing India’s own developmental experience in the full spectrum of activities, 
ranging from creation of infrastructure to building capacities to education, healthcare, 
agriculture and community development. 
3.6  India’s aid programmes are aimed at enhancing the developmental needs of 
partner countries, which invariably makes an impact on livelihood of and socio-
economic profile of common people. Notable among them is the Government of India 
funded High Impact Community Development Projects, which are smaller in scale but 
people-oriented projects that are implemented mainly in areas of rural infrastructure 
such as drinking water supply networks, irrigation canals, farm roads, basic health 
units across social, economic and cultural sphere in friendly neighbouring countries. 
Being short gestation projects, these are delivered faster and spread across various 
parts of the recipient countries that directly impact the people at grassroots 
levels thereby generating significant good will for India and contributing towards 
deepening of people-to-people ties.India has also been extending several capacity 
building initiatives through the Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation 
Programme (ITEC) for civilian and defence officials, and customized training 
programme for various government officials from friendly foreign countries thereby 
contributing towards human resource development in these countries.  
3.7  To a query of the Committee on the steps being taken by the Ministry to 
publicize our international aid projects and its impact in host country as well as in 
India, the Ministry in a written reply stated that the Ministry through social media 
platforms and press releases publicize international aid projects and its impact in host 

51 | P a g e 
 
country as well as in India. Further, meetings and high-level visits are also 
undertaken with regard to project monitoring and execution showcasing the outcome 
of development assistance programme. 
3.8  The Committee specifically desired to know what distinguishes India’s 
technical and economic cooperation programs from those of other major donor 
countries and the specific areas or approaches where India has gained recognition in 
the international aid landscape. In this regard, the Committee have been informed by 
the Ministry in a written reply thatIndia’s relations with its neighbours stand on their 
own footing and are independent of the relations of those countries with third 
countries. The Government of India has a well-articulated policy of ‘Neighbourhood 
First’ with a focus on creating mutually beneficial, people-oriented, regional 
frameworks for stability and prosperity. The Government of India attaches high 
priority to our relationship with our neighbouring countries. These engagements cover 
a wide canvas of subjects like projects on greater connectivity, improved 
infrastructure, strong cooperation in sectoral areas (like education, culture, trade & 
investments, defence and security) and broader people-to-people contacts. India’s 
development partnerships are now maturing into new areas of focus such as Digital 
Public Infrastructure (DPI) and healthcare, public transport, renewable energy, small 
and medium enterprises, among other areas, based on the priorities of the partner 
countries involved. Our development partnership model is consultative and 
beneficiary driven. Adequate budgetary provision is done under international scheme 
heads based on realistic assessment of ongoing projects as well as new 
commitments. India’s development partnership model has received a high degree of 
acceptance from its partners because of its demand-driven nature and the focus on 
keeping the requirements and priorities of the partner country at the heart of projects 
executed abroad. This would help establish India’s image as a trusted partner 
globally and generates goodwill and creates opportunities, economic and cultural, for 
India to enhance its engagement with the host country.  
3.9  The Committee were also informed by the Ministry that the long-term 
objectives of India’s foreign aid policy are to protect and promote India’s national 
interests, defend territorial integrity and sovereignty and ensure peaceful, secure and 
stable regional and global environment that is conducive to India’s growth and 
development. Article 51 of the Constitution provides directions to conduct India’s 

52 | P a g e 
 
foreign policy in pursuit of just and honourable relations between nations and to promote 
international peace and security. These factors play an enduring role in shaping our foreign 
policy. India’s foreign policy adjusts to our renewed objectives, and to the evolving global 
environment while safeguarding our core interests. In effect, India’s global strategy is aimed 
to diversify and expand the country's political, economic and geo strategic options to ensure 
that India continues on its upward trajectory as a fast-growing inclusive economy, with a 
rising profile in global affairs.As India’s foreign policy adjusts to evolving global 
circumstances, it continues to promote Indian national interests, vis-à-vis the emerging geo-
technological landscape. India continues to build its capacities to strengthen its fight against 
terrorism and threats to its territorial integrity and sovereignty. India’s diaspora remains 
central to our foreign policy agenda, as would the objective of effective service delivery and 
citizen-centric governance.  
3.10 With regard to the contribution of budget allocation during 2026-27 to achieve these 
goals, the Ministry stated that budget allocation for 2026-27 will be utilized in pursuit of these 
goals. This is reflected in allocations sought and made for organizing BRICS Summit, 
opening new Missions in Africa and Latin American region, enhancing Diaspora connect and 
increasing aid to partner countries especially in extended neighbourhood.    
3.11  To a query of the Committee regarding the challenges faced by the Ministry in 
implementation of development partnership in other countries, the Ministry in a written reply 
has stated that implementation of large infrastructure projects is a challenging task 
irrespective of their location. The challenges are further increased when such projects are 
undertaken abroad. The nature of challenges varies from country to country depending upon 
the special circumstances under which the project is to operate. Some common issues faced 
during project execution or implementation include non-availability of encumbrance-free site, 
difficulties in land acquisition, Right of Way issues, contractual issues, delays in 
environmental impact assessments, etc. Apart from these, other major challenges include 
the changing politico-security situation in countries abroad which renders changed priority 
accorded to the project. It has been India’s endeavour to put in place a robust mechanism for 
handling all aspects of project implementation, including project formulation, appraisal, 
execution, monitoring, review and evaluation, so that challenges facing projects are 
approached in a systematic and organized manner. Priority is given to the efforts to closely 
involve and engage the host beneficiary government and other stakeholders in all aspects of 
the project to manage the challenges in nascent stage and alleviate their impact.  
3.12  On the difficulties being faced by the Ministry on implementing projects abroad, 
Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 2026 sated as under: 

53 | P a g e 
 
“Sir, you had a question in your opening remarks about the pattern of spending and 
expenditure on these projects, and I would just like to take a minute in sharing with the 
hon. Committee the challenges that we confront and encounter in project execution. All 
of these projects are executed abroad, and they are very, very unlike projects that are 
executed inside the country. The general financial rules, for instance, that we are 
familiar with in our country, our partner governments are not necessarily familiar with 
these rules, and therefore to expect them to re-engineer their internal financial 
architectures to align with our expenditure rules is a very challenging prospect. These 
are the kinds of challenges that impact the timely utilization of funds and sometimes 
lead to decreased resource allocation under a few partnership heads. These 
challenges vary from country to country depending on the special circumstances 
related to the project. Some of the common issues that we encounter are the difficulty 
in applying Indian procurement rules in foreign commercial and legal ecosystems, the 
non-availability of encumbrance-free sites, especially for large projects involving real 
estate, difficulties in land acquisition, right-of-way issues, contractual issues, delays in 
environmental impact assessments which necessarily have to be done by the host 
country, etc. One of the more important points, which I alluded to earlier as well, was 
the changing political, economic and security situation in countries abroad. Most 
recently, for instance, in Afghanistan, Bangladesh and Myanmar, are again a pointer to 
the kinds of unique challenges that we face in our capacity to utilize our budget 
completely and effectively. Notwithstanding this, our endeavour has been to put in 
place a robust mechanism to handle all aspects of project implementation so that the 
challenges that we face abroad, which we have now begun to anticipate as well, are 
approached in a systematic and organised manner. We work closely with and involve 
and engage the host government and other stakeholders in all aspects of the project to 
manage the challenges at an early stage in order to alleviate the impact of these 
challenges. There are also several high-level review mechanisms that have been set 
up, both at the level of the missions as well as at headquarters. We have also been 
taking some important steps to further improve execution. For instance, the Ministry 
has established a project management unit in-house to further strengthen project 
appraisal and monitoring mechanisms. Despite all of these challenges, I would still say 
that we are recognised by fellow developing countries for our willingness and our ability 
to provide technical and financial assistance through our development cooperation 
initiatives. This is a model, because it is consultative and because it is beneficiary-
driven, engages our partner countries in exactly the right manner”. 
 
I. Aid to Bangladesh 
3.13  The budgetary allocation made under the sub-head 'Aid to Bangladesh' has been 
reduced from Rs 120 crore during BE 2025-26 to Rs. 60 crore in BE 2026-27. The Ministry 
has informed that this is due to the political and security disturbances in Bangladesh post 
July- August 2024, the execution of ongoing grant projects has been delayed and no new 
project commitments were made in FY 2025-26. The delays, lack of new commitments, and 
completion of the Akhaura- Agartala rail line project in FY 2025-26 has necessitated a 
reduced budget allocation of Rs 60 cr.

3.18  The Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 
2026 stated as under: 
“With regard to Bangladesh……The budget has had to be slashed to Rs. 60 
crore in the coming year because, over a period of the last 18 months, we 
have not been able to have the kind of discussions with Bangladesh that 
would lead to new projects coming on stream, where we would be able to put 
outlays on the table. We have really been focused in Bangladesh on 
completing ongoing projects.In addition to that, naturally, we cannot impose 
our will in a foreign country. We have to go by the demands of that country - 
what they need, what they want to do, and which kinds of projects they want 
to take forward. We realised very soon after the interim Government came 
into power that they had their concerns and doubts about certain projects, 
and many projects had not moved for a very long time despite agreements 
having been arrived at much earlier. So, we did an exercise in cooperation 
with the interim Government to rationalise the project portfolio that was on 
the table. Following that rationalisation, a number of projects were delisted, 
not because we did not want to do them, but because, by common 
consensus with the interim Government, there was no path forward to taking 
those projects ahead. We are therefore dealing with a smaller bouquet of 
projects in Bangladesh, for which the allocation of Rs.60 crore has been 
made.As I said, we now have a new elected Government that has come into 
office in Bangladesh. We hope to engage with them, and they have said as 
well that they want to engage. The hon. Speaker was there recently, 
representing the Government of India at the swearing-in ceremony. I assume 
that the new Government will have its priorities on the kinds of development 
projects they want to take up with us. If there is a need for more outlays, that 
need will be discussed at the RE stage with the Ministry of Finance, and we 
will get more funds on that front”.

Aid to Bhutan 
3.19  Government of India’s aid and assistance programmes in Bhutan are currently 
covered under both Revenue and Capital Heads with Grants coming under Revenue 
and Loans coming under Capital sections of the budget.  The funds under the Grants 
head are utilized for various development projects including hydroelectric power 
projects, development subsidy to Bhutan, subsidy to Power Grid Corporation of India for 
drawal of power from Chukha hydroelectric project, payment to IOC for supply of 
kerosene oil and liquefied petroleum gas to Bhutan at a subsidized price, excise duty 
refund to Bhutan, projects/schemes under Project Tied Assistance framework, 
scholarships to Bhutanese students in various institutions in India etc.

3.21  An allocation of Rs. 1768.64 crore has been made under this head during BE 
2026-27, which was the highest country aid allocation. The Ministry, in a written reply 
stated as under: 
(i) India’s development assistance to Bhutan is an important pillar of bilateral 
partnership. Over the past five decades since Bhutan’s first plan commencing in 
1961, India's development assistance and technical support has helped advance 
Bhutan's developmental parameters significantly, and the Kingdom graduated 
from Least Developed Country (LDC) to Middle-Income Country (MIC) status in 
December 2023. GoI has also provided financing support for construction of 
hydropower projects in a mix of loan and grant.  

58 | P a g e 
 
(ii)  In line with Bhutan’s objective to transform into a ‘High Income Economy’ 
by 2034, Bhutan aims to increase its GDP from around USD 3 billion currently to 
around USD 5 billion by the end of the 13th Five Year Plan (FYP) period (2024-
2029). Accordingly, the total outlay for the 13th FYP has been set by Bhutan as 
INR/Nu. 51,228 Crore, a 63% increase from the 12th FYP (2018-2023). In July 
2024, Bhutan commenced the 13th Five Year Plan. GoI’s development support of 
INR 10,000 Crores for the 13th FYP period as a percentage of RGoB’s budgetary 
outlay is around 19.5%, an increase compared to 16.67% for the 12th FYP 
period. 
(iii)  For 2026-27, India’s budgetary support for Bhutan is INR 2288.56 Crores. 
It includes INR 1768.64 Crores as Grant and INR 519.92 Crores as Loan to 
Bhutan. The Grant has increased by INR 161.64 Crores (10%) compared to the 
RE 2025-26 allocation of INR 1607 Crores, while the Loan has increased by INR 
176.92 Crores (51%) compared to the RE 2024-25 allocation of INR 343 Crores.  
(iv)  The development assistance is based on the priorities of Bhutan and 
underscores India’s unique partnership with Bhutan. The budget requirement is 
projected accordingly. The enhanced allocation under this head is to facilitate 
implementation of the hydroelectric projects and activities to be undertaken by 
RGoB in the 13th FYP period. While the Punatsangchhu-II HEP is nearing 
completion, the increased allocation is required for Punatsangchhu-I HEP, where 
the works have recommenced and full-scale works at the main dam structure will 
start from March 2026. Moreover, the projects being undertaken under the 
Project Tied Assistance have advanced from DPR preparation stage to 
implementation stage, requiring higher funds.   
3.22  The Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 
2026 sated as under: 
“As you pointed out, the largest share of the aid portfolio has traditionally 
been extended as grants and loans to Bhutan. It constitutes nearly 30 per 
cent of the Ministry’s development assistance Budget. We are implementing 
large infrastructure projects there such as hydroelectric plants, power-
transmission lines, housing, roads and bridges, together with number of 
grassroot, high-impact community development projects. The Government 
has made a commitment of Rs. 10,000 crore towards Bhutan’s 13th Five 

59 | P a g e 
 
Year Plan that runs from 2024 to 2029, which is double the amount of 
assistance we provided during the 12th plan.” 
3.23  The details of various ongoing projects in Bhutan along with the funds allocation 
for each project are provided as under:  
(i)  The 13th FYP of Bhutan commenced in July 2024. INR 10,000 Crores has 
been allocated by GoI for the 13th FYP period, which is scheduled to end in June 
2029. Of this, INR 7,000 Crores has been earmarked for Project Tied Assistance 
(PTA) projects, INR 1,000 Crores for High Impact Community Development 
Projects (HICDP), INR 1,500 Crores for RGoB’s Economic Stimulus Programme 
(ESP) and INR 500 Crores for Programme Grant.  
(ii)  The scope of PTA projects range from agriculture to irrigation, ICT, health, 
tertiary education, school education, scholarship, capacity development, human 
resources development, roads, energy, urban development, judiciary, 
constitutional offices, etc. Till date, 71 PTA projects worth INR 6650 Crores have 
been approved by both governments and have commenced implementation. 
(iii)  HICDPs are short-gestation projects mainly in areas such as drinking 
water supply networks, irrigation canals, farm roads, basic health units, and other 
rural infrastructure. Till date, 572 HICDP projects worth INR 846 Crores have been 
approved by both governments and have commenced implementation. 
(iv)  The Programme Grant (PG) component is a direct budgetary support to 
Bhutan. Till date, INR 175 Crores has been released for PG. 
(v)  ESP is a INR 1,500 Crores programme designed to reinvigorate the 
economy through investments by RGoB in priority sectors. Till date, INR 1000 
Crores has been released by GoI for ESP.

3.26  On the returns to India for the assistances provided to Bhutan, the Ministry, in 
a written reply stated that India’s development assistance to Bhutan is an important 
pillar of the close bilateral partnership, which is based on mutual trust and 
understanding at all levels. The projects under the Plan assistance are being carried 
out across wide range of sectors, which help maintain unmatched goodwill at all level 
of Bhutanese society. From a strategic and economic point of view, the 

61 | P a g e 
 
implementation of hydro-electric projects under the Inter-Governmental Agreements 
is a win-win partnership for both India and Bhutan. The surplus power from these 
projects are exported to India. While for Bhutan, the power exports are a primary 
source of revenue, it provides clean & renewable electricity to India, reducing India’s 
dependence on fossil fuels and ensuring long-term power purchases at predictable 
prices. 
3.27 Elaborating further, the Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee 
on 23 February 2026 sated as under: 
“You had a question about the “Punatsangchhu-II Hydroelectric Project”. It will 
be for a period of two years. As per the intergovernmental agreement, there is 
a Project Development Authority, which is a joint body of the two countries. It 
remains functional for two years after the completion of the project in order to 
address various issues, including those related to the defect liability period 
and full testing….It is a joint responsibility. We are represented in the Project 
Development Authority. Therefore, part of it remains our responsibility, and 
that is the reason it will continue for that period….onPunatsangchuu-I, there 
was a geological event that had happened some years ago, landslide that had 
taken place. And on account of that, a number of geological studies went 
through a serious number of iterations with our geological survey people and 
the Department of Water Resources. I am happy to report that these issues 
have now been addressed to the satisfaction of all concerned and work on 
Punatsangchuu-I is also restarted now.”

3.29  Under the sub-head ‘Aid to Nepal’, the budgetary allocation has seen an

Nepal is one of the largest aid heads, with an allocation of Rs 700 crores for BE 

62 | P a g e 
 
revised to Rs 830 Crores in RE 2025-26 and BE 2026 - 27 allocation of Rs 800 crore. 
India’s engagement with Nepal includes, inter alia, the development of Integrated 
Check Posts (ICPs), rail link projects, supply of defence stores, capacity building and 
scholarships as well as humanitarian assistance (flood relief, bailey bridges etc). 
Government of India is also building Nepal’s National Police Academy and has been 
implementing earthquake reconstruction projects in the areas of education, health 
and cultural heritage.  
3.30  BE 2026-27 allocation has been kept close to RE 2025-26, keeping in view the 
requirement of funds for post-earthquake reconstruction and education, health, 
cultural sector apart from regular ongoing projects such as Integrated Check Posts at 
Bhairahawa; cross-border rail links; embankment construction on Nepalese rivers; 
Small Development Projects; construction of Nepal Police Academy; scholarships 
etc. For efficient allocation of overall budget, it has been proposed to start the 
allocation at BE stage from a lower base with scope for augmentation of funds to be 
sought at the RE stage, depending on the pace of expenditure and requirement at 
that juncture.

3.41  An allocation of Rs.350.00 crore was made under this head during BE 2025-
26. However, the allocation has been decreased to Rs.200.00 and Rs. 300.00 crore 
at RE 2025-26 and BE 2026-27 stage, respectively. In this regard, the Ministry has 
furnished the reply that the political turmoil and the security situation in Myanmar 
have impacted the pace of progress of key projects underway in Myanmar and the 
scope of newer projects has also witnessed a declining trend. However, projects 
stalled or delayed due to COVID-19 pandemic and political crisis have been revived 
and work has progressed further. It may be mentioned that construction works are 
underway on Kalewa-Yargi road section and Road component of Kaladan Multi-
Modal Transport Corridor. India has also extended support through Humanitarian 
Assistance and Disaster Relief (HADR) response to economic, climate, humanitarian 
hardships in Myanmar. In addition, new proposals including small community 
infrastructure, education, capacity building initiatives, healthcare have been 
proposed, however, budgetary allocation has been appropriately rationalized due to 
the sensitivities of the on-ground project implementation.  
3.42  When asked whether the allocated funds are adequate for the completion/ 
progress of the projects, the Ministry, in a written reply has stated that the funds 
allocated are commensurate to the given pace of work and implementation schedule 

66 | P a g e 
 
of the projects. The allocated funds are determined based on an assessment of the 
current proposals in the pipeline. 
3.43  Due to prevailing security challenges in Myanmar, various infrastructure 
projects progress suffered, resulting with lower expenditure.  The current allocation is 
commensurate in light of the current scope and pace of work under progress. The 
utilization is dependent upon the ensuing political situation and the security concerns 
in project areas. Our relations with Myanmar stand on their own footing and are 
independent of the relations of Myanmar with third countries. 
3.44  On being asked how the ongoing political turmoil affect our developmental 
projects in Myanmar, the Ministry stated in a written reply that due to the prevailing 
security situation in Myanmar, the pace of progress on the development projects has 
been impacted. Ground-level difficulties are being addressed through coordination 
among the various stakeholders involved in the implementation of the project, and 
work is continuing in the segments where access is available.  
3.45  Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 2026 
sated as under: 
“In Myanmar, the political and security situation, which remains fraught in large 
parts of the country, has had an adverse impact on the pace of progress of 
ongoing projects. Despite this, through various local-level arrangements that 
have been arrived at, our construction works are underway, for instance, on 
the Kaladan Multimodal Transit Transport Corridor and the Kalewa-Yargi 
road project. We also continue to implement several quick impact projects at 
the grassroot level. Last year, following the earthquake in Myanmar, we were 
also able to extend significant post-earthquake reconstruction assistance to 
Myanmar. Myanmar has also gone through elections. There the Government 
will take office soon. We will discuss the Government’s development priorities 
and see what is possible to undertake with them in the coming financial 
year.” 
Kaladan Project  
3.46  Kaladan Multi Modal Transit Transport (KMMTT) is a strategic connectivity 
project to connect the eastern ports of India with Myanmar by developing a port at 
Sittwe on the western coast of Myanmar and linking the Sittwe port with Kaladan river 
channel and road to the Mizoram state of India. The project has two components: (i) 
Waterways component and (ii) Roadways component. 

67 | P a g e 
 
a. Port and Waterway Component: Sittwe port was inaugurated by GoI and 
GoM jointly on 9th May, 2023 and now the same is fully operational. MOU 
between GoM&GoI has been extended till October 2026 for O&M of waterways 
component. 
b. Road component of KMMTT includes a new greenfield road project of 109.2 
km to be built from Paletwa in Myanmar to Zorinpui on India-Myanmar border 
(Mizoram) to double-lane National Highway (NH) specifications. M/s IRCON 
International Ltd is the Project Implementation Agency (PIA) for the complete 
stretch of 109.2 Kms. M/s IRCON started the work in October 2023, however, 
the progress has been affected due to prevailing security situation in the 
Myanmar. There has been gradual but steady progress on road construction in 
various stretches where secure work-front is available.  
3.47  The security and political situation in Myanmar had in recent past caused 
delays in ongoing projects. All efforts are being made to expedite the work progress 
inspite of security and logistic challenges. IRCON has recently procured additional 
167 construction machines and has mobilized them on site. 
Amount booked till date in FY 2025-26: INR 72.66 cr; 
Anticipated expenditure in FY 2026-27: INR 178 cr 
 
VII. Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation (ITEC) Programme

and technical assistance to developing countries. Under this Head, the budgetary 
allocation for BE 2025-26 was Rs. 180 crore and it has been increased to Rs. 220 
crore during BE 2026-27.

3.50 The Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 
2026 sated as under: 
“Sir, I wanted to also invite attention to a very important programme of the 
Ministry, which is the Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation, the arm 
through which we engage in capacity building, primarily of the Global South 
countries. In 2025-2026, an amount of Rs. 180 crore was provided, and this has 
been increased significantly by 22 per cent in 2026-2027 to Rs. 220 crore. In 
the ongoing financial year, MEA has offered close to 12,000 civilian and nearly 
3,800 defence slots under ITEC at eminent civil and defence training 
institutions. These training courses are very well received by countries of the 
Global South. …..we constantly receive and engage with ministers, senior 
officials of our partners in the Global South countries who have all gone through 
one ITEC programme or the other. We ensure that these courses respond to 
the developmental challenges of our partner countries, and they provide a very 
rich experience of technical content as well as an experience of cultural 
immersion.” 
3.51  The primary beneficiaries of the ITEC programme are countries of the 
neighborhood, as well partner countries in the Global South. Efforts are made to 
provide courses on a regional basis for countries in important regions like IOR, Indo-
Pacific or CARICOM. Capacity building courses under ITEC are offered to specific 
countries/regions through packages (for example Sagar Amrut scholarships or QUAD 
fellowships) based on inputs received from the concerned territorial divisions.

 3.53 On conducting impact assessment of ITEC programmes, it is stated that 
Ministry undertakes regular reviews of the structure and functioning of ITEC 
programme, along with assessing the impact that the trainings provided under the ITEC 
programme. On an institutional footing, a comprehensive review of the ITEC 
programme along with a review of the list of participating institutions on the basis of 
their specific experience, performance and impact was conducted from November 
2017-March 2018, as per the recommendations contained in the Eleventh Report (16th 
Lok Sabha) of the Standing Committee of External Affairs on Demands for Grants of 
Ministry of External Affairs for the Year 2016-17. 
3.54  Some of the major outcomes of the comprehensive review were as follows: 
 
I. 66 institutes were inspected out of 68 against a standard check list drawn for this 
purpose; 7 institutes were discontinued for various reasons. Several eminent 
institutions, like IITs, IIMs and Central Training Institutes and Higher Education 
Institutes were included in the ITEC programme, to bring the total list of institutes 
to approx 100 as of date. 
II. ITEC portal including the database was comprehensively updated with latest 
course offerings. Better alumni engagement modalities like personalised emails 
and greetings were initiated. Missions were instructed to maintain closer 
engagement with alumni through modalities like celebration of ITEC Day, pre-
departure orientation briefings etc. 
III. Inputs from more than 70 countries/Missions regarding process improvement 
and better courses were incorporated. 
IV. Daily allowance was revised from approx Rs 835 per day to Rs 1500 per day, so 
as to reduce boarding and lodging related inconveniences to ITEC trainees. 
V. New modalities like ITEC-Executive (aimed at DG/JS and above level officers), 
eITEC, ITEC-Onsite were introduced to make the ITEC programme more 
impactful. 
 
3.55   Missions / Posts regularly engage with host governments to better understand 
their requirements for capacity building and training requests. Institutes also seek 
feedback from participants regarding the quality and relevance of trainings. 
Testimonials and feedback from participants are regularly posted on the ITEC website 

70 | P a g e 
 
and social media. Feedback is also obtained by missions from ITEC alumni during 
ITEC-Day engagements, for which special grant is also released by the Ministry. As one 
of the world’s longest running structured capacity building programme, (started in 
1964), ITEC is now an important strand of India’s Development Partnership. The 
popularity of ITEC programme may be assessed from the large number of requests 
received for customized courses each year, as also the extremely positive assessments 
received at fora such as Foreign Office Consultations, Joint Commission Meetings, 
HOMs conferences and during the incoming and outgoing visits of VVIPs. 
3.56  Candidates for ITEC programme come from different regions of the world and 
there is a possibility that some candidates may face particular constraints during the 
training particularly regarding language. In this regard, Ministry has replied thatITEC 
participants usually do not face any constraints about language during their training 
since English is mandatory for every participant who is selected for ITEC training. Such 
assessments are done both at the Missions, when the candidate first applies, as well by 
the Institute prior to the commencement of the course. In the case of customised 
courses, where language may be a barrier, suitable arrangements for interpretation are 
made. In the current financial year, Ministry has also initiated conduct of ITEC courses 
in foreign languages (Spanish and French) where the entire course content is delivered 
through translated material & simultaneous interpretation. 
3.57  Regarding involvement of private institutes in providing Training under these 
programmes, the Ministry in a written reply stated India has a rich ecosystem of training 
institutes in the form of central training institutes, higher educational institutes and 
autonomous bodies which cater to most sectors of training. Since sharing of India’s 
governance story and development models/flagship schemes with our partner countries 
is stated objective of ITEC, such trainings are delivered mostly through these 
institutions. However, where required in cases of providing niche trainings, Ministry 
engages private institutes through the process of single source nomination on a case-
to-case basis.

3.59  The budgetary allocation for ‘Aid to African Countries’ has been kept constant 
at Rs. 225 crores for 2025-26 and 2026-27 at BE.  As per the Ministry, the budgetary 
allocations are projected based on a careful assessment of utilization of funds in the 
previous financial years and the activities planned to be carried in the coming 
financial year. Historically the most prominent items of expenditure under the ‘Aid to 
African Countries’ budget head of the MEA, pertain to the ‘India-Africa Maitri 
Scholarship Scheme’ and ongoing bilateral development projects. At the time of 
submission of budget estimates, there was no clarity about holding of the fourth 
edition of the India-Africa Forum Summit (IAFS-IV) in next financial year. Therefore, 
budgetary provisions were not made for the India-Africa Forum Summit during the FY 
2026-27. However, IAFS – IV is likely to be held in 2026. The organization of IAFS-IV 
as well as its follow-up will require injection of a large amount of funds, which will 
substantially raise the budgetary allocation under the ‘Aid to African Countries’. 
3.60  Africa, with its vast geographical extent and strategic significance, plays a 
crucial role in India’s geopolitical and economic priorities. When asked why the 
Ministry has not recommended a more enhanced budget allocation under this head to 
reflect Africa’s growing importance in India’s foreign relations, and the increasing 
influence of other global players in Africa, the Ministry in a written reply stated that 
engagement with Africa remains a key priority for India. In order to strengthen and 
expand its diplomatic footprint across the African continent, India has opened 18 new 
Missions. With the establishment of these Missions, India now has diplomatic 
Missions in 46 out of 54 African countries. The Ministry will continue to actively 
monitor implementation of the projects announced during IAFS-III which are presently 
at various stages of execution. To further enhance India’s development partnership 
with the African countries, India would continue to partner with African countries 
taking into account both India’s geopolitical and economic interests as well as 
development priorities of the partner countries. India is seen as a strategic and 

72 | P a g e 
 
trusted partner by the African Union, one that understands the development 
necessities and priorities of the African countries 
3.61  With regard to budgetary allocation for the India-Africa Forum Summit, the 
Ministry has submitted that the budgetary provision made for the organization of 
India-Africa Forum Summit-IV within the budgetary head “Aid to African Countries” 
during the FY 2023-24 & 2024-25 was Rs 40.00 crores. No budgetary provisions 
were made for the India-Africa Forum Summit for the FY 2025-26. Ministry, with the 
budgetary allocation of Rs. 211.72 crores (RE 2025-26) provisioned under the “Aid to 
African Countries” head, would continue to focus on catering to the ongoing projects. 
The allocation provided under the “Aid to African countries” for the FY 2026-27 will be 
utilized towards expenses related to hosting the Summit in 2026. Additional funds, if 
required shall be sought based on the actual expenses incurred for the organization 
of IAFS-IV.  
3.62  The Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 
2026 sated as under: 
“Sir, Africa is a key foreign policy priority that is reflected in our support of Line 
of Credit projects. In order to enhance our diplomatic presence in the African 
Continent, which you referred to, India has opened 18 new Missions there in 
the recent past. Our partnership with Africa is based on a model of cooperation 
which is responsive to the needs of African countries under the Indian 
Technical and Economic Cooperation Programme. Over 31,000 candidates 
from Africa have been trained in India over the last 10 years alone, and India 
has also increased the allocation of the India-Africa Maitri Scholarships from 
slightly over 1100 in the previous year to close to 1500 in the coming year. I 
would just like to inform the hon. Committee that in consultation with the African 
Union, we are moving towards the scheduling of the Fourth India-Africa Forum 
Summit during the course of the coming financial year….Sir. We are in very 
intense discussions with our African counterparts on this. It will happen this 
year.”

Burkina Faso and Mozambique; Entrepreneurship Development Centre set up in 
Rwanda; India – Africa Regional Material Testing Lab for Highways established in 
Uganda. There are other projects that had to be de-scoped / rationalized based on

the interest shown by the host countries, internal situation prevailing in these

Key areas of cooperation between India and African countries  
3.64  India's partnership with Africa is based on a model of cooperation which is 
responsive to the needs of African countries. Our activities in African countries are

budget allocation for the Financial Year 2025-26 will focus on areas such as offering

countries, food aid to African countries, donation of vehicles/ambulances, donation of

3.70  The allocation for ‘Aid to Afghanistan’ was Rs. 100 crore during BE as well as 
RE 2025-26, but it was increased to Rs. 150 crore in BE 2026-27. As per Ministry, 
this was because following India’s recent renewed engagement with Afghanistan and 
the discussions held during the visits of Afghan Minister of Foreign Affairs, Minister of 
Industry and Commerce, and Minister of Public Health, it has been decided to 
undertake a series of key projects to support Afghanistan’s development. These 
projects will focus on healthcare, capacity building, humanitarian assistance, and 
developmental cooperation. The increased budget allocation, from Rs 100 crore in 
BE 2025-26 to Rs 150 crore in BE 2026-27, will be allocated towards these initiatives. 
3.71  Regarding safety concerns of Indian personnel involved in developmental and 
humanitarian work, the Committee have been informed by the Ministry in a written 
reply that the Government accords highest priority to the security of Indian Mission in 
Kabul and its personnel. Regular contact is maintained with the relevant agencies to 
ensure focused attention on all security-related aspects. India’s development and 
humanitarian assistance initiatives are coordinated directly by our Mission in 
collaboration with the Afghan government. 
 3.72 In the CFY 2025-26, India supplied 124 tons of medicines and medical 
supplies, 3.2 tons of essential supplies through UNODC, 42 tons of earthquake relief 
materials, and 20 ambulances to Afghanistan. Additionally, a "Jaipur Foot camp" was 
organized, during which 75 prosthetic limbs were successfully fitted to Afghan 
nationals. Special Scholarship Scheme for Afghan Nationals is implemented through 
ICCR with a capacity of 1000 per annum.  
3.73  To a query of the Committee regarding specific initiatives being planned or 
continued under this aid to support women and children, especially in sectors like 
education, healthcare, and economic empowerment, the Ministry replied that  the 
Government is offering e-ICCR scholarships to about 3,000 Afghan students, 

76 | P a g e 
 
including about 1,000 women and girls. The Government has also been supporting 
women Self Help Groups (SHGs) in Afghanistan through financial and logistical 
support.  
3.74 The Foreign Secretary during the sitting of the Committee on 23 February 
2026 sated as under: 
“We have also been pursuing a pragmatic, incremental, as well as a cautious 
approach in our engagement with the Afghan authorities, guided by our 
national security interests and our underlying commitment to the welfare of 
the Afghan people as a whole. This calibrated policy has enabled us to both 
monitor regional security developments as well as extend humanitarian and 
development support. A number of key Afghan figures have visited here in 
recent times, which has led to an understanding on undertaking a series of 
key projects to support Afghanistan’s development.This is what explains the 
increased budget allocation from Rs. 100 crore in 2025-2026 to Rs. 150 
crores for the next financial year.”

2026-27 is required to make payments towards the supply of SME machinery from

3.78  Latin America holds significant potential in terms of trade, investment, and 
strategic partnerships. To tap this potential, the Ministry in a written reply has stated 
that economy wise, the trade volume has been steadily growing and showing good 
progress in the quantum of investments. Diversification of trade relations through 
investments in pharmaceuticals, energy, automotives, agriculture and technology are 
a positive outcome of India’s efforts in the region.  The increase in budget allocation 
is in alignment with the region's growing importance in India’s foreign policy.  
3.79  On the key challenges preventing the full exploitation of economic, cultural, 
and diplomatic opportunities with Latin America, the Committee note that 
diplomatically, India has established strategic relationships with Central American 
countries, gaining support in multilateral forums. Geographical distance and logistical 
barriers make trade and business exchanges more costly and less efficient. Political 
instability in some countries, like Haiti and Cuba, has created economic uncertainty, 
deterring investment. Sanctions on certain countries complicate trade and diplomacy, 
while cultural and linguistic differences sometimes hinder deeper understanding and 
cooperation. Despite these challenges, India has made built presence in the region 
and is well-positioned to further strengthen its economic, cultural, and diplomatic ties 
with Central America countries. 
3.80  To strengthen collaboration with Latin American countries in international 
forums and regional groupings, India has provided grants, aid and Lines of Credit to 
Latin American countries, financing several development projects. Additionally, 
India’s support through the India-UNDP fund has been instrumental in addressing 
key challenges in the region. During the COVID-19 pandemic, India demonstrated its 
solidarity by providing vaccines and medicines to Central American countries, 
underlining its commitment to humanitarian assistance. India has consistently 
engaged with key regional groupings in Central America, including CARICOM 

78 | P a g e 
 
(Caribbean Community) and SICA (Central American Integration System). India 
values these regional organizations as vital platforms for cooperation on issues such 
as trade, climate change, and regional security. In November 2024, Prime Minister 
Modi chaired the 2nd India-CARICOM Summit held in Georgetown, Guyana and 
attended by Head of State/Government of CARICOM members.  In September 2025, 
5th India-SICA Foreign Ministers’ Meeting was held in New York on the sidelines of 
the 80th session of the United Nations General Assembly. Through such 
engagements, India continues to build strategic partnerships, enhance economic 
collaboration, and support the region’s collective goals on the global stage. 
3.81  India’s engagements with the Latin American region at bilateral and 
multilateral level are quite strong.  It is evident through high level visits and mutual 
cooperation in international and multilateral forums. On cultural front, India is present 
in the region through its initiatives of celebrating Yoga Day, participating in film 
festivals and also through cultural exchanges. India provides grant assistance to the 
region through Quick Impact Projects, capacity building through ITEC, humanitarian 
assistance in times of distress, including supply of food grains and medicines. 
3.82  With an intention to increase bilateral trade with Latin American countries, the 
Government of India is negotiating a Comprehensive Economic Partnership 
Agreement with Chile, expansion of Preferential Trade Agreement into Free Trade 
Agreement with Peru and significant and substantive expansion of India’s 
Preferential Trade Agreement with MERCOSUR countries, which includes Argentina, 
Bolivia, Brazil, Paraguay and Uruguay. 
3.83  GoI is also encouraging public and private Indian companies to enter into critical 
minerals and rare earth material sector of South American countries, through direct 
license, or through JV with a local company, and invest through capital and/or 
technology.

CHAPTER 4 
ISSUANCE OF PASSPORT 
Passport services constitute one of the most visible and citizen-centric 
functions discharged by the Ministry of External Affairs. A passport, as a document of 
national identity and sovereign guarantee, is issued under the authority of the 
Passports Act, 1967, and represents the State's formal undertaking to afford 
protection to its bearer in foreign jurisdictions. The Government of India, recognizing 
the growing demand for passport services driven by increasing outward mobility, a 
large and expanding diaspora, and rising aspirations of its citizens, has over the 
years undertaken a series of structural, technological, and administrative reforms to 
make passport issuance more accessible, transparent, and efficient. The 
establishment of the Passport Seva Programme under a Public-Private Partnership 
model marked a watershed in the delivery of passport services, transforming what 
was once a largely manual and time-consuming process into a technology-driven, 
citizen-friendly system operational across an extensive network of Passport Seva 
Kendras and Post Office Passport Seva Kendras spread throughout the country. 
I. BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
4.2 The sub head ‘Passport and Emigration’ caters to expenditure on passport 
services, printing of travel documents including e-passports, lease/purchase of 
passport printers, payments to State Governments and Union Territories for 
verifications, expenses of pre-departure orientation and skill upgradation of emigrant 
workers, etc. The total budgetary allocations under the sub-head for past four years 
are as under:

well as the cost of e-Passport booklet, has increased substantially. Additionally, 
the annual volume of passport applications processed has risen significantly,

B. PASSPORT SEVA PROJECT (PSP) 
4.6 The Passport Seva Programme (PSP) Division provides passport-related 
services across India and abroad. Passport issuance has emerged as one of the 
most prominent statutory and citizen-centric services provided by the Ministry. There 
are 93 Passport Seva Kendras (PSK) in Public-Private Partnership (PPP) mode and 
442 Post Office Passport Seva Kendras (POPSK) in association with the Department 
of Posts as extended arms of the 37 Passport Offices. The total number of Passport 
Seva Kendras functioning in the country stood at 545 as of December, including 
PSKs and POPSKs. For Indians living abroad, Passports and Emergency 
Certificates are being rendered through 194 Indian Missions/ Posts abroad 
connected to the PSP systems. 
4.7 The list of Passport Seva Kendra (PSKs) and Post Office Passport Seva 
Kendra (PoPSKs) is attached as Annexure I and II respectively.

4.8 Apprising the Committee about the Passport Seva Programme, the Foreign 
Secretary during testimony before the Committee on 23 February 2026 submitted as 
under: 
“I want to speak briefly about the Passport Seva programme, which I know is 
of great interest to all Members of this Committee. This programme is one of 
the most successful citizen-centric programmes of the Government of India, 
run in a public-private partnership mode. It serves as a benchmark in India's e-
governance initiatives. It has won awards for its performance and 
demonstrates the power of digital transformation at the heart of many 
Government initiatives. In adherence to the hon. Committee's 
recommendations, the Ministry of External Affairs has opened eight new 
POPSKs: Rajnagar and Bakhra under RPO Patna, Kalpetta under RPO 
Kozhikode, Khargone, Bhind, and Mandla under RPO Bhopal, Kushinagar 
under RPO Lucknow, and Pollachi under RPO Coimbatore, between March 
2025 and December 2025. This development brings the total to 93 Passport 
Seva Kendras and 452 Post Office Passport Seva Kendras operational under 
37 Regional Passport Offices in the country, covering 512 Lok Sabha 
constituencies.  
I am acutely aware that this leaves a balance of 31 Lok Sabha constituencies 
where Passport Seva Kendras and Post Office Passport Seva Kendras have 
yet to be established. We plan to establish new Passport Seva Kendras and 
Post Office Passport Seva Kendras in at least seven more unserved Lok 
Sabha constituencies during 2026. We are working closely with the 
Department of Posts to establish more POPSK centres.”
