Mobile Van   0.28% 

85 | P a g e 
 
4.11 When enquired about the outreach of passport services to far-flung areas of 
North East and other hilly terrains, the Ministry replied that all the far-flung areas of 
the North East and other hilly terrain have been covered with PSKs/POPSKs as there 
is one PSK/POPSK in each Lok Sabha Constituencies (LSCs) of the North East and 
other hilly terrain. There are 7 PSKs and 19 POPSKs covering all the LSCs in the 
North Eastern part of India. LSCs in other hilly terrains of India also have a PSK or a 
POPSK. It is submitted that 03 new POPSKs have been approved by the Ministry in 
Kargil in Ladakh Lok Sabha Constituency, Kotdwar in Garhwal Lok Sabha 
Constituency and Pithoragarh in Almora Lok Sabha Constituency. 
 
4.12 Apprising the Committee about the details, status and challenges of the e-
passport project, the Ministry stated that it introduced e-Passports along with PSP 
V2.0. The e-Passport is a hybrid passport that combines paper and electronic 
elements. It includes a Radio Frequency Identification (RFID) Chip and an embedded 
antenna. The project involves incorporating a digital aspect into the existing physical 
passport, with an extensive global digital signature authentication process. Crucial 
information will be printed on the passport's data page and simultaneously stored in 
the embedded chip, adhering to ICAO's specifications outlined in Document 9303. All 
the Passport Offices and Indian Missions/Posts abroad are issuing ePassports since 
May 2025 and October 2025, respectively. As on 31 January 2026, approximately 
1.02 Crore e- passports have been issued to Indian citizens. 
4.13  On being asked about the rolling of PSP 2.0, the Ministry stated that Passport 
Programme Version 2.0 (PSP V2.0) has been rolled out in all 37 Regional Passport 
Offices (RPOs) and their Passport Seva Kendras (PSKs) and (POPSKs) in the 
country, in a phased manner and the pan-India roll out was completed on 26 May 
2025. Further, the Global Passport Seva Programme Version 2.0 (GPSP V2.0) has 
been implemented in 203 Indian Missions/Posts worldwide, in a phased manner and 
the roll out of GPSP V2.0 was completed on 28October 2025. 
4.14 While tendering testimony before the Committee on 23 February 2026, the 
Foreign Secretary further added: 
I am also pleased to inform the hon’ble Committee that the upgraded Passport 
Seva Programme Version 2 (PSP Version 2.0), has been rolled out in all 37 
Regional Passport Offices, along with their Passport Seva Kendras and Post 

86 | P a g e 
 
Office Passport Seva Kendras across the country. This includes the e-
passport. …I would also emphasise that PSP Version 2.0 is more than just the 
e-passport; it is an entire digital ecosystem designed to enhance the passport 
application and grant experience. It goes beyond simply having the RFID chip 
inside the passport, which enables greater efficiency in processing. 
We achieved a pan-India rollout by 26th May, 2025. Furthermore, the Global 
Passport Seva Programme Version 2, which covers its overseas aspect, has 
already been implemented in 203 Indian missions and posts worldwide, 
completing on 28th October, 2025. Therefore, the entire PSP Version 2.0 is 
now operational both in India and abroad at all passport issuing authorities.  
As I mentioned, the PSP Version 2.0 will establish a digital ecosystem among 
the ever-expanding stakeholders and ensure smooth governance and end to 
end service delivery end-to-end through standardized and liberalized 
processes, utilizing the latest emerging technologies such as artificial 
intelligence, chatbots, and analytics. This addresses your point about training 
our officials in emerging technologies and incorporating them into their daily 
work. This is an example of that.  
In PSP Version 2.0, artificial intelligence-enabled chat and voice bots will 
assist in addressing citizens' queries during the application submission 
process as well as support the intelligent handling of passport-related 
grievances. Citizens will experience an enhanced interface through the 
revised passport portal, website and mobile app. Features will include 
automatic and dynamic form-filling, streamlined document processing and UPI 
and QR code-based payment options.  
The Ministry has also introduced e-passports alongside PSP Version 2.0. The 
e-passport is a hybrid document that combines paper and electronic elements 
and includes a radio frequency identification chip and embedded antenna. The 
project incorporates a digital dimension into the existing physical passport, 
featuring an extensive global digital signature authentication process. The 
crucial information will be printed on the passport's data page and 
simultaneously stored in the embedded chip, adhering to the International Civil 
Aviation Organisation's specifications.  
I recall that concerns were raised regarding this being a scheme to 
continuously track passport holders. I clarified at that time that this is not a 
transmitting chip; it is only an information-holding device. The precise location 
of passport holders will remain anonymous and known only to them. 
All passport offices in India and Indian missions abroad have been issuing e-
passports since May and October 2025, respectively. As of 31st January 2026, 
approximately 10 million e-passports have been issued to Indian citizens. The 
Ministry has organised over 500 passport melas on weekends at various 

87 | P a g e 
 
Passport Seva Kendras and over 100 passport seva camps in remote 
locations across the country, bringing passport services closer to citizens.” 
4.15 Regarding measures taken to make quantitative and qualitative improvements 
in the delivery of passport services in the country during 2025-26, the Ministry stated 
as follows: 
(a)   Several quantitative and qualitative improvements in the delivery of 
passport services in the country have been made to ensure that passports are 
delivered to the citizens by the Ministry in a timely, transparent, more 
accessible, reliable manner and in a comfortable environment through 
streamlined processes and committed, trained and motivated workforce. The 
Ministry has worked extensively to bring passport services closer to the 
people and has opened 93 Passport Kendras and 452 Post Office Passport 
Kendras across the countries which are working as extended arms of 37 
Regional Passport Offices. The Ministry is continuously making efforts to 
make end-to-end passport issuance process digital and paperless.  
 (b) The upgraded Passport Seva Programme Version 2.0 (PSP V2.0) aims 
at providing the next level of citizen experiences using latest and upgraded 
technology for delivery of passport related services to the citizens in a speedy, 
convenient and transparent manner. The PSP V2.0 is enabled with Chatbot 
and Artificial Intelligence. The PSP V2.0 also will leverage the potential of 
Advance Analytics, Natural Language Processing, Software Defined Network, 
and Virtualization Architecture for easy provisioning and orchestration of 
required resources. The digital platform of PSP V2.0 is agile, data driven and 
shall ensure enhanced security from internal and external threats including 
advance threats emanating from cyberspace. 
 (c) The next version of Passport Seva Program, PSP V2.0 has been 
enabled with facial and fingerprint (biometric) matching solution for identity 
verification and lesser documentation process and thereby expeditious 
processing of passport applications. For reissue cases, data is auto populated 
for the applicants during form filling and applicants are required to fill the data 
available in the PSP system. AI enabled BOT has been enabled in the portal 
to guide applicants during form filling. The applicant will be able to chat with AI 
enabled BOT through the PSP portal with respect to queries related to 

88 | P a g e 
 
appointment, application process, documentation requirements, tracking 
status of their already submitted application etc.  
(d) Ministry has started the process of integration of PSP system with Aadhaar, 
PAN, Voter ID etc. to validate the authenticity of these documents submitted by 
the applicants. Verification at the source database of these documents would not 
only help in expeditious processing of passport applications but would also help 
verifying the authenticity. In order to provide enhanced level of citizen experience 
in a paperless mode, the Ministry has successfully integrated with DigiLocker 
platform of Government of India. This would enable citizens to submit various 
documents required for Passport Services through Digi Locker in a paperless 
mode. They are not required to carry the original documents.   
 
4.16 On being asked about the progress made in the work relating to establishment 
of PSKs/POPSKs and the amount allocated for each project during 2026-27, the 
Ministry submitted that Under PSP V2.0, responsibility for establishment of PSKs has 
been given to the Service Provider (SP) and there is no separate financial liability 
upon the Government related to establishment of PSK. In the case of POPSKs, an 
amount of Rs 5 Lakh is being paid to Department of Posts for establishment of each 
POPSK. 
4.17 The Committee specifically asked whether there has been any significant 
improvement in service delivery of passports in terms of normal and tatkal passports 
to applicants and end-to-end time taken for both types of passports during the year 
2025-26. The Ministry responded that there has been improvement in service 
delivery of passports. The average passport issuance time (excluding Police 
Verification time) during the year 2025-26 for normal passports is 7-10 working days 
and for tatkaal Passports is 1-3 working days. 
4.18 Providing the details of the number of Indian Missions/Posts integrated into 
Passport Seva Programme through Global Passport Seva Project (GPSP), the 
Ministry stated that all Indian Missions/Posts where Passport related services are 
rendered (203) as on date have been integrated with Global PSP system. The 
integration of Indian Missions/Posts has enabled a standardized and robust service 
delivery platform to Indian Diasporas seeking passport related services. 

89 | P a g e 
 
4.19 The Committee wanted to know an overview of the integration of Digilocker 
with Passport Seva Programme along with the number of applicants who have 
utilized the facility ever since its launch. In a written reply, the Ministry stated: 
(a) Ministry integrated with MeitY's DigiLocker system in February 2021. This 
allows passport applicants to upload digitally verified documents from DigiLocker 
such as marksheets, Aadhaar, PAN, driving license etc. reducing paperwork and 
manual handling. 
(b) Passport applicants have uploaded 10,90,988 Aadhaar through DigiLocker as 
on 31 January 2026 for availing passport related services. 
(c) Passport validation service launched in DigiLocker on December 2, 2025. 
Indian citizens can now use this service to instantly validate their passport details 
with their consent and provide it other government departments, public agencies, 
and authorized private entities as per the requirements. 
4.20 Enumerating the lessons learnt from implementing the Passport Seva Project, 
the Ministry stated that the implementation of the Passport Seva Program (PSP) in 
India has provided several key lessons in e-Governance, service delivery, and public 
administration. Here are the major takeaways: 
a) Digital Transformation is essential with robust infrastructure 
b) A well-designed IT infrastructure is crucial for efficiency, but it must 
be scalable and resilient to handle large volumes of applications. 
c) Public-Private Partnership (PPP) enhances efficiency 
d) Collaboration with Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) had streamlined 
operations, showing that private expertise can improve public services. 
e) Police Verification takes time 
f) Manual police verification takes time in passport issuance, integration with 
external databases depicting indication on clear/adverse police report may help 
in fast processing in issuance of passport.  m-Passport Police App helped 
accelerate verification. 
g) Decentralization Improves Accessibility. Establishing Post Office Passport Seva 
Kendras (POPSKs) made passport services more accessible to rural 
populations. Instead of travelling to distant Passport Seva Kendras (PSKs), 
applicants could visit local post offices, reducing application backlogs. 

90 | P a g e 
 
h) User Experience Matters in E-Governance. Simplified forms, SMS alerts, and 
multilingual support improved accessibility and good user experience. 
i) Cyber security & Data Protection Must be Prioritized. A centralized digital 
system is vulnerable to cyber threats, and therefore multi-layered security 
system has been implemented. In addition, encryption, secure payment 
gateways, advance fraud detection and risk mitigation were introduced. 
j) Public Awareness & Digital Literacy are Crucial.  Many citizens, especially in 
rural areas, were unaware of online services, leading to dependence on 
agents. Awareness campaigns via social media, TV, and local 
outreach helped educate applicants. 
k) Seamless Integration with Other Government Databases is needed.  Lack of 
integration with Aadhaar(PSP is in process of integration with Aadhaar), birth 
certificates, and police records led to verification delays. Gradual real-time 
database linking improved document authentication and reduced fraud. 
l) Grievance redressal is Key to Citizen Satisfaction. An efficient complaint 
resolution mechanism is essential to address user concerns. The Passport 
Seva Call Center was introduced for 24/7 support. 
m) Continuous Policy & Process Improvement is Necessary. Regular updates 
and policy refinements are needed to keep up with changing needs. Tatkaal 
and passport reissue processes were simplified for faster service. 
4.21 With regard to PRIDE database, the Committee asked whether the database 
is integrated with system of MHA, the Ministry replied that it has integrated Passport 
Seva Programme with Bureau of Immigration (BOI) and National Intelligence Grid 
(NATGRID). 
4.22 Regarding the steps taken for data security and privacy, the Ministry stated 
that the Passport data being collected is protected against unintentional, unlawful, or 
unauthorized access, disclosure, or theft as part of Data Confidentiality. The Passport 
Seva system has been designed in such a way that the Confidentiality, Integrity and 
Availability (CIA) of Passport related data being collected from the citizens is 
maintained throughout its lifecycle. The Passport Seva Project has well defined 
Information Security Management System (ISMS) Policy document, which illustrates 

91 | P a g e 
 
the confidentiality, integrity and availability of information assets by providing policies, 
procedures and operating procedure documents (OPDs) designed to mitigate or 
eliminate all acknowledged risks. Layered security approach along with 
considerations of key security architectural principals, not only strengthen Passport 
Seva Security framework but has also enabled eGovernance enterprise security 
system to generate capabilities in dealing with growing security challenges, 
emanating from internal operating environment and fast changing exploit scenarios 
from Cyberspace. The zero trust security framework has been deployed in PSP 
ecosystem. 
4.23 On the issue of Grievance Redressal Mechanism related to passport services, 
the Ministry submitted as under: 
(a) Citizen Satisfaction in PSP v2.0 has significantly improved due to 
technological up gradation that resulted in ease of availability of passport 
appointment, smooth processing of applications in PSKs/PoPSKs, enhanced security 
features to prevent tampering and reduced timelines of passport issuance. The MEA 
has made every effort to systematize grievance redressal mechanism through 
various ways in the organization i.e. feedback, letters, registered communications, 
emails, or even in person etc. The grievances are received through CPGRAM Portal 
designed by DARPG. In addition to this below mentioned facilities are made available 
to the public:  
(i) Public Inquiry Officer:  A dedicated Public Grievance Cell is established in PSP 
Division to handle and monitor the redressal of public grievances received in the 
individual Passport Offices. Apart from this every Passport Office has individual 
Public Inquiry Officer to deal with the grievances. Applicant can meet in-person to 
the Public Inquiry Officer in the Passport Offices for redressal of his/her grievances.  
(ii) Telephonic Inquiry: There are four (4) dedicated telephone lines in the Passport 
Seva Project (PSP) Division on which applicants directly call for information. Apart 
from this every Passport Office also has separate telephone lines to reply the 
queries of the public. The details in this regard are available in website at 
www.passportindia.gov.in.  
(iii)Toll Free Number (1800-258-1800): Apart from this, round the clock Toll Free 
Number (1800-258-1800) (multilingual national Call Center) is also available for 

92 | P a g e 
 
providing any information on Passport services including basic information about 
documentations and how to apply for the passport. 
(iv)Social media platform i.eTwitter:  Public grievances are also handled through 
Twitter handle of MEA as well as of the individual Passport Offices portal. 
(v) Grievance/Feedback on website: In the Passport website, under 
Grievance/Feedback column, applicants can also write their grievances directly to 
the concerned Passport Offices. 
(b) During the year 2025-2026, majority of grievances are resolved satisfactorily 
within 20 days. Corrective actions are taken immediately on the feedback in 
coordination with the Service Provider. 
 
C. ‘mPassport Police’ App 
4.24 The Ministry has launched the ‘mPassport Police’ App for speedy submission 
of Police Verification Report (PVR) to bring down the time limit of verification to 
twenty-one days. When asked about the impact of mPassport Police App in 
expediting Police Verification Reports, the Ministry stated that on 22 January 2016, 
the Ministry launched mPassport Police App for Android/iOS mobile phones for use 
by State/UT police personnel tasked with conducting police verification, enabling 
them for an expeditious submission of digital Police Verification (PV) Report directly 
from the Police Station. The mPassport Police App enables the Police authorities to 
receive and submit the PV data directly through the mobile phones or tablets of the 
concerned police personnel, who capture the PV report in the system digitally while 
conducting the verification of applicant’s credentials.  With the introduction of this 
App, the need to download and print the physical ‘Personal Particular Forms’ and 
‘Questionnaire’ is avoided, resulting in paperless end-to-end digital flow of the PV 
process, enabling further reduction in the time required for completion of PVR.  The 
State/ UTs where the App has been launched has been benefiting for end-to-end 
digital verification & tracking of PVR and are using the App extensively.   
4.25 Regarding the App, the Foreign Secretary during evidence stated as under: 
“I would like to mention that the mPassport Police app has already been 
launched in 25 States and Union Territories, covering more than 9,425 police 

93 | P a g e 
 
stations across the country. The Ministry and the concerned RPOs are 
constantly following up with the States and Union Territories where the 
mPassport Police app has not yet been introduced, which mainly involves the 
Northeastern States. We are extending all technical support to the concerned 
States, including through training and hand-holding for the police personnel on 
a regular basis through the service provider of the scheme. I would just like to 
point out that the rollout of the mPassport Police app in States and Union 
Territories is dependent on the readiness of the IT infrastructure in the 
concerned States. We are extending full technical support to the States and 
Union Territories for the police verification and submission processes. We are 
providing financial support to the States for every application process they 
undertake for the verification and submission of the police verification report.” 
4.26 It may be noted that 25 States/UTs, covering more than 9340 Police Stations 
across the country, have already launched mPassport Police App. The Ministry and 
the concerned RPOs are coordinating with the States/UTs where mPassport Police 
App has not yet been launched. The list of 12 States/UTs where mPassport Police 
App is yet to launched is placed below:

states for launching mPassport Police App, the Ministry stated that in the State/ UTs

Police Department for better understanding of the mPassport Police App. States/UTs 
have been benefiting from end-to-end digital verification and tracking of PVR and are 
using the App extensively. 
4.31 The Ministry also informed that the District Police authorities are now 
processing Police Verification digitally (paperless) right from Police Station level. With 
these measures initiated by the Ministry and with the participation of State/UT Police, 
Police Verification process has become faster and Passport Offices in India and 
Indian Missions/Posts abroad are now receiving Police Verification Reports within 2 - 
21 days, excluding North East and Jammu & Kashmir. 
4.32 Following steps have been taken to reduce the time taken for Police 
Verification and Issuance of Normal Passports – 
(a) Digital Integration of PSP with all Police Districts 
(b) Launch of mPassport Seva Police App

(d) Training to Police Authorities  
(e) Expansion of Passport Seva Kendras (PSKs) and addition of new Post 
Office passport Seva Kendars (POPSKs) 
(f) Online Application Filing, Payment of Fee and Appointment Booking  
(g) Introduction of AI enabled BOT for citizens 
(h) End-to-End Digital Workflow for application processing 
(i) Introduction of ePassports

Chapter 5 
ESTABLISHMENT AND ORGANIZATION 
The Ministry, headquartered in New Delhi, functions through a network of 
divisions, wings, and overseas missions and posts spread across the globe, each 
playing a vital role in advancing the country's diplomatic objectives and protecting its 
national interests. The strength, structure, and efficiency of the Ministry's human 
resources, spanning the Indian Foreign Service (IFS), the Indian Foreign Service 
(Branch B), and various supporting cadres, are therefore of paramount importance. 
I. Organizational Structure 
5.2 The Ministry of External Affairs is headed by the External Affairs Minister, who is 
currently aided by two Ministers of State for External Affairs. The Ministry has six 
Secretaries – Foreign Secretary, Secretary (South), Secretary (East), Secretary 
(Economic Relations), Secretary (West) and Secretary (CPV & OIA). 
5.3 MEA also consists of the following Divisions/Section/Unit Cell -

5.5 The Committee were keen to know about the current total staff strength of the 
MEA, including both diplomatic staff and non-diplomatic staff. The Ministry responded 
that current status of manpower availability with regard to sanctioned/required 
strength - As of 01 January 2026, the current sanctioned strength of the Ministry 
stands at 6035. Out of this, 54.08 percent are located in Missions and Posts abroad. 
These positions spanned various cadres within the Ministry, such as the Indian 
Foreign Service (IFS), IFS General Cadre, Branch B, Stenographers Cadre, 
Interpreters Cadre, Legal and Treaties Cadre. Missions/Posts abroad also consist of 
a Local cadre strength of 3179.  
5.6  Explaining the reason for the gap in the sanctioned and actual strength of MEA 
at various levels, the Ministry stated that the actual strength in MEA is dependent on 
recruitment cycles, cadre management, and retirements in various cadres. Also, 
there is a time gap between creation of posts as part of opening of new 
Missions/Posts, Cadre Review etc, and filling up of the newly created posts by 
indenting these new posts through Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) and 
Staff Selection Commission (SSC). Vacancies in IFS are being filled by direct 
recruitment conducted by UPSC for recruitment of IFS officers and by promotion from 
feeder cadre. Vacancies in group B and C are being filled by promotions and by 
direct recruitment to Group B and C posts by SSC. Indents are placed with UPSC 
and SSC on time to fill the vacancies in Group A, B and C posts in MEA. 
 
5.7 The Ministry of External Affairs accords highest priority to optimize the 
deployment of its human resources to help fulfil its growing responsibilities. The 
Ministry regularly reviews the requirement of manpower at various levels and takes 
appropriate action, including recruitment of additional Officers and Staff, through 
placement of requisite indents with the Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) 
and the Staff Selection Commission (SSC) respectively.  
 
5.8 The Ministry received approval of the Union Cabinet for the 5th Cadre Review 
of the Indian Foreign Service in October 2023 to be implemented between 2023-27. 
These were aimed at keeping the Ministry’s personnel requirements in line with 
India’s expanding diplomatic footprint, enabling the growth of bilateral trade, 

102 | P a g e 
 
investment, and economic engagements, bolstering political outreach in multilateral 
fora, and helping garner support for India’s foreign policy objectives. 
 
5.9 Keeping in mind the requirements of Ministry, and to fill up the vacancies on a 
priority basis, Ministry has increased our indent with the UPSC and SSC.  396 
officers have joined the Indian Foreign Service since 2015 by direct recruitment 
through the Civil Services Examination.

5.12 The Committee were keen to know about the current gender representation 
within the MEA, particularly in the Indian Foreign Service (IFS). In a written reply the 
Ministry stated that the current gender representation within the MEA for IFS officers 
stands at 27.78%. In the last 10 years, the trend of Women officers entering the Indian 
Foreign Service has changed slightly from 36.58% in 2016 to 36.53% in 2025. 
5.13 Over the issue of under representation of women in MEA, the Foreign 
Secretary during the course of oral evidence on 23 February 2026 made the following 
submission: 
“I have some figures on the gender representation. The current representation 
within MEA of IFS officers stands at 27.78 per cent and in the last ten years, 
over the last decade, the trend of women officers entering the foreign service 
has remained constant between 36.58 per cent in 2016 to 36.53 per cent in 
2025. In fact, in the last decade, it has never dropped below 30 per cent. It 
was at a high of 41 per cent in 2020 and 42.8 per cent in 2021. It was 39 per 
cent in 2024 and 36 per cent in 2025. So, we are doing increasingly much 
better on this particular issue.” 
5.14 On being asked about the challenges regarding gender equality and 
representation within the MEA, the Ministry submitted that the nature of the Foreign 
Service involves frequent transfers, overseas postings in diverse environments, and 
long working hours. These features of the service can pose work-life balance 
considerations, particularly for officers with caregiving responsibilities during early 
and mid-career stages. Assignments across different countries make mentoring and 
professional guidance important for career development. 
 
5.15 As regards the manner in which these challenges are being addressed, the 
Ministry stated that it has implemented several measures to address these 
challenges and promote gender equality and representation within the Service, 
including the operation of creche facilities to provide institutional childcare support at 
the workplace; the institution of a structured mentorship programme to facilitate 
professional development and provide career guidance, including dedicated support 
for women officers; the provision of maternity leave, child care leave, and other 
flexible leave arrangements in accordance with Government of India rules, which are 
uniformly implemented within the Ministry; and the constitution of an Internal 
Complaints Committee within the Ministry of External Affairs in compliance with the 

104 | P a g e 
 
Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) 
Act, 2013, to ensure a safe, secure, and respectful professional environment. 
5.16 When asked about the present status of implementation of 5th Cadre Review, 
the Ministry submitted that the fifth cadre review is being implemented currently by 
the Ministry, and it is expected to be completed by 2027. As of 01 January 2026, the 
current sanctioned strength of the Ministry stands at 6035 (not including local cadre 
strength of 3179 in Missions/Posts). These positions spanned various cadres within 
the Ministry, such as the Indian Foreign Service (IFS), IFS General Cadre, Branch B, 
Stenographers Cadre, Interpreters Cadre, Legal and Treaties Cadre. As per the 
Cadre Review recommendations, a fixed number of posts are created yearly. The 
timeline as recommended by the Committee is being adhered to strictly.  
5.17 Regarding the possibility for lateral entry of skilled expertise in specialized 
special skills such as climate change, health, cyber security, artificial intelligence etc, 
the Ministry stated that to address the requirement of specialized skills, the Ministry 
has been placing appropriate indent for recruiting IFS officers by direct recruitment 
through Civil Services Examination. Ministry of External Affairs also takes officers 
with specialized skills from other Departments/Ministries on deputation. The Ministry 
also engages consultants with relevant skillsets in key areas of functioning of the 
Ministry such as counter terrorism, project management, bilateral and multilateral 
diplomacy, international law, consular and disarmament related work. The Ministry 
has placed a request with DOPT for filling up one post at DS/Director level for a legal 
expert through lateral entry mode as per the lateral entry policy framed by DoPT.

It may be noted that the reduction in budgetary allocation for the SSIFS for FY 2024-

5.20 The Ministry also provided details of training programmes conducted for 
Indian diplomats/officials and foreign diplomats/officials during the past three 
financial years, separately in Annexure IV and Annexure V.   
5.21 When asked about the measures taken to incorporate feedback from trainees 
to improve future training programme, the Ministry stated that feedback for each 
course is gathered and analysed at two levels. During the course itself, participants 
give written feedback on all sessions/speakers.  At the end of each course, the 
participants give their overall written feedback and suggestions about various 
aspects of the course. A feedback session (in-person or virtual) is also held for the 
participants at the end of the course which is chaired by the Dean with participation 
of Joint Secretary and Course Coordinators at the SSIFS where the participants 
share their feedback with them. Feedback from trainees is considered carefully and 
SSIFS endeavours to incorporate valuable suggestions from the trainees to the 
extent possible. 
5.22 On the Committee’s query whether there have been instances of non-
conducting of any regular training programme during the last three years, the Ministry 
specified that all regular training programmes were conducted during the past three 
financial years.  

106 | P a g e 
 
5.23 On being asked about any proposal to introduce new training programmes or 
modify existing framework for training programmes during 2026-27, the Ministry 
stated that as per the National Standards for Civil Service Training Institutions 
(NSCSTI) recommended by Capacity Building Commission (CBC), SSIFS has 
aligned all its activities in the framework of eight pillars. This specifically required a 
special effort in the pillar of digitalization and training delivery where SSIFS had for 
the first time launched a Learning Management Software (LMS) Platform through a 
protected part on its website which allows the training since January 2025 to become 
paperless, efficient, transparent, and therefore more effective.  This learning platform 
allows online conduct of quizzes & exams, sharing of reference materials, and 
submission of assignments, all digitally.  Assessment process has also become real-
time and trainees could now see their assessments on weekly basis, thereby giving 
them continuous information & motivation in the areas where efforts are needed to 
improve further.  SSIFS plans to use this platform to introduce new pedagogical 
methods using technology to make learning processes more efficient and smarter, 
including using AI tools. The results of Officer Trainees learning curve after launch of 
LMS have shown marked improvements in their use of digital tools to improving on 
time management and in presenting their thoughts and ideas in more captivating 
manner to the audience. 
5.24 With regard to steps taken to professionalize the Foreign Service Institute 
(FSI), particularly in terms of inducting domain experts and faculty from foreign 
countries, the Ministry stated that apart from the in-house faculty at SSIFS, which is 
currently at 10-12 members (including the deputed Foreign Service officers), the 
Institute draws its visiting faculty broadly from four different categories – (i) retired 
and professionally active Indian Ambassadors; (ii) serving officials at MEA (including 
current Heads of Missions abroad virtually); (iii) reputed domain experts from think 
tanks, industry bodies and similar institutions in India; and (iv) serving officials in 
other Ministries having domain knowledge on subject related to development, culture, 
education, technology etc.  
5.25 When it fits well to the program, SSIFS also invites foreign experts, for 
example in the recently concluded 6th Annual Disarmament & International Security 
Affairs Fellowship Program, two international experts were invited apart from others 
which were drawn from the above four categories. Also, facilitated by the Ministry of 

107 | P a g e 
 
Foreign Affairs, the Netherlands, experts from Clingendael Academy conducted a 3-
day workshop on the Law of the Seas for the Indian Foreign Service Officer Trainees 
of 2025 batch undergoing Induction Training Programme. 
5.26 The Committee further enquired about partnerships or collaborations exist 
between India and international organizations/governments to share best practices in 
diplomatic training. In a written reply, the Ministry submitted that currently, SSIFS has 
107 MoUs with 103 countries and 04 plurilateral groupings (BIMSTEC, BRICS, IBSA 
& League of Arab States). One of the key areas under these MoUs is to exchange 
best practices through regular contact. SSIFS has over 100 visiting faculties and 
partnerships with 12 institutions of repute in India and two international institutions, 
the details of which have been compiled in a single document and uploaded on 
SSIFS’s website. 
 
III. Policy Planning and Research Division 
5.27 Policy Planning and Research is the nodal division of the Ministry for initiatives 
relating to medium-term policy planning, research and outreach to the strategic, 
thinktank and academic community specializing in foreign policy, as well as engaging 
bilateral partner countries on policy planning dialogues. Policy Planning Division is 
also the nodal Division for the Voice of Global South Summit (VoGSS) initiative of the 
Government and for coordinating India’s Neighbourhood First Policy in the Ministry. 
5.28 The Committee wanted to know about the manner in which the Policy Planning 
Division contribute to the formulation of foreign policy and long-term strategic goals 
for India? the Ministry responded that the Policy Planning & Research Division is also 
the nodal division of the Ministry for outreach to the think-tank, strategic, and 
academic community on policy and public diplomacy initiatives. The Division along 
with its network of autonomous bodies (Indian Council of World Affairs and Research 
and Information Systems for Developing Countries), think tanks and academic 
collaborations, provides a well-organized mechanism for the purpose of Policy 
Planning and Research in foreign affairs. These think tanks include both Government 
funded and private organizations. 
5.29 The Division organises four flagship Track 1.5 strategic outreach initiatives, 
namely the Raisina Dialogue, Global Technology Summit, Asia Economic Dialogue, 

108 | P a g e 
 
and the Indian Ocean Conference, as well as a gamut of country and region-specific 
Track 1.5 engagements, in association with various think-tanks. Narrative-building on 
India’s foreign policy is a key purpose of these outreach initiatives. In such 
conferences, policy ideas are also gathered from specialists from outside the 
government, for seeding into and deliberation in the government apparatus. In 
addition to these, the Division also organizes national level seminars in collaboration 
with universities and academic institutions in different locations in the country to aid in 
the dissemination of foreign policy awareness.  
5.30 In recent years, the Division has also taken the initiative to expand its footprint 
beyond New Delhi through the following initiatives in FY 2024-25 and CFY 2025-26 
(as on date):

5.31 The Division also conducts Track 1.0 policy planning dialogues with the 
Foreign Offices of more than 20 countries. These engagements are either bilateral, 
trilateral or plurilateral in nature. 
5.32 The Division is tasked with responsibility of providing inputs for Foreign Policy 
Planning. It prepares research papers, policy briefs and situation reports pertaining to 
global developments and international affairs in general and India’s foreign policy in 
particular. The Division also prepares the Daily Report and Connecting the Dots, Arth 
Niti, Raksha Niti, and Tech Niti, Monthly Reports and other reports. These reports are 
disseminated within the Ministry and our Missions abroad. 
5.33 The Division’s research output also goes to several Government 
agencies/departments/ministries including PMO, Ministry of Defence, NSCS, etc. as 
well as to heads of some prominent think tanks in India. 
5.34 The Division is also mandated to prepare and publish the Annual Report of the 
Ministry which is tabled in Parliament. This is an intensive, three-month-long process 
during which this Division collates and synthesizes inputs from all our Divisions. The 
Annual Report of the Ministry is the only such comprehensive document on India’s 
foreign policy in the public domain and is referred to by the academic and think-tank 
community both within India and abroad. 
5.35 The Division is also the nodal division for India’s “Neighbourhood First Policy” 
where it conducts regular mid-term evaluation and research on India’s engagement 

110 | P a g e 
 
with its immediate neighbourhood in consultation with the concerned territorial and 
functional Divisions of the Ministry. 
5.36 Regarding coordination with state governments, parliamentarians, and non-
governmental stakeholders to ensure that foreign policy decisions reflect diverse 
viewpoints and national interests, the Ministry informed that all the strategic outreach 
initiatives held outside Delhi and supported by this Division witness participation by 
Ministers and senior officers of the concerned state government. Agenda items of 
such initiatives are also decided by organisers in consultation with various 
stakeholders in the concerned state. The information shared and policy 
recommendations generated at such initiatives serve as feedback to both MEA and 
the state governments concerned. 
5.37 PP&R Division has briefed the Parliamentary Standing Committee on External 
Affairs on its activities, as well as the activities of the Indian Council for World Affairs 
(ICWA) and RIS, on several occasions. The Annual Report of the MEA, that is 
prepared by PP&R Division, is circulated in the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha every 
year and it continues to be the most comprehensive policy publication of the MEA. 
5.38 The non-governmental stakeholders in the policy space approach PP&R 
Division regularly with proposals for collaboration in research, organisation of 
strategic outreach initiatives or even seeking guidance from officers on foreign policy 
priorities for MEA. The Division is in regular touch with the experts and academicians 
in these think-tanks and closely follows their work for a better understanding of this 
rapidly growing sector. 
5.39 Consultations are held with various other Ministries/Departments/agencies of 
the Government of India depending on the subject or issue under consideration. The 
outcomes are utilized for various policy documents and other inputs prepared by the 
Division for the Ministry’s leadership. 
5.40 When asked about training programs or capacity-building initiatives are 
provided to personnel in the Policy Planning Division, the Ministry stated that officers 
with the PP&R Division are required to read extensively on the emerging trends in 
foreign policy and developments in global affairs. They are required to track such 
trends and prepare regular and occasional reports on developments in the areas of 

111 | P a g e 
 
geo-technology, geo-economy, and geopolitics, that are shared within the Ministry in 
our Missions and Posts abroad as well as at headquarters. 
5.41 Officers are also required to regularly attend the various strategic outreach 
initiatives that are supported by it, as well as seminars and conferences on subjects 
related to foreign policy held in Delhi and outside. Reports are prepared on the same 
which are also shared within the Ministry. The deliberations at these initiatives are 
picked up for future policy recommendations. 
5.42 The Committee enquired whether the Policy Planning Division collaborate with 
universities, think tanks, or research institutions to leverage specialized knowledge. 
The Ministry stated thatthe Division collaborates with universities such as Rashtriya 
Raksha University, Indian Institute of Science and National Institute of Advanced 
Studies etc as well as think-tanks & research institutions such as NEDFi, Carnegie 
India, Observer Research Foundation,  Pune International Centre, The Energy 
Forum, Centre for Public Policy Research, Ananta Centre for undertaking research 
studies as well as various strategic outreach initiatives which involves participation of 
domain experts on such key policy issues. Key takeaways from the deliberations and 
suggestions of domain experts at these outreach initiatives are leveraged to better 
understand such complex global issues.   
5.43 In addition, Indian Missions/Posts abroad undertakes various strategic 
outreach activities and research studies in collaboration local think-tanks, research 
institutes and universities under Strategic Outreach Fund of the Ministry focusing on 
a number of key areas of interest to India, which includes: India’s foreign policy; 
strengthening India’s bilateral ties with the respective host countries including 
political, economic, military and technology cooperation; invest cooperation, regional 
connectivity and global supply chains etc. Key takeaways from such initiatives are 
also utilized to gain specialized knowledge on such issues. 
5.44 Further, the Ministry in July 2024 launched ‘MEA Niti Shakti Initiative’ which 
enables the divisions within the Ministry to commission research on specific topics 
from relevant think tanks and universities having required domain expertise. This 
initiative would further help the divisions within the Ministry to equip themselves with 
the specialized knowledge on various key areas.  

112 | P a g e 
 
5.45 Elaborating the manner in which external expertise has been inducted, the 
Ministry stated that through its nodal division for policy planning and public diplomacy 
initiatives; i.e. PP&R Division, organizes collaborative conferences and seminars with 
various think tanks enabling the participation of domain experts on key foreign policy 
issues. The Raisina Dialogue, the Global Technology Summit, the Asian Economic 
Dialogue and the Indian Ocean Conference are some of the flagship conferences 
organised by the division, in partnership with prominent think-tanks. These 
conferences are intrinsically Public Private Partnership (PPP) where think tank 
partners play a key role in designing the event, curating themes, listing speakers and 
panelists, and raising funds (generally about 50-80% of overall budget) from other 
sponsors, with Ministry’s financial support forming only a part of multiple funding 
sources.  
5.46 The track 1.5 and 2.0 conferences of MEA form a significant component of 
India’s public diplomacy outreach and serve multiple diplomatic objectives such as 
agenda setting, narrative building, and shaping international discourses on 
geopolitics, besides academic deliberations on topical foreign policy themes.  
5.47 Over the years, the Ministry’s flagship conferences such as Raisina Dialogue 
and Global Technology Summit have steadily grown in stature and profile, ranking 
amongst the top international strategic dialogues, comparable to global peers like 
Munich Security Conference. Achieving such an enhanced profile for these initiatives 
was one of the original objectives conceived in 2015, when policy decisions were 
undertaken to expand the Ministry’s track 1.5 outreach to strategic community.  
5.48 The Ministry has also been utilizing external expertise in the form of subject 
specialist consultants for the purpose of research and policy planning. These 
consultants are used for producing research papers, policy briefs, coverage of 
strategic conferences and seminars, preparation of template speeches, think-tank 
compendium, as well as daily analysis of significant global events.

Ministry of External Affairs collaborates with from time to time, through the Policy

Institutes 
Remarks 
ICWA ICWA was established in 1943 as a think tank and was registered as 
a non-official, non-political and non-profit organization under the 
Registration of Societies Act, 1860. By an Act of Parliament in 2001, 
the Council has been declared an institution of national importance. 
The Hon’ble Vice President of India is ICWA’s ex-officio President. 
ICWA is administered as per the provisions of the Indian Council of 
World Affairs Act, 2001. The budget for ICWA is provided by the 
PP&R Division, MEA. 
RIS The RIS was established in 1983 as a registered society. The Society 
was envisaged to serve as a national focal point to identify and 
discuss priority areas of research concerning multilateral issues, 
primarily on economic, social, scientific and technological matters. It 
was decided that the society will undertake research activities or may 
assign areas of research to specified national institutions. The society 
will also forge effective links between research institutions of other 
Nonaligned and developing countries. It would also organize 
discussions, seminars and workshops and involve research 
institutions of other countries in these undertakings. It would also 
advise the Government of India on all matters pertaining to 
multilateral economic and social issues as may be referred to from 
time to time. 
MP-IDSA The Manohar Parikar Institute for Defence and Strategic Analysis is 
an autonomous body under the Ministry of Defence. PP&R Division 
collaborates with IDSA for carrying out various conferences and 
thematic projects.

5.50 Ministry undertakes research collaborations with other countries through ICWA 
and RIS.  ICWA maintains a regular programme of interactions and academic 
collaborations with its partner institutions in several countries. Similarly, the RIS has a 
comprehensive programme of research collaboration with international organisations 
and academic bodies of other countries. The ASEAN-India Centre located within the 

116 | P a g e 
 
RIS is specifically mandated for research collaboration on issues of mutual interest 
for India and ASEAN countries. DAKSHIN, the global South centre of excellence was 
set up a Special Purpose Vehicle under RIS in 2023, to steer institutional 
collaboration between think-tanks of Global South countries to conduct research, 
identify specific projects, and share best practices in the fields of health, education, 
digital connectivity/solutions, and agriculture. Till date RIS has reached out to think-
tanks in 121 countries, and signed Letters of Association with think-tanks in 65 
countries. The outcome of such collaboration is usually in the form of academic 
research which is published in various prominent academic journals. 
 
5.51 Further, the Ministry, in June 2024, has established Strategic Outreach Fund 
(SOF) - to provide budgetary support to Political Wings of our Missions to conduct 
strategic outreach activities in their respective countries, as well as commission short 
term research projects with think-tanks, on topics/issues of immediate relevance to 
bilateral relations of India and the country of Mission’s accreditation. Since its launch, 
the initiative has seen enthusiastic participation of our Missions/Posts, with as many 
as 48 proposals in FY 2024-25 and 64 proposals in CFY 2025-26 (till date) having 
been granted NOC for incurring an expenditure of Rs.4.25 crore (approx) and Rs. 
4.82 crore (approx.), respectively. 
 
5.52 The Committee wanted to know about the key principles shaping and guiding 
Indian foreign policy making in the changed international order. In a written response 
the Ministry stated that the world is transitioning into a more multipolar world. There 
has been a change in the nature and distribution of power. The contemporary 
geopolitical and geoeconomic realities do not reflect conventional bipolar or unipolar 
models.  Consequently, many more countries are in a position to influence and shape 
outcomes. In this increasing multipolarity, India aspires to occupy one of the key 
poles of the emerging geopolitical landscape. This can be achieved by optimal 
combination of capabilities, relationships and positioning. India’s ability to move 
forward in this loose architecture of multiple interest-based alliances will determine 
the path towards it emerging as a leading power in coming times. 
5.53 Given the rapidly changing international scenario and the evolving international 
order, India continues to pursue its pragmatic and outcome oriented engagements to 

117 | P a g e 
 
enhance security, upholding its territorial integrity and promote economic 
transformation. India pursues these goals through strengthened bilateral, regional 
and multilateral partnerships. While India strives to build its influence in key global 
forums, it is also offering to the world its own unique solutions to global issues, like 
the International Solar Alliance (ISA) and the Coalition for Disaster Resilient 
Infrastructure (CDRI).  Our neighbourhood and the Indian Ocean Region will continue 
to be accorded the highest priority, where the focus will be on creating mutually 
beneficial people oriented regional frameworks for stability and prosperity.  
 
5.54 The post-pandemic international environment has been characterised by 
disrupted supply chains, tariff-related trade frictions, and a heightened focus on 
resilient and trade-oriented growth, alongside increased reliance on humanitarian 
assistance and disaster relief (HADR) in response to compounded crises. This period 
has also witnessed major wars, an energy and oil crisis, commodity price volatility, 
and sustained pressures on global food and energy security. In this challenging 
context, India has adopted a calibrated and pragmatic approach, ensuring energy 
security through diversified sourcing, maintaining macro-economic stability, and 
contributing to regional and global crisis response, while continuing to integrate with 
global trade and supply-chain networks. 
 
5.55 Against this backdrop of geopolitical turbulence and systemic uncertainty, India 
has navigated the evolving international order through a foreign policy anchored in 
strategic autonomy, enabling engagement across multiple poles while preserving 
national decision-making independence. India has combined crisis-response 
diplomacy with agenda-setting leadership, emerging as a Vishwaguru at the world 
stage, as reflected in its G20 Presidency, its forthcoming role in shaping global 
discourse through initiatives such as the AI Impact Summit, and continued 
engagement with plurilateral platforms including BRICS. Despite global trade 
disruptions, India has strengthened its economic and diplomatic position through 
expanded trade and economic engagement with major partners, including progress 
on key trade arrangements with the European Union and enhanced trade and 
technology cooperation with the United States, reinforcing its role as a stable, 
credible, and constructive actor in the international system. 
 

118 | P a g e 
 
5.56 India continues to step up its engagement with all the major powers of the 
world, taking independent decisions in its national interest. Working as an advocate 
for reformed multilateralism and globalisation is a central part of India’s foreign policy 
agenda in the post-COVID world. The multilateral system has been under pressure, 
with individual countries balancing their competing interests instead of advancing our 
collective interests. India will lend voice and support to the creation of a new, people-
centric template for multilateral cooperation.  
5.57 Domestic transformation has become integral to India’s foreign policy and the 
goal of our diplomatic engagements continue to bring visible economic benefits like 
foreign investments, technology and jobs to our people. Finally, a key principle 
guiding India’s foreign policy is strengthening the bonds of Indian diaspora with their 
homeland and leveraging their capabilities and resources to promote India’s interests 
abroad. 
5.58 The primary objectives of India’s foreign policy are to protect and promote 
India’s national interests, defend territorial integrity and sovereignty and ensure 
peaceful, secure and stable regional and global environment that is conducive to 
India’s growth and development. Article 51 of the Constitution provides directions to 
conduct India’s foreign policy in pursuit of just and honourable relations between 
nations and to promote international peace and security. These factors play an 
enduring role in shaping our foreign policy.  
5.59 India’s foreign policy adjusts to our renewed objectives, and to the evolving 
global environment while safeguarding our core interests. In effect, India’s global 
strategy is aimed to diversify and expand the country's political, economic and geo 
strategic options to ensure that India continues on its upward trajectory as a fast-
growing inclusive economy, with a rising profile in global affairs. 
5.60 As India’s foreign policy adjusts to evolving global circumstances, it continues 
to promote Indian national interests, vis-à-vis the emerging geo-technological 
landscape. India continues to build its capacities to strengthen its fight against 
terrorism and threats to its territorial integrity and sovereignty. India’s diaspora 
remains central to our foreign policy agenda, as would the objective of effective 
service delivery and citizen-centric governance.  

119 | P a g e 
 
5.61 Indian foreign policy is also shaped by geography. Therefore, the spatial 
orientation of India’s foreign policy remains its immediate neighbourhood, under the 
Neighbourhood First Policy. Supported by Act East policy and Maritime Heads for 
Active Security and Growth for All in the Region (MAHASAGAR) vision, India has 
been strengthening ties with like-minded partners to safeguard a rules-based order 
across the Indo-Pacific. India’s Think West policy and engagement has been 
acquiring fresh momentum. 
5.62 In the short term, the international environment remains marked by heightened 
geopolitical uncertainty, ongoing conflicts, energy and commodity market volatility, 
trade fragmentation, and rapid technological change. In response, India’s foreign 
policy is focused on safeguarding economic stability, strengthening security 
partnerships, and advancing technology-led growth through calibrated diplomatic 
engagement. Efforts are being directed towards expanding trade and investment, 
including progress in trade engagement with the European Union and strengthened 
trade and technology cooperation with the United States, while addressing tariff and 
non-tariff barriers and diversifying supply chains. India is also contributing to global 
norm-setting on emerging technologies, including artificial intelligence and digital 
public infrastructure, alongside measures to enhance supply-chain resilience in 
critical sectors such as energy, semiconductors, health, and critical minerals. These 
priorities are complemented by continued emphasis on regional and maritime 
security, energy security, humanitarian assistance and disaster relief, and the 
protection of Indian nationals abroad amid ongoing global turbulence. 
5.63 The multilateral system remains under even great pressure, with individual 
countries balancing their competing interests instead of advancing our collective 
interests. Indian foreign policy advocates for a new, people-centric template for 
multilateral cooperation. While India strives to build its influence in key global forums, 
it is already offering to the world its own unique solutions to global issues, like the 
International Solar Alliance (ISA) and the Coalition for Disaster Resilient Infrastructure 
(CDRI).  
5.64 The new global order is also defined by deepened competitiveness and 
challenges to established rules and norms. In this scenario, India will continue to play 
a role that contributes to greater stability in times of global volatility. India continues to 

120 | P a g e 
 
strengthen its own capacities and build on its relations with partners to promote a 
rules-based international order. India will continue to step up its engagement with all 
the major powers of the world, taking independent decisions in its national interest. 
5.65 In this increasingly multipolar world, India aspires to occupy one of the key 
poles of the emerging geopolitical landscape. With the right amalgamation of 
capabilities, relationships and positioning, this aspiration can be achieved.  India’s 
ability to move forward in the matrix of multiple interest-based alliances will determine 
the path towards it emerging as a leading power in coming times. 
5.66 The Committee specifically asked the Ministry whether it has explored the idea 
of having a grand strategy that clearly spells out coherent foreign policy objectives, 
goals and strategy for the long run. In a written reply, the Ministry submitted that while 
there is no single overarching and formalized strategic document that can delineate a 
roadmap for India’s foreign policy apparatus, the underlying principles of our 
diplomatic strategy are long-established and enduring. The Ministry continually 
assesses and refines its diplomatic stance and policies in response to evolving global 
challenges. India’s positions on relevant issues and its interests, in the context of 
these principles, have been articulated through various platforms. 
5.67  These principles include safeguarding India’s sovereignty and security, 
protecting and promoting India’s national interests, enabling India’s domestic 
economic transformation, ensuring peaceful, secure and stable regional and global 
environment that is conducive to India’s growth and development and building 
influence in global forums through bilateral, multilateral and regional efforts. This 
pragmatic and outcome-oriented foreign policy orientation has revitalized our 
traditional ties, re-energized our strategic, commercial, and economic relations with 
new partners and re-connected us with our Diaspora abroad. It has provided us with 
a foundation for dealing with the new and fast-changing realities of the world.  
5.68 On the Committee’s specific query about challenges in developing a 
comprehensive foreign policy document or grand strategy for India, the Ministry 
replied that there are no challenges deterring the formulation of a comprehensive 
foreign policy document. Planning Policy & Research Division is in charge of the 

121 | P a g e 
 
publication of the Annual Report of the Ministry of External Affairs that continues to 
be the most comprehensive policy publication of the Ministry in the public domain.

5.70   The foreign policy strategies published by other countries are studied closely 
by the PP&R Division in tandem with the concerned territorial Divisions in MEA, to

countries. This serves as additional intelligence and guiding highlights in 
conversations at the Policy Planning Dialogues and other bilateral engagements.

CHAPTER 6 
EMBASIES AND MISSIONS 
India maintains an extensive network of Missions and Posts abroad, 
comprising Embassies, High Commissions, Consulates General, Consulates, and 
other diplomatic offices, which together constitute the primary institutional 
mechanism through which the Government of India pursues its foreign policy 
objectives and manages its external engagements. Established under the provisions 
of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961, and the Vienna Convention 
on Consular Relations, 1963, these Missions and Posts are mandated to represent 
India's sovereign interests, strengthen bilateral and multilateral relations, promote 
trade, investment, and cultural ties, and provide consular and passport services to 
Indian nationals residing or travelling abroad. As on date, the Ministry of External 
Affairs administers a total of 219 Missions and Posts making India's diplomatic 
network one of the most expansive among developing nations. 
I. Budgetary Provisions 
6.2  The budget sub head ‘Embassies and Missions’ provides for expenditure on 
India’s representation abroad and functioning of Missions/Posts abroad. An outlay of 
Rs. 4206.22 crore was allocated during BE 2025-26 and it was increased to Rs. 
4632.27 crore at the RE stage. The allocation has been further increased to Rs. 
5059.30 crore in BE 2026-27. The budgetary allocation under the sub-head for the 
past four years is as under:

6.4 The Ministry has stated that enhanced allocation in the budget of India’s 
diplomatic Missions and Posts abroad is due to opening of 05 new Mission/Post in 
CFY. The allocation is made in a way to start from a lower base with augmentation of 
funds to be sought at the RE stage, based on requirement. In the current FY 2025-26, one new Mission in Quito (Ecuador) and four new Posts in Boston (United States of America), Los Angeles (United States of America), Kazan (Russia) and Yekaterinburg (Russia) have been operationalized.  
6.5 The Committee enquired whether the Ministry has prepared a timeline for the 
completion and operationalization of these new Missions/Posts. The Ministry 
responded that the process of operationalization of new Missions and Posts is 
executed in a systematic manner, taking into account developments in bilateral 
relations, economic and trade priorities and consular matters, as well as logistical and 
administrative requirements. This enables orderly and efficient operationalization, 
aligned with our strategic and foreign policy objectives. 
6.6 When asked about the sufficiency of funding levels for the smooth functioning 
of Embassies and Missions abroad, the Ministry stated that while it operates within a 
complex global environment with rising responsibilities, Ministry emphasizes the 
strategic optimization of available resources, calibrating support to India’s expanding 
global footprint while ensuring fiscal prudence. Ministry ensures that its budgetary 
allocation is effectively utilized to meet India’s strategic, diplomatic and economic 
goals. 
6.7 With regard to financial powers delegated to each Missions/Posts to address 
the day-to-day requirements/challenges, the Ministry submitted that Government of 
India's Representatives Abroad have been delegated powers in order to streamline 
the functioning of Missions/Posts by reducing their dependence on the Ministry for 
their day-to-day operations. These financial powers are enumerated in the 'Financial 
Powers of Government of India's Representatives Abroad' published by the Ministry, 
which has been concurred to by the Department of Expenditure wherein General

Monetary limits for recurring and non-recurring expenditure under different heads are 
also detailed. The exercise of these powers is subject to rule 12 of the Delegation of 
Financial Power Rules, 2024.  
6.8 However, these powers are exercised judiciously, strictly adhering to the 
canons of financial propriety and instructions are read along with the relevant 
provisions in General Financial Rules, FRSR, etc. The proposals beyond the 
delegated powers of the GoI's Representatives abroad are sent to the Ministry for its 
approval. 
II. Functioning of Missions/Posts 
Geographical Spread 
6.9 Region wise list of Mission/Posts in various parts of the world is attached in 
Annexure VI.   
6.10 The Ministry informed that in the calendar year 2025, one new Mission in Quito 
(Ecuador) and seven new Posts in Manchester (United Kingdom), Belfast (United 
Kingdom), Fukuoka (Japan), Boston (United States of America), Los Angeles (United 
States of America), Kazan (Russia) and Yekaterinburg (Russia) after receiving 
Cabinet approval.   
6.11 With the growing diplomatic relations between India and other countries, the 
Committee also wanted to know the Ministry's plans for expanding India’s 
representation abroad, particularly with regard to establishing new Missions, Posts, 
Consulates, and Indian Cultural Centres. The Ministry submitted thatin tune with 
India’s foreign policy goals, growing diplomatic relations between India and other 
countries and to strengthen soft power of International Council for Cultural Relations 
(ICCR) abroad, ICCR and MEA are in the process of reviewing the locations of Indian 
Cultural Centres abroad with a view to enhancing their global presence and impact, 
including the possibility of opening new Cultural Centres in strategically important 
countries. 
6.12 Enumerating the criteria determining the decision for establishment of 
Embassies/Missions abroad, the Ministry stated that as one of the top economies of 
the world and with significant advancement in scientific and technological areas, 

128 | P a g e 
 
there is enhanced interest from across the world to engage with India. India’s 
diplomatic outreach to the world has also significantly increased, manifesting in India 
taking the lead in regional groupings, increase in the number of high-level 
exchanges, bilateral agreements and MOUs signed, growth in economic and 
developmental cooperation, and initiatives led by India on the international stage 
such as the International Solar Alliance (ISA), Coalition on Disaster Resilient 
Infrastructure (CDRI) etc.  
6.13 The endeavour is to maximize India’s presence abroad in the most optimal 
manner and to build a conducive environment for India’s growth and development 
through partnerships with friendly countries. Partner countries are identified for 
opening of resident Indian Missions on the basis of potential for deepening strategic 
cooperation; strong potential for bilateral trade and investment; strong diaspora 
presence; to bolster political outreach in multilateral fora; and for signalling India’s 
commitment to stepping up of diplomatic engagement with important 
countries/regions etc.
