6.15 Providing reasons for not having resident Missions in these countries above, 
the Ministry stated that the scope and depth of the bilateral relations with the 
countries where India does not have a resident Indian Mission or Post are regularly 
reviewed and Missions are opened in these countries according to need. Opening of 
a resident Indian Mission or Post is a time-taking exercise requiring internal planning 
and approvals for logistics, deployments, infrastructure as well as coordination and 
liaising with the host Government. India has diplomatic engagements with those 
countries (wherein resident Missions are not currently functional) through concurrent 
accreditation arrangements. 
 
 
 

130 | P a g e 
 
Monitoring Mechanism 
6.16 On being asked, the Ministry also provided status of the PEMS (Performance 
Evaluation Management System) portal and its efficacy in assessing the performance 
of Missions/Posts abroad and improving their operations. Ministry is currently 
evaluating the performance of 203 Missions/Posts for FY 2024-25 under PEMS 6.0. It 
may be mentioned that before the launch of PEMS 6.0 in May 2025 a comprehensive 
revision of the PEMS questionnaire for Missions/Posts and Multilateral Missions was 
conducted. In the comprehensive revision, two new areas of work related to (i) 
Human Resource Management and Welfare; and (ii) 3Ts (Trade, Tourism and 
Technology) were added.  
6.17 The introduction of PEMS portal has been instrumental in analyzing the 
performance of Missions/Posts, as it provides a clear and structured way to assess 
the functioning of Missions/Posts in various areas of work. After the evaluation, 
Missions/Posts are informed about the areas where their performance lacked to take 
further corrective steps. Further, the Divisions in Ministry are also shared the 
performance of Missions/Posts against the relevant so that they can take appropriate 
steps to further improve the compliance rate. 
6.18 Based on the PEMS evaluation, Ministry conducts physical inspection of 
selected Missions/Posts based on their numerical grading. In the physical inspection, 
inspection team conducts comprehensive inspection encompassing issues related to 
Administration, Establishment, general security & other related issues. The inspection 
findings are thereafter shared with Mission/Post with the recommended corrective 
actions. The recommendations are then followed up through the Action Taken Report 
by Mission/Post until all recommendations are complied. 
6.19 Moreover, Missions/Posts routinely raise their concerns pertaining to 
functioning of PEMS portal and PEMS questionnaire. The main concern raised is 
regarding the applicability of questions for Missions and Posts. To address this issue, 
while seeking inputs for PEMS questionnaire, Divisions of Ministry are requested to 
suggest questions that are applicable to more than 75% Missions/Posts. Another 
concern raised by Missions/Posts is regarding difficulty in sharing their responses 
and uploading supporting documents on the portal. These issues are promptly 
resolved with the assistance of technical cell. 

131 | P a g e 
 
6.20 Regarding the measures being taken to ensure the safety and security of our 
Missions/Posts abroad and provisioning through budget the Ministry specified that It 
promptly handles the overall security related requirements and matters of Indian 
missions/posts abroad, which includes deployment of security personnel, 
enhancement of security infrastructure including equipment, security clearance of 
various properties, coordinating with security forces and concerned 
ministries/organizations on security matters and related issues concerning Indian 
missions/posts abroad. Ministry also reviews existing security arrangements and 
allied procedures including contingency preparedness and issues suitable 
instructions. It conduct analyses of incidents having security implications and periodic 
threat assessment of Indian missions/posts. In addition, regular security audits of the 
missions/posts are conducted to identify and address any security related issues. 
While a separate budget head is not provisioned for security of Missions, the 
expenditure related to security matters is incurred from establishment heads as per 
requirement. 
6.21 When asked about the training and resources provided to the embassy 
officials to handle consular challenges such as illegal migration, trafficking, and other 
related issues, the Ministry responded thatSSIFS provides/facilitates elaborate 
training to officials of the Ministry to enable them to handle issues related to illegal 
migration, trafficking and other consular challenges. Elaborate sessions are 
organized by CPV (Consular, Passport and Visa) Division of the Ministry as a part of 
Induction training programs (for Officer Trainees and other officials/staff), Mid-Career 
Training Programs as well as other Promotion-Related Training Programs. Induction 
Training Programs also consists of visit to Passport Seva Kendras. 
6.22 In addition, as a part of the Induction Training Program of the Officer Trainees, 
there is a mandatory attachment with an Indian Mission abroad, during which the 
Officer Trainees are exposed to the consular activities of the Mission and the 
challenges involved. 
6.23 There are also stand-alone IVFRT (Immigration, Visa, Foreigner’s Registration 
& Tracking) program focusing on various aspects of Consular affairs, imparting 
necessary training for the non-diplomatic officials to effectively work in the Consular 
section of the Missions/Posts. 

132 | P a g e 
 
6.24 The Government accords utmost priority to the safety, security and well-being 
of Indian nationals abroad and has robust mechanism to monitor their grievances. 
Embassy officials posted in Missions abroad work under the guidance of the head of 
the Mission and instructions issued by the Ministry from time to time. Missions/Posts 
abroad remain vigilant and actively monitor, in case any Indian national in any foreign 
country is in distress or have any grievance. The Government has established 
various channels to enable Indian nationals abroad to reach out to the Mission/Post 
in case they need any assistance. They can contact the Missions/Posts through walk-
in interview, open house, email, multilingual 24x7 emergency numbers, grievance 
redressal portal like MADAD, CPGAMS, and eMigrate, and social media etc. Pravasi 
Bharatiya Sahayata Kendras (PBSK) have been set up in New Delhi and at Dubai 
(UAE), Riyadh & Jeddah (Kingdom of Saudi Arabia) and Kuala Lumpur (Malaysia). 
Missions/Posts abroad also utilize the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) to 
provide necessary assistance, including legal and repatriation, if needed, on means 
tested basis to Indian citizens abroad and their dependents in times of distress. 
6.25 The Committee were also keen to know about some of the recent 
technological innovations and initiatives that Indian Missions have adopted to 
streamline operations, improve diplomatic efficiency, and enhance service delivery. 
The Ministry informed that it gives utmost importance to Economic Diplomacy and the 
promotion of 3Ts – Trade, Technology and Tourism. Indian Missions abroad play an 
important role in identification of opportunities for Indian business and export, existing 
in their countries, and making India a preferred destination, a reliable destination for 
investment. In order to focus on promotion of 3Ts, a dashboard has been set up for 
the three parameters, which enables Missions to provide the data and progress 
related to trade, technology and tourism as well as the activities undertaken by 
Mission in pursuance to these objectives.  
6.26 Missions/Posts abroad actively monitor Ministry’s online platform named 
MADAD portal (www.madad.gov.in), which provides comprehensive consular 
assistance including, inter alia, repatriation of Indian citizens stranded abroad and 
transportation of mortal remains. In addition to this, MADAD mobile application is 
available on various mobile operating systems.  

133 | P a g e 
 
6.27 eMigrate, is an initiative by the Overseas Employment Division of MEA to 
automate emigration processes. It uses e-governance to simplify the emigration 
process. The eMigrate system integrates the offices of Protector of Emigrants (PoE) 
and Protector General of Emigrants (PGoE), optimizing their operations. Additionally, 
it electronically links all essential stakeholders, including Indian Missions, PGoE, 
PoE, Employers, Project Exporters, Recruiting Agents, Emigrants, and Insurance 
Agencies, on a unified platform for enhanced collaboration and efficiency.  
6.28 Additionally, the Directorate General of Inspections (DGI) was set-up in 2014 
to streamline the system of inspection of functioning of Indian Missions/Posts abroad. 
In 2016, the Ministry put in place a web based portal - Performance Evaluation and 
Monitoring System (PEMS) to identify Missions/Posts for physical inspection. The 
PEMS evaluation is an annual exercise where Missions/Posts are provided scores 
based on the actions taken by them in various areas of work and their compliance 
with the instructions of the Ministry. The scores generated after each evaluation gives 
Missions/Posts an indication of their performance and encourages them to work 
harder and complete all the actions recommended by Ministry in a timely manner to 
achieve higher scores. 
6.29 The Integrated Mission Accounting System (IMAS) Version 2.0 is an 
independent centrally deployed web-based platform integrated accounting software 
which covers all the accounting needs of the Indian Missions/Posts abroad. With the 
help of this application, MEA Headquarter has access to the current data pertaining 
to accounting from the Centralised server for further analysis, processing, and 
generation of reports instantly. The Ministry has also developed an online platform 
‘MEADOWS’ for effective human resource management through which 
officials/officers can apply for posting, filing of Immovable Property returns, submitting 
Annual Performance Appraisal Reports, apply for leave, office stationery and IT 
equipment etc. 
6.30 Ministry, as per directives of Capacity Building Commission, is integrating 
Mission Karmayogi within its working to focus on continuous capacity building by 
imparting uniform training/knowledge to all the personnel of the Ministry, thus 
increasing efficiency of the Ministry which is aligned with the demands of their official 

134 | P a g e 
 
duties and diplomatic mandates. The Karmayogi trainings have also been linked to 
the annual performance appraisals of the employees. 
6.31 On being asked about the manner in which the Indian Missions abroad 
collaborate with the Indian Diaspora to create positive awareness and strengthen 
India’s image globally, the Ministry informed that Indian Missions/Posts abroad 
actively engage with the Indian diaspora, recognizing them as a vital bridge between 
India and their host countries, guided by India’s diaspora engagement policy founded 
on “4Cs” – “Care”, “Connect”, “Celebrate” and “Contribute”. Indian Missions and 
Posts maintain regular engagement with diaspora communities through open houses, 
consular camps and outreach programmes, enabling welfare support, grievance 
redressal and trust-based relationships. To strengthen cultural ties and showcase 
Indian culture abroad, this Ministry supports Indian Missions/Posts abroad under the 
Promotion of Cultural Ties with Diaspora (PCTD) scheme. Youth engagement 
remains a key focus through initiatives such as the Know India Programme (KIP) and 
the Bharat Ko Jaaniye Quiz, fostering awareness of India’s heritage and 
contemporary developments amongst young diaspora members. Indian 
Missions/Posts work closely with Indian community organisations abroad to organise 
public celebrations of major festivals thereby introducing Indian traditions to the wider 
host societies.  
6.32 Further, Indian Missions/Posts also actively engage with the Indian diaspora 
by providing welfare support through the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF), 
ensuring assistance during emergencies and crises. Diaspora networks play a vital 
role in coordination during evacuation operations and other contingencies. These 
efforts by the Indian Missions/Posts enhance India’s global presence and deepen 
engagement with overseas communities. 
Human Capital 
6.33 The Committee also enquired about the main concerns regarding manpower 
at India's Missions/Posts abroad, in terms of the availability of personnel relating to 
the sanctioned and required strength. The Ministry stated that it accords highest 
priority to optimize the deployment of human resources to help fulfil its growing 
responsibilities. The Indian Embassies and Consulates are staffed in accordance with 

135 | P a g e 
 
their respective sanctioned strengths. Vacancies in Indian Missions/Posts abroad are 
regularly reviewed and officers posted against these vacancies. The Ministry 
regularly reviews the requirement of manpower at various levels in Embassies and 
Consulates abroad and takes appropriate action, including recruitment of additional 
Officers and Staff, through placement of requisite indents with the Union Public 
Service Commission (UPSC) and the Staff Selection Commission (SSC) respectively. 
6.34 Current status of manpower availability with regard to sanctioned/required 
strength - as of 01 January 2026, the current sanctioned strength of the Ministry 
stands at 6035. Out of this, 54.08 % are located in Missions and Posts abroad. These 
positions spanned various cadres within the Ministry, such as the Indian Foreign 
Service (IFS), IFS General Cadre, Branch B, Stenographers Cadre, Interpreters 
Cadre, Legal and Treaties Cadre. Missions/Posts abroad also consist of a Local 
cadre strength of 3179.

NALANDA UNIVERSITY 
7.2 Nalanda University is an international institution of higher learning established 
by an Act of Parliament — the Nalanda University Act, 2010 — with the objective of 
reviving the ancient seat of learning that flourished in Bihar between the 5th and 12th 
centuries and was renowned across Asia as a centre of scholarship and intellectual 
exchange. The University has been established as an international institution under 
the aegis of the East Asia Summit, with 17 EAS member countries as its founding 
partners, and is funded by the Government of India with contributions from partner 
countries. The Ministry of External Affairs, as the nodal Ministry for this institution, 
provides budgetary support to Nalanda University and oversees its development in 
close coordination with the University's Governing Board. 
7.3 Nalanda University commenced its academic programmes in 2014. As on 
date, the University offers postgraduate and doctoral programs across six schools: 
Historical Studies, Ecology and Environment Studies, Buddhist Studies, Philosophy & 
Comparative Religions, Languages & Literature/Humanities, Management Studies, 
and International Relations & Peace Studies. Additionally, four research centers have 
been established: The Bay of Bengal Studies, Common Archival Resource Centre, 
Centre forConflict Resolution & Peace Studies, and Nalanda University Centre, 
Neemrana, Rajasthan 

137 | P a g e

and Recurring expenditure and is as per cabinet approval. The breakdown of

7.5 Regarding the contribution of other countries, the Ministry informed that as per 
Section 9(2) of the Nalanda University Act, 2010, which outlines the objectives of the 
University, "The University shall be a non-profit public-private partnership, which shall 
seek support from each of the Member States and from other sources but shall be 
autonomous and accountable to the Governing Board." Furthermore, under the 
framework of the East Asia Summit (EAS), participating countries were encouraged 
to support the funding of Nalanda University on a voluntary basis through 

138 | P a g e 
 
contributions from governments and other sources, including public-private 
partnerships. The University has received some financial contributions from various 
foreign governments and private donors, which reflect the global collaboration in 
supporting its mission.  Details of the contributions made by the various countries and 
endowments made by private individuals are as follows: - 
A. Contributions from Foreign Governments 
(i) Government of China: Contributed USD 1 million in December 2011. 
(ii) Government of Thailand: Contributed a total of USD 1,32,600 by March 2016. 
(iii) Government of Indonesia: Contributed USD 30,000 in December 2013. 
(iv) Government of Lao PDR: Contributed USD 50,000 in March 2013. 
(v) Government of Australia: Contributed AUD 1 million (approximately USD 
737,000) in June 2016. 
(vi) Shri Krishna N. Pimple, Honorary Consulate of the United Republic of 
Tanzania: Contributed INR 6,75,000 (approximately USD 8,000) till June 2023 
under the Prof. Sunaina Singh Merit Scholarship. 
B. External Private Endowments 
(i) Rajendra & Ursula Joshi Foundation, Switzerland: Contributed USD 1 million in 
August 2015. 
(ii) Prof. Pyong Rae Lee, Korea: Contributed USD 11,535 in March 2017. 
(iii) Mr. Hung Jia Nan, Taiwan: Contributed approximately USD 10,000 in January 
2021. 
(iv) Mr. Lee Wan Lik, Ex-Chairman of Azeus Group: Contributed USD 10,667 in 
June 2023. 
7.6 The Government of India collaborates with international organizations, foreign 
governments, and donors to support Nalanda University through various channels, in 
line with the Nalanda University Act, 2010. As part of the East Asia Summit (EAS) 
framework and the Act, participating countries are encouraged to contribute 
voluntarily to the university's funding, through government donations and other 
sources like public-private partnerships. This has led to financial contributions from 
multiple foreign governments and private donors, highlighting global support for the 
university’s objectives, as mentioned in the above response. To ensure long-term 
financial sustainability, Nalanda University has a provision for the Endowment 
Committee with an intention for developing a fundraising strategy that includes 

139 | P a g e 
 
attracting philanthropic donations, corporate sponsorships, and support from global 
foundations. An endowment fund is being established to finance educational 
programs, research initiatives, faculty fellowships, and student scholarships. Efforts 
are underway to secure multi-year financial support through discussions with 
international organizations and private donors. While these efforts are key to the 
university's future, Government of India funding is crucial in the university’s early 
stages to meet international commitments and ensure operational viability. Over time, 
the goal is for the university to become more self-reliant, but this will require 
continued government support during its formative years. Indian Missions promote 
the university’s initiatives, engage with foreign academic institutions, and foster 
collaborations with international donors to ensuring Nalanda University remains a 
vital hub for global knowledge exchange and regional cooperation.    
7.7 When asked about the steps taken to address the lack of donor contributions, 
the Ministry stated that while contributions from foreign governments and private 
donors have been received from time to time, such support has varied due to 
changing global economic and national priorities. To ensure financial stability and 
continuity of operations, the Government of India has provided sustained budgetary 
support, particularly during the University’s formative years and the development of 
its campus. The Ministry of External Affairs continues to engage EAS countries and 
other partner nations through diplomatic channels to encourage voluntary 
contributions, endowments, scholarships, faculty exchanges, and academic 
collaborations. Indian Missions abroad are promoting the University in their outreach 
efforts. 
7.8 The Committee wanted to know about the progress made as well as 
challenges faced in construction of Nalanda University. In a written reply, the Ministry 
submitted that Nalanda University, an international institution of national importance, 
is undertaking a comprehensive and phased programme for the development of its 
permanent campus at Rajgir. The conceptualization of the University’s infrastructure 
initiative is deeply rooted in reviving the civilizational legacy of ancient Nalanda, whilst 
concurrently aspiring to create a contemporary, sustainable, and globally bench-
marked academic ecosystem. The University has made significant progress, 
transitioning from operations in temporary academic facilities to the commissioning of 

140 | P a g e 
 
a fully developed permanent campus. A landmark achievement in the University’s 
pursuit of infrastructure development was the formal inauguration of the new net -
zero campus in June 2024 by the Hon’ble Prime Minister.   
7.9 The University has developed a comprehensive academic infrastructure to 
support teaching, research, and academic engagement across its Schools and 
Centers. The core facilities include two fully operational Academic Blocks housing 
approximately 40 classrooms, with a combined seating capacity of about 1,890 
students. In addition, 84 faculty cabins with attached discussion rooms have been 
established to facilitate academic consultations and research activities. Laboratory 
facilities have also been created for the respective Schools to support specialized 
academic and research requirements. 
7.10 The Central Library building stands as the flagship knowledge center of the 
campus. Its structural completion was accomplished under the EPC (Engineering, 
Procurement, and Construction) model, ensuring both quality and efficiency. 
7.11 To foster a fully residential academic environment, the University has 
developed comprehensive residential and community facilities within the permanent 
campus. Student hostels currently accommodate approximately 550 residents, and 
capacity is being augmented to provide an additional 650 residential seats. Dining 
facilities have been established to cater to around 1,500 students. 
7.12 Infrastructure development at Nalanda University is closely aligned with 
principles of environmental sustainability through climate-responsive passive 
architectural design, implementation of energy-efficient systems, comprehensive 
water management and landscape-based ecological planning and emphasis on 
green mobility and reduction of carbon footprint 
7.13 The project faced several challenges. Being in Rajgir, Bihar, a remote and 
predominantly agricultural area with limited infrastructure, posed logistical and 
operational difficulties. The construction of the NET-ZERO campus required highly 
skilled manpower, which was not readily available locally. Skilled professionals had to 
be mobilized from various parts of India. Additionally, the procurement and 
transportation of high-quality construction materials to this remote location was 
complex and time-consuming. The COVID-19 pandemic in 2020-2022 further 

141 | P a g e 
 
exacerbated these challenges. Despite the Government of India granting a six-month 
blanket extension for ongoing projects under force majeure provisions, the pandemic 
caused severe disruptions during its three waves. These included supply chain 
disruptions, reverse migration of laborers, and cross-border movement restrictions, 
leading to unavoidable delays and time overrun. Despite these challenges, Nalanda 
University has undertaken the construction of its campus.     
7.14 On the Committee’s specific question about milestones achieved in terms of 
academic programs, infrastructure development, and student enrolment, the Ministry 
made the following submission: 
Nalanda University has made substantial progress in academic expansion, 
infrastructure development, and student enrollment. The University has 
transitioned from functioning in temporary facilities to operating from its fully 
developed permanent campus at Rajgir, inaugurated in June 2024 by the 
Hon’ble Prime Minister. The campus, spread across approximately 455 acres, 
comprises over 200 buildings/structures, extensive water bodies, and green 
landscapes, developed in accordance with sustainable and climate-responsive 
principles under a Net-Zero energy framework. 
Academic Programmes: The University currently has six Schools, several 
Centers of study, and over 30 academic programmes, including Master’s, Global 
Ph.D., and Post-Doctoral Fellowship programmes. Since commencing academic 
activities in 2014 with two Schools, the University has progressively expanded its 
academic portfolio. New Schools such as Management Studies (2020) and 
International Relations and Peace Studies (2024) have been added, along with 
new Centers including the Center for Bay of Bengal Studies (2022) and other 
specialized Centers in 2025. Recent academic additions include new Master’s 
programmes in Archaeology (2024), International Relations (2024), Philosophy 
(2025), Hindi (2025), Economics (2025), and Mathematics (2025), as well as 
expansion of Global Ph.D. and Post-Doctoral Fellowship streams. The University 
also runs a wide range of certificate, diploma, and short-term programmes 
catering to domestic and international participants. 
Infrastructure Development: The permanent campus is now substantially 
operational. Major construction packages relating to academic blocks, residential 
buildings, utilities, electrical systems, HVAC systems, data and security 
infrastructure, solar power generation (6.5 MW Solar Farm), solid waste 
management, landscaping, and boundary infrastructure have been completed 
(100% physical progress in most packages). The campus includes two fully 
operational academic blocks, auditoriums, a 1000-seater multipurpose hall, yoga 
and meditation center, amphitheatre, laboratories, faculty cabins, student hostels 
(capacity approximately 550, being augmented further), faculty housing, sports 

142 | P a g e 
 
complex, and cultural facilities. The Central Library building and 2000-seater 
auditorium are at advanced stages of completion under EPC mode. Ongoing 
works include select interior development and sustainability augmentation 
measures. 
Student Enrolment: Student enrollment has shown steady and significant growth. 
From an initial intake of 12 students in 2014–15, total enrollment (including 
Master’s, Global Ph.D., Post-Doctoral Fellowship, and Short-Term Programmes) 
has increased to 1,925 students in 2025–26. Of these, 658 students are enrolled 
in Master’s and Global Ph.D. programmes (317 Indian and 341 international), 
reflecting the University’s growing international character. Short-Term 
Programme enrollment stands at 1,267 for 2025–26. The rising enrollment 
trajectory indicates alignment with the University’s phased expansion plans and 
projected capacity of the new campus.” 
7.15 Responding to the Committee’s query on how Nalanda University fit into India’s 
broader foreign policy strategy, the Ministry stated that Nalanda University aligns with 
India’s foreign policy strategy by serving as a platform for regional cooperation, 
educational diplomacy, and enhancing global influence. The revival of Nalanda, with its 
historical significance as a center of knowledge, contributes to India’s efforts to rebuild 
connections with neighbouring countries and the wider international community. The 
university’s role is particularly relevant to India’s Act East Policy, which focuses on 
strengthening ties with ASEAN countries and the Indo-Pacific region. 
7.16 Through initiatives like the ASEAN-India University Network, Nalanda University 
facilitates academic collaboration and fosters deeper relationships between India and 
ASEAN nations. The university also provides a space for students from different 
countries, encouraging cross-cultural exchanges that contribute to regional integration 
and cooperation. These actions support India’s diplomatic goals of promoting stability 
and shared growth in the region. 
7.17 Nalanda University also contributes to India’s global outreach by offering training 
and educational opportunities that promote understanding across borders. By engaging 
with a diverse range of countries, including those in the Global South, the university 
helps advance India's objectives of building international relationships and increasing its 
influence in global discussions. 
7.18 The university’s work in revitalizing historical connections and focusing on 
shared heritage further supports India’s diplomatic goals of regional cooperation and 

143 | P a g e 
 
international partnership. Through these educational and collaborative efforts, 
Nalanda University plays a significant role in advancing India’s foreign policy strategy 
and its broader goals of global engagement.    
7.19 As regards the plan to strengthen Nalanda University as tools of India’s 
educational diplomacy, the Ministry informed that the government’s plan to 
strengthen Nalanda University as a tool of India’s educational diplomacy revolves 
around its strategic role in fostering international academic collaboration and regional 
integration. Nalanda University serves as a key platform for strengthening India’s 
diplomatic ties, particularly within the Southeast Asian region, and extends its 
influence globally. 
7.20 As the nodal institution of the ASEAN-India University Network (AINU), 
Nalanda University facilitates structured academic exchanges and faculty programs 
with institutions from countries such as Indonesia, Thailand, Malaysia, and Laos. 
These collaborations help in promoting sustained academic engagement within the 
region, furthering India’s educational outreach. Additionally, the university has 
entered into bilateral Memorandums of Understanding (MOUs) with institutions in 
various countries, including Spain, Sri Lanka, Cambodia, the Czech Republic, 
Indonesia, Bangladesh, South Korea, Japan, Nepal, and the United States. These 
MOUs support joint research initiatives, academic exchanges, and collaborations that 
align with India’s goal to expand its educational footprint internationally. 
7.21 Nalanda University is also strengthening its regional influence through the 
Center for Bay of Bengal Studies, which focuses on research related to transnational 
issues and cooperation in the Bay of Bengal area. This initiative serves as a hub for 
dialogue on regional challenges and promotes deeper collaboration between India 
and its neighbouring countries.         
7.22 Over the issue of academic and research collaborations between Nalanda 
University and other global institutions, and how are these efforts being funded in the 
absence of significant external contributions, the Ministry stated that Nalanda 
University has established a broad network of academic and research collaborations 
with reputed institutions in India and abroad through formal Memoranda of 
Understanding (MoUs). As detailed in the attached lists for kind perusal and 

144 | P a g e 
 
reference, the University presently maintains active bilateral MoUs with institutions 
across Asia, Europe, and North America, including partners in Japan, Republic of 
Korea, Indonesia, Vietnam, Sri Lanka, Cambodia, Nepal, Mongolia, Russia, Spain, 
the United States, the Czech Republic, and several leading Indian academic, 
research, and cultural institutions. 
7.23 These collaborations encompass joint research initiatives, faculty and student 
exchanges, academic conferences and workshops, co-publication of research, 
archival and archaeological studies, Buddhist and Asian studies, public policy, 
sustainable development, and cultural exchange programmes. Many of these 
partnerships are closely aligned with Nalanda University’s mandate of promoting 
civilizational dialogue, South-South cooperation, and knowledge exchange rooted in 
Asian perspectives. 
7.24 With regard to funding, it is submitted that a significant portion of the 
University’s academic and research collaborations is supported through internal 
budgetary provisions. In the absence of substantial direct external financial 
contributions from partner institutions or foreign governments, Nalanda University 
follows a prudent cost-sharing and resource-optimization approach. 
7.25 In addition to the above, Nalanda University is an active participant in the 
ASEAN–India Network of Universities (AINU). Under this framework, the University 
has entered into multiple MoUs with universities across ASEAN member states, 
including Vietnam, Indonesia, Myanmar, Thailand, Malaysia, and Lao PDR, as well 
as select Indian institutions.  
7.26 It is respectfully submitted that the academic and research activities 
undertaken under the AINU framework are fully funded by the ASEAN Secretariat. 
The financial support provided covers programme-related expenditures. 
Consequently, these collaborations do not place any financial burden on Nalanda 
University and are not dependent on bilateral contributions or internal budgetary 
allocations. 
7.27 Overall, Nalanda University’s academic and research collaborations have 
steadily strengthened its national and international academic profile through a 
combination of internally supported initiatives and fully funded multilateral frameworks 

145 | P a g e 
 
such as AINU. These efforts reflect careful financial planning, strategic partnerships, 
and alignment with India’s broader academic objectives.  
7.28 In view of limited financial engagement from other countries, the Committee 
further enquired how does the Ministry plan to position the university as leading 
educational. The Ministry responded that it is positioning Nalanda University as an 
institution of national importance with an international character, aligned with India’s 
broader foreign policy and educational diplomacy objectives. Rather than limiting its 
identity to South Asia, the University is being developed as a knowledge platform 
engaging the Global South, ASEAN, BIMSTEC and the wider Indo-Pacific region. Its 
academic focus on Buddhist studies, Indic knowledge systems, sustainability, 
ecology, peace studies and civilizational dialogue provides a clear and differentiated 
academic profile. 
7.29 The commissioning of the permanent Net-Zero campus in June 2024 has 
significantly strengthened the University’s institutional capacity. The campus provides 
integrated academic, residential and research infrastructure, enabling expansion in 
student intake, faculty recruitment and international collaborations. This infrastructure 
base supports the University’s long-term academic and research goals. 
7.30 With regard to financial engagement, while the Nalanda University Act 
provides for voluntary international contributions, such contributions have been 
limited and not consistent. To ensure stability and continuity, the Government of India 
has taken a leading role in funding both capital and recurring expenditure. An 
allocation of ₹200 crore has been made for FY 2025–26. This ensures that the 
University’s functioning and expansion are not dependent on fluctuating external 
funding. 
7.31 At the same time, efforts are being made to broaden the funding base through 
endowment development, partnerships with philanthropic institutions, research 
collaborations and academic networks. The objective is to build a sustainable 
financial model while retaining India’s primary responsibility in ensuring institutional 
stability and strategic direction.   
 
       

146 | P a g e 
 
SOUTH ASIAN UNIVERSITY 
7.32 South Asian University (SAU) is an international university established by the 
eight member nations of the South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation 
(SAARC), with its campus located in New Delhi. The University was established 
pursuant to an Inter-Governmental Agreement signed at the 14th SAARC Summit 
held at New Delhi in 2007, and was accorded the status of an international 
organisation by the Government of India through the South Asian University Act, 
2008. 
7.33 The Union Cabinet in July 2009 approved India's contribution of USD 198 
million (excluding land cost of 100 acres) towards capital expenditure of South Asian 
University (SAU). The actual construction at the campus project site began late in 
second half of 2015 due to the time taken to resolve the issues of Gram Sabha land, 
forest land and geo-morphological status of the allotted land. The construction 
project was initially planned for the entire campus in the master plan, however, due 
to land encumbrances, the construction work had to be split in packages. COVID-19 
and frequent National Green Tribunal (NGT) bans on construction also delayed the 
project. 
7.34 In the construction works so far, 4 packages have been taken up. Package – I 
(boundary wall) & Package II (5 buildings- faculties, guest house and staff housing) 
are completed; Package III (7 buildings- faculties, administration, South Asian 
Studies Centre, Convention Centre, Library and Utility buildings) is 98% complete. 
The scope of work for Package IV is currently under consideration. Out of the 
Cabinet approved amount of USD 198 million as capital expenditure, as on date, an 
amount of US $ 171.461 million has been disbursed to SAU.     
7.35 The capital budget of SAU is sourced only from India and is spent on creating 
necessary infrastructure (as capital assets). SAU’s operational budget is as per its 
academic year i.e. July-June. It is contributed by all the member states and India’s 
contribution is 59.8%. The operational budget for academic year 2026-27 is around 
USD 26.56million (approx. Rs. 239 crore) and the various elements of the budget are 
as below:

7.36 On being asked about contribution of other countries, the Ministry stated that 
India contributes 100% towards capital budget of SAU. The operational budget of 
SAU for academic year 2025-26 is USD 10.6 million. India contributes 59.8% 
towards the operational budget of SAU and the rest is contributed by other member 
states. Regular efforts have been made by SAU for payment by certain member 
states with regard to their pending dues. The matter has also been taken up by 
Government of India during the SAU Governing Board meetings and SAARC 
Programming Committee meetings.  
7.37 Regarding the steps being taken to address the lack of donor contributions 
from other South Asian nations and beyond, the Ministry responded that SAU has 
been established by SAARC member countries. India works with other member 
states to take all necessary efforts to support SAU on regular basis. During the 
annual SAU Governing Board meetings and SAARC Programming Committee 
meetings India takes up the matter of pending dues to SAU with the concerned 
member states requesting them to make contributions towards SAU in a timely 
manner.

7.40 The University plans to increase the capacity to 4000 students by 2029-30 in a 
phased manner by increasing the intake of the students every year. However, the 
challenge is the pending dues by member states other than India. That affects the 
day-to-day funding of the institution and creates hurdles in attracting students by 
showcasing it as a well-managed educational institution. 
 
7.41 When enquired about the manner in which SAU fit into India’s broader foreign 
policy strategy, the Ministry stated that India being a founding member of SAARC 
makes significant contributions to SAARC institutions through various initiatives in 
diverse areas and shoulders asymmetrical responsibilities to support the 
development and progress of all countries in the region. South Asian University 
(SAU) is one such initiative with an aim of creating a jointly managed centre of 
academic excellence and a symbol to provide high quality education to the youth of 
South Asia. 
7.42 On the plan to strengthen SAU as tools of India’s educational diplomacy, the 
Ministry stated since SAU is a SAARC entity and as host to the University, India 
provides 100% of the capital cost towards establishment of the University. India also 
provides scholarships (SAARC Silver Jubilee Scholarships) to eligible students at the 
University. India works with the member states during SAU Governing Board 
meetings to strengthen SAU in terms of academic programs and other measures 
needed. 
7.43 The Ministry further stated that over the years SAU has collaborated with 
following global institutions. In view of the funding issues such collaborations are 
constrained for SAU. India regularly takes up with the member states the issue of 
funding shortage due to pending dues by them. The list of collaborations is given 
below: 

151 | P a g e 
 
 
7.44 The Committee further enquired about the Ministry’s plan to position SAU as 
leading educational institutions in South Asia, particularly in light of the limited 
financial engagement from other nations in the region. The Ministry stated that as 
SAU is a SAARC institution, regular and full support including contributions by 
member states is necessary for the success of the institution. India funds 100% of the 
capital cost and 60% of the operational cost of SAU. However, taking a prominent 
role in funding and managing of SAU is constrained by the fact that SAU Governing 
Board has representation from all SAARC member states and the decisions are 
taken by consensus.

CHAPTER 8 
AUTONOMOUS BODIES AND INSTITUTIONS 
 The Ministry of External Affairs provides Grants-in-Aid to a number of 
autonomous bodies and institutions that serve as force multipliers of India's foreign 
policy, public diplomacy, and international engagement. These institutions, each with 
its own distinct character and mandate, function as force multipliers of India's foreign 
policy. Allocation under this head is towards Ministry’s Grants-in-Aid to Indian 
Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR), Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA), 
Research and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS) and other 
institutions such as International Society of International Law. The allocations made 
to these institutions are given below:

8.2 When asked about the reasons for increase in allocation from Rs.394.18 crore 
at BE to Rs.400.44 crore at RE during 2025-26, and actual utilization made, the 
Ministry provided the following reply: 
ICCR: As may be seen from the above table, no increase has been made at RE 
stage of current financial year. Enhanced allocation in BE 2026-27 has been made 
on account of expanding research & academic activities, expanding scope of cultural 
diplomacy under ICCR as well as revision in scholarship rates, cultural hospitality 
norms etc for ICCR schemes and programmes. The amount allotted in BE 2025-26 
has been fully released to ICCR and is on track for full utilization by ICCR.  
ICWA: A sum of Rs.15.48 crore was allocated to ICWA in BE 2025-26.  The 
budgetary allocation has been increased to Rs.22.36 in RE 2025-26.  A sum of Rs.16 

153 | P a g e 
 
crore has been utilized till January, 2026.  The increased allocation in RE 2025-26 
and BE 2026-27 over BE 2025-26 is necessitated by several factors such as 
increased number of activities undertaken by ICWA, translation of Pre-Departure 
Orientation Training (PDOT) Manual and PDOT Country Brief from English to Hindi 
and six other Indian languages for the use of Indian nationals visiting abroad for the 
purpose of employment, modernization of ICWA Library with necessary IT 
equipment, digitization of old newspaper clippings, proposed technical upgradation of 
website, replacement of old and unserviceable office furniture and equipment and 
redressal of long pending grievances of ICWA employees for grant of financial 
upgradation under MACP Scheme and extension of medical facility to retired 
employees of ICWA. There was no increase in expenditure until 2024-25 after an 
expenditure of Rs.15.06 crore was incurred during the year 2019-20.  
RIS: The increase in allocation at the Revised Estimates (RE) stage during 2025-26 
was necessitated on account of additional commitments arising from new and 
expanded activities undertaken by RIS, particularly in connection with India’s 
commitments in BRICS, G20, BIMSTEC, ASEAN and other international mandates. 
Further, RIS organized major international events relating to the Global South, 
development cooperation, and India’s maritime connectivity initiatives. These 
activities required additional financial support towards program implementation, 
logistics, coordination, research support, and related administrative expenditure. The 
substantial increase in BE 2026-27 is primarily due to the continuation and expansion 
of new programmes in view of India’s Chairship of BRICS, IORA and other 
international mandates such as G20, ASEAN and India-African Forum Summit 
(IAFS). RIS is also expected to undertake expanded engagements relating to the 
Global South, development cooperation initiatives, and India’s maritime connectivity 
agenda. In order to effectively discharge these responsibilities, RIS proposes to 
recruit additional human resources at various levels on a contractual basis to 
strengthen research, coordination, and administrative capacities. The enhanced 
allocation of Rs. 24.87 crore at BE 2026-27 is therefore justified to meet the 
increased programmatic activities, institutional commitments, and associated 
establishment expenditure. 
8.3 When asked about the monitoring mechanism for utilization of funds by 
autonomous bodies like ICCR, ICWA, and RIS, the ministry responded that there is 

154 | P a g e 
 
an established mechanism whereby the Ministry of External Affairs ensures 
accountability and transparency of the spending by Autonomous Bodies. The Grant-
in-Aid is disbursed by the Ministry to these bodies in instalments during each 
financial year. The utilization of funds is monitored. Each instalment of the Grant-in-
Aid is sanctioned on utilization and requirement basis. The accounts are audited by 
the Director General of Audit, Central Expenditure. The audited Annual Accounts of 
Autonomous Bodies are laid on the Table of both the Houses of Parliament every 
year. The Financial Advisor of the Ministry is a member of the Finance Committee, 
Governing Body and Governing Council of ICWA. 
8.4 Illustrating the manner in which the research and recommendations from 
autonomous bodies like ICWA and RIS into its foreign policy formulation process, the 
Ministry stated that ICWA shares research outcomes with the Ministry of External 
Affairs (MEA), which provides academic insights, and policy perspectives, on issues 
of international affairs and foreign policy. They are often incorporated into Foreign 
Policy Daily Reports and Think Tank Compendium by the Policy Planning Division for 
wider internal circulation among policy makers. 
8.5 Research insights often feed into the Ministry’s policy briefs, speeches. 
ICWA’s Annual Global Review is utilized as a reference book by the policy 
community. Sometimes they are also appropriately utilized by the Division in its 
bilateral and multilateral policy planning dialogues with other Foreign Offices, 
preparation of research papers, policy briefs and situation reports pertaining to global 
developments and international affairs in general and India’s foreign policy in 
particular. For instance, ICWA’s recent inputs related to IORA, Africa, Latin America, 
Migration and Mobility have been used appropriately in Ministry’s documents and 
speeches. 
8.6 The reports and publications emerging from ICWA’s international dialogues 
act as reference points for India’s long-term foreign policy strategy. While the 
immediate influence may not always be overt, the intellectual outputs from these 
activities often contribute to the development of India’s strategic vision, which is 
periodically articulated through several policy documents and speeches. 
8.7 The University Connect Programme pioneered by RIS for its Delhi Process 
annual international conference has been supported by the government during 

155 | P a g e 
 
India’s G20 Presidency through commissioning of G-20 Lecture Series, consisting of 
101 Lectures by eminent personalities in universities across the country to generate 
greater awareness on India’s importance and role in the G20 process.   Similarly, the 
idea of replication of India’s best digitally driven development practices in Global 
South has been supported by the Government with the establishment of DAKSHIN – 
Global South Centre of Excellence – at RIS.  The research programme of RIS in the 
field of Blue Economy has also resulted in the established of a Centre of Maritime 
Economic Cooperation at RIS with the support of Indian Ports Association for 
research on integrated development of India’s ports.  
8.8 Enumerating the steps that are being taken to increase the visibility and 
impact of these bodies, particularly in countries with strategic importance to India’s 
foreign policy, the Ministry informed that efforts are made to increase dialogues, 
regular interactions with  think tanks and universities in countries with strategic 
importance to India’s foreign policy; joint publications with experts from counterpart  
institutes; disseminate  its research publications  to MoU partners and experts 
through email and social media platforms; facilitate visiting  residential  
scholars/researchers; provide platform to  experts and scholars from strategically  
important countries to contribute research articles in its journals, special publications 
and Guest columns.  
8.9 ICCR and MEA are in the process of reviewing the locations of Indian Cultural 
Centres abroad with a view to enhancing their global presence and impact, including 
the possibility of opening new Cultural Centres in strategically important countries. 
ICCR has also onboarded 91 members of the Indian diaspora and foreign citizens to 
teach Indian dance, music, Yoga, etc. at these Cultural Centres and Missions 
abroad. Further, ICCR has been actively encouraging collaborations in a PPP format 
with local entities and diaspora organizations to scale up the impact of its activities. 
All ICCR programmes including scholarships, ICCR Chairs abroad, ICCR’s Visitors 
Programmes, cultural and academic exchanges, etc. are regularly fine-tuned to 
respond to the evolving geopolitical scenario, bilateral relations and other emergent 
realities. For instance, a new institutional dialogue on cultural cooperation has been 
launched with UAE, a strategically important partner country. 

156 | P a g e 
 
8.10 Ministry, in close coordination with Missions and Posts, is taking appropriate 
steps to enhance the visibility and footprint of RIS with its deeper engagement with 
think tanks in the Global South as part of India’s efforts to be a major Voice of Global 
South.  As part of this strategy, Government has established DAKSHIN - Global 
South Centre of Excellence - at RIS itself to promote the exchange of best 
development practices among interested partners in Global South, including wider 
replication of India’s best digitally driven development practices in the field of health, 
agriculture and financial inclusion, including UPI, to begin with.  Government is also 
engaged in the process of promotion of triangular cooperation which would ensure 
transfer of India’s development know how and technology among interested partners 
in Global South with financing from   partners in the developed world. 
 
A. Indian Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR) 
8.11 The Indian Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR) was established in 1950 with 
the primary objective of establishing, reviving and strengthening cultural relations and 
mutual understanding between India and other countries. ICCR operates under the 
administrative oversight of the Ministry of External Affairs and is funded through 
grants-in-aid from the Ministry. ICCR maintains a network of Indian Cultural Centres 
in countries across Asia, Africa, Europe and the Americas, which serve as hubs for 
the promotion of Indian languages, classical and folk arts, music, dance, cinema, and 
literature. The Council administers a large portfolio of cultural exchange 
programmes, scholarship schemes for foreign students, and artist and scholar 
exchange initiatives, and facilitates the participation of Indian artists and cultural 
delegations in international festivals and events. 
8.12 The Committee desired to know the role played by MEA in ICCR and 
contributions of the latter in carrying out the foreign policy goals. The Ministry stated 
that MEA provides diplomatic guidance with regard to all ICCR’s activities and 
initiatives which are undertaken in close consultation with Indian 
Missions/Consulates/ ICCR’s Cultural Centres abroad. ICCR’s outreach initiatives 
like the ICCR’s India Chairs in foreign universities; visits of distinguished foreigners 
under ICCR’s Visitors Programmes; reciprocal exchanges of artists’ troupes with 
partner countries; organization of conferences, seminars, art exhibitions and art 
residencies; running of Indian Cultural Centres to disseminate and teach Indian 

157 | P a g e 
 
culture, installations of busts and statues of iconic Indian leaders, etc. are undertaken 
by ICCR in close coordination with Indian Missions and MEA. ICCR also executes 
annual commemorative events like the International Day of Yoga on behalf of MEA in 
close liaison with Indian embassies/consulates. Last year, more than 2000 IDY 
events were held in 193 countries. MEA exercises administrative and financial 
control over ICCR’s functioning. All the above activities of ICCR, including new 
stand-alone initiatives, are animated by specific foreign policy objectives. For 
instance, ICCR organized the first BIMSTEC Traditional Music Festival in India in 
August 2025; assisted in organizing several elements of PBD 2025 and facilitated 
international participation in the Mahakumbh in Prayagraj in 2025. In the past, ICCR 
has similarly supported MEA in organizing mega events like the G20 Summit, BRICS 
Summit, SCO, ASEAN, SAARC, LUSOPHONE Summit etc. ICCR also administers 
12 Scholarship schemes on behalf of MEA, based on MEA’s foreign policy priorities 
and guidance. ICCR is similarly guided by MEA’s diplomatic objectives and 
instructions while implementing its 21 Scholarship schemes. 
8.13 Further describing the manner in which ICCR can play a more effective role in 
policy planning, research and foreign policy formulations, the Ministry stated that as 
As part of its mandate, ICCR organizes Seminars and Conferences on specific 
aspects of India's foreign policy and culture, in India and abroad. For instance, ICCR 
has organized Conferences to promote Indology, Buddhist linkages, Ramayana, 
Sanskrit, Hindi, Yoga, Democracy, Indian spirituality, Ayurveda, etc. ICCR also 
provides short-term Research Fellowships and long-term PhD fellowships to 
foreigners interested in researching specific aspects of India and Indian heritage. It 
also gives Travel Grants to support participation in international academic events. In 
all these activities, ICCR liaises closely with MEA’s PP&R Division to ensure greater 
synergy in realizing our shared foreign policy objectives and scaling up their impact.

 8.18 Regarding availability of extra budgetary resources with ICCR, the Ministry 
stated that ICCR is fully funded by MEA through Grants-in-Aid. ICCR has an 
Auditorium and Art Gallery at its Regional Office in Kolkata and an Auditorium at 
Delhi (HQ). ICCR generates a meager revenue through renting out these facilities. 
Besides, some Indian Cultural Centres abroad charge modest fees from local 

160 | P a g e 
 
students for learning yoga, Indian Music and Dance etc. ICCR has no further source

8.19 Elaborating on the efforts being made by ICCR to further generate additional 
resources, the Ministry stated that since ICCR’s Cultural Centres abroad are either 
co-located in our Embassies or are treated as adjunct diplomatic entities of 
Embassies, they are restricted by local laws from engaging in commercial activities. 
Even at ICCR HQs, ICCR has forged partnerships with Indian entities like NDMC and 
DDA in hosting cultural events as well as entities promoting Indian culture overseas. 
8.20 In terms of the budget allocation, the Committee asked about the key cultural 
programs or initiatives that have received the most funding in recent years. The 
Ministry stated: 
Scholarships and Welfare of Foreign Students: In the academic year 2025-26, ICCR 
offered 3960 scholarship slots to foreign nationals under its 21 Scholarship 
Schemes, including 1000 scholarships for Afghan nationals to pursue online Under 
Graduate/Post Graduate (UG/PG) courses. Scholarships for foreign nationals to 
study in India is an important tool to leverage India’s soft power and cultural 
diplomacy besides positioning India as a global education hub. ICCR scholarships 
also provide an opportunity for foreign students to appreciate Indian arts, heritage 
and culture and become informal Ambassadors of India abroad. For instance, the 
current Prime Minister of Sri Lanka and High Commissioner of Sri Lanka to India, are 
both ICCR alumni. 
 
Outgoing Cultural Delegations: ICCR in its efforts to create international 
understanding through culture, sponsored 71 cultural troupes of different 
genres such as Kathak, Mohiniattam, Bharatanatyam, Manipuri, Odissi, Bollywood 
etc. and Folk Dance & Music Groups (Punjabi, Assamese, Gujarati & Rajasthani etc.)  
to showcase Indian art forms to foreign audiences. 

161 | P a g e 
 
Indian Cultural Centres Abroad: ICCR operates 38 Indian Cultural Centres in foreign 
countries and has deployed 83 India based teachers and 91 Local Resource Persons 
to teach Indian music, dance, languages and yoga at these Cultural Centres and our 
Embassies and Consulates abroad. The fact that there are more members of the 
Indian diaspora and local foreigners teaching Indian culture at ICCs as compared to 
India-based Teachers, shows ICCR’s success in enlisting local partners for India’s 
culture promotion abroad. 
International Day of Yoga: To promote Yoga worldwide, ICCR facilitated the 
celebration of the 11th International Day of Yoga on June 21, 2025 in206 
Missions/Posts abroad. Over 2000 IDY events at 1300 venues in 193 countries were 
organized worldwide, the highest so far. 
 
Incoming Cultural Delegations (ICD): ICCR has organized large scale international 
cultural festivals/events/progammes such as International Dance & Music Festival, 
International Folk Festival, ‘Year of connecting the world through Ramayana 2024’ as 
well as cultural events alongside other multilateral and bilateral engagements such 
as India’s SCO Presidency, Festival of Russian Culture in India, Pravasi Bharatiya 
Diwas, 1st BIMSTEC Traditional Music Festival, International Festival in Maha 
Kumbh Mela. (Details of the key cultural programmes/achievements in recent years 
placed at Annexure VII)   
These initiatives have served as a platform to not only showcase India’s rich 
cultural heritage and diversity but also promote cultural and artistic 
exchanges/collaborations, fostering people to people ties, thereby advancing India’s 
cultural diplomacy objectives. 
8.21 The Committee also sought to know about the budget allocated for 
scholarships and educational exchanges under ICCR, and how does this fund 
contribute to promoting India’s cultural ties with other countries. In a written 
submission, the Ministry stated that duringthe financial year 2024-25, ICCR has 
incurred an amount of Rs.87.33 Crores for handling the scholarship schemes from its 
own budget, Rs.157.60 Crores for the scholarships handled by ICCR on behalf of 
Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) and Rs.6.55 Crores for the scholarships handled 
by ICCR on behalf of the Ministry of AYUSH.   Scholarship has been an important 

162 | P a g e 
 
tool to leverage India’s soft power and cultural diplomacy, positioning India as a 
global education hub besides nurturing young minds to become future ambassadors 
of India. Since the scholarship programme of ICCR is being run for more than four 
decades, the students who studied in India are now working in their governments 
from functional to supervisory level.  Many alumni of ICCR have attained important 
positions in their respective countries and they act as goodwill ambassadors for India 
in their respective countries.  During 2025-26, 3649 students have availed ICCR 
scholarships and the top 5 recipient countries were Bangladesh (556), Sri Lanka 
(176), Nepal (170), Tanzania (85) and Kenya (84). The current Prime Minister of Sri 
Lanka and High Commissioner of Sri Lanka to India, are both ICCR alumni. 
8.22 As regard to plans to increase the budget or expand the scope of scholarships 
in the coming years, the Ministry stated that it is gradually enhancing ICCR’s budget 
for scholarships as also enhancing the budget allocation for the scholarship schemes 
run by ICCR on behalf of the Ministry.  ICCR administers 1476 scholarship slots from 
its own budget, 2497 slots on behalf of the MEA and 74 slots on behalf of AYUSH 
Ministry.  Total number of scholarships slots will be enhanced during the A.Y. 2026-
27 in comparison to the A.Y. 2025-26.  ICCR has also been working closely with the 
academic institutions to ensure a high standard for academic studies as well as 
infrastructural facilities for foreign students ensuring that these students go back to 
their respective countries as India’s goodwill Ambassadors.  
8.23 Asked about Ministry’s assessment of the impact of these cultural programs 
on India's global image and diplomatic relations, the latter responded that though 
harder to quantify, ICCR gauges soft power diplomacy/impact through the number of 
cultural exchanges partnerships with various stakeholders; collaborations with 
organisations/entities in host country, prominent diaspora organisations, educational 
institutes etc., ICCR also assesses the success of its initiatives on parameters such 
as: audience response, feedback received from Indian Embassies/ Consulates / 
Indian  Cultural   Centres,  number   of   cultural  events / festivals   held   in   India,  
number  of performances in India, number of countries and artists participating in 
these programmes, public outreach, media coverage, social media engagement etc.  
8.24 ICCR’s network of Indian Cultural Centres (ICCs) worldwide functions as a 
hub for cultural outreach. The performance and impact of these centres is assessed 

163 | P a g e 
 
through quality and number of programmes delivered, engagement levels, 
partnerships formed. Similarly, Scholarships for foreign nationals to study in India is 
an important tool to leverage India’s soft power and cultural diplomacy besides 
positioning India as a global education hub. ICCR scholarships also provide an 
opportunity for foreign students to appreciate Indian arts, heritage and culture and 
become informal Ambassadors of India abroad. For instance, the current Prime 
Minister of Sri Lanka and High Commissioner of Sri Lanka to India, are both ICCR 
alumni. 
8.25 The ICCR Chairs Programme has been one of the key instruments of India’s 
cultural diplomacy. Due to interests in Indian Studies, there has been enormous 
demand for establishing Indian Chairs in renowned Universities across the world. As 
a result, ICCR has signed 57 MoUs to establish Indian Chairs in foreign Universities. 
8.26  Since the Ist International Day of Yoga celebrated in 2015, the popularity of 
Yoga across the world continues to grow. Last year, more than 2000 IDY events 
were celebrated in 193 countries, the highest ever so far.  
8.27 Due to these continuous efforts to enhance India’s cultural footprint globally, 
Indian art and culture has been recognised across the globe, with the UN’s 
inscription of ‘Yoga’, ‘Diwali’, ‘Garba’, ‘Kalbelia’, ‘Ramayan’ in UNESCO’s intangible 
cultural heritage (ICH) list. 
8.28  On being asked about coordination of ICCR with other Ministries (such as the 
Ministry of External Affairs, Ministry of Tourism, and Ministry of Education) in the 
allocation and utilization of its budget for cultural diplomacy, the Ministry stated that 
ICCR administers 21 scholarship schemes including 6 scholarship schemes (1476 
slots) with ICCR’s own budget and 12 scholarship schemes (2497 slots including 
1000 slots for Afghan Nationals for online course) on behalf of the Ministry of 
External Affairs and 3 scholarship schemes (74 slots) on behalf of Ministry of AYUSH 
to facilitate studying of foreign students in India. ICCR regularly accommodates the 
requests to sponsor/ facilitate cultural programmes to coincide with various 
prominent events in India and abroad being organized by other GoI 
Ministries.  These are generally catered through ICCR funds. Similarly, ICCR closely 
works with Ministry of Culture in implementation of Cultural Exchange Programmes 
for which the Ministry of Culture is Nodal Ministry. ICCR is also the nodal agency for 

164 | P a g e 
 
India-UAE Cultural Council which has various stakeholders/Ministries under different 
tracks of cooperation. ICCR also coordinates with various Ministries/Departments 
such as Ministry of Tourism, National Archives of India, Nalanda University, Ministry 
of Youth Affairs, Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways etc. for various initiatives 
of ICCR. 
ICCR’s Cultural Centres 
8.29 ICCR’s Cultural Centres are the principal instrument of India’s institutional 
outreach.  The Indian Cultural Centres abroad play a vital role in projecting India's 
soft power, promoting cultural diplomacy, and strengthening international relations. 
With 38 centres worldwide, these institutions serve as hubs for disseminating India's 
rich cultural, artistic, and academic heritage.  Also, to promote Yoga, Veda and 
Sanskrit globally, the Council deployed 54 Teachers of Indian Culture (TICs) at these 
ICCs. 
 
8.30 The Council has been operating 38 Indian Cultural Centres (ICCs) in different 
parts of the world. 
1) Swami Vivekanand Cultural Centre, Georgetown, Guyana 
2) Swami Vivekanand Cultural Centre, Paramaribo, Suriname 
3) Indira Gandhi Centre for Indian Culture, Port Louis, Mauritius 
4) Jawaharlal Nehru Indian Cultural Centre, Jakarta, Indonesia 
5) Jawaharlal Nehru Cultural Centre, Moscow, Russia 
6) The Tagore Centre, Berlin, Germany 
7) Maulana Azad Centre for Indian Culture, Cairo, Egypt 
8) The Nehru Centre, London, United Kingdom 
9) Indian Cultural Centre Tel Aviv, Israel 
10) Swami Vivekanand Cultural Centre, Durban, South Africa 
11) Lal Bahadur Shastri Centre for Indian Culture, Tashkent, Uzbekistan 
12) Swami Vivekanand Cultural Centre, Astana, Kazakhstan 
13) Mahatma Gandhi Institute for Cultural Co-operation, Port of Spain, 
Trinidad & Tobago 
14) Swami Vivekanand Cultural Centre, Colombo, Sri Lanka
