8.31 About plans to further expand the footprints of Indian culture and soft power 
by opening more cultural centres, especially in those regions where Indian diaspora 
may not be present in large numbers, the Ministry responded that to further expand 
the footprints of Indian culture and soft power, ICCR and MEA are working on to make the 
Cultural Centres abroad more effective and to cater to key strategic locations 
including in those countries where Indian diaspora is not present in large numbers. 
8.32 The Committee also desired to know whether there are any objective criteria 
for setting up of new cultural centres. The Ministry stated that setting up of Indian 
Cultural Centres abroad is part of ICCR’s efforts to promote India's soft power, foster 
bilateral relations driven by strategic importance and the potential for cultural 
engagement across the world. 
8.33 Responding to specific question about the status of construction of ICCR 
Cultural Centres at Washington and Paris, the Ministry stated as under: 
Swami Vivekananda Cultural Centre (SVCC), Paris: The renovation work at SVCC 
Paris has been completed and the Centre is operational now. The delay in 
renovation of the property was mainly due to reconciliation in divergence of tendering 

166 | P a g e 
 
practices, meeting norms for fire safety, selection of contractors, bankruptcy of a 
major structural contractor (without which other works could not be initiated and 
completed) mid-way in 2019 because of which the balance works had to be 
undertaken through the hiring of a new contractor. The renovation work schedule 
was also adversely affected by COVID 19 lockdown besides strict restrictions 
imposed in central Paris from June 2024 to September 2024 due to the Paris 
Olympics.  
Indian Cultural Centre (ICC), Washington DC: The delay in the renovation of the 
property to make it suitable for use as Cultural Centre was on account of issues 
pertaining to change in local building codes, structural issues and deteriorating 
security situation in the neighbourhood. The delay was compounded by the 
intervening period of the Covid Pandemic. In April 2024, a Property Management 
Team (PMT) visited Washington DC to assess and inspect Government-owned 
properties, including the U Street property (proposed for Cultural Centre). In its 
subsequent report on the visit, the PMT recommended that the property may either 
be sold or put to some other use. Subsequently, Mission examined the matter and 
endorsed the recommendations of the PMT that the property should be sold. The 
matter is being examined by the Ministry.  
8.34 When asked about the cost has been incurred on their construction so far, the 
Ministry gave the following reply:  
Cost of renovation for SVCC, Paris: Euro 3,041,529.68 (excluding VAT) till 
28.02.2025. 
Cost of Construction (ICC, Washington): An amount of US$ 4,41,581.91 towards 
consultancy services till 28.02.2025. 
8.35 On the Committee’s query about steps taken for empanelment of artists from 
all parts of the country, the Ministry replied that ICCR empanels Indian artists and 
groups across a wide range of performing art disciplines.  The empanelment list 
maintained by ICCR includes artists from all parts of the country, covering a 
spectrum of all Indian art forms. Empanelment list reflects representation of 
performers from different States and Union Territories in both classical and folk 
traditions. Over 2000 artists from across the country are on ICCR’s artists’ panel. 

167 | P a g e 
 
8.36 Apprising the Committee about the status of restructuring of ICCR as 
recommended by this Committee in their Reports, the Ministry stated that in the re-
organization of ICCR’s work and to ensure efficiency and economy in its operations, 
and taking all aspects into consideration, the Statutory Bodies of ICCR had decided 
to merge seven Regional Offices namely Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Chennai, Goa, 
Hyderabad, Trivandrum and Varanasi with the Zonal Offices which became 
operational w.e.f. 1st July, 2022. Consequent upon this restructuring, presently ICCR 
has its Zonal, Sub-Zonal and other offices in 11 cities of the country. These offices 
essentially coordinate and facilitate the Council’s activities within their respective 
jurisdictions. They oversee disbursal of ICCR scholarships and international 
students’ welfare; facilitate international cultural and academic exchanges in 
collaboration with state governments, local organizations and universities; and 
identify and promote local artists.  
8.37 The establishment of Indian Cultural Centres (ICCs) abroad has taken place 
over multiple decades, reflecting the relative political, cultural and economic 
significance of the location. In many cases, that has undergone a change, resulting in 
location of Cultural Centres in countries of less importance and the neglect of some 
that have grown in importance. To rectify this anomaly, ICCR and MEA are in the 
process of reviewing the locations of Indian Cultural Centres abroad with a view to 
enhancing their global presence and impact, including the possibility of opening new 
Cultural Centres in strategically important countries. 
8.38 Elaborating the manner in which ICCR can play a more effective role in 
promotion of India’s soft power and cultural diplomacy abroad, the Ministry stated 
that ICCR’s mandate has evolved and grown over the years, in keeping with India’s 
rising global profile. ICCR’s Cultural Diplomacy is today an intrinsic part of India’s soft 
power projection abroad and is undertaken in close liaison with our Missions/Posts 
abroad, bearing in mind local interests, sensitivities and specificities. All ICCR 
activities, including those conducted through its extended arms Indian Cultural 
Centers, the Chairs of Indian Studies in Foreign Universities, and the foreign 
students’ scholarships programme, are conducted in close coordination with Indian 
Missions/posts in the country concerned. Given the importance of ensuring that our 
cultural outreach aligns with India’s diplomatic objectives and priorities, calendar of 

168 | P a g e 
 
activities and local realities, ICCR’s soft power projection and outreach is tailored to 
suit the unique needs and realities of each partner country and the specificity of our 
bilateral relationships. To achieve the above, ICCR would need more budgetary 
allocations and resources. 
 
B. Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) 
8.39 The Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) is one of India's oldest and most 
established think-tanks on foreign policy and international relations, founded in 1943 
and accorded the status of a statutory body under the Indian Council of World Affairs 
Act, 2001. Located at Sapru House in New Delhi, ICWA serves as a premier 
institution for research, dialogue, and public discourse on India's foreign policy and its 
engagement with the world. The Council organises seminars, conferences, and track-
1.5 and track-2 dialogues with counterpart institutions from across the globe, 
fostering exchanges that supplement India's formal diplomatic engagements. 
8.40 An outlay of Rs. 15.48 crore was provided for ICWA during BE 2025-26 which 
has been increased to Rs. 22.36 crore at the RE stage. The budgetary allocation for 
2026-27 at BE is Rs. 28.88 crore. The Ministry has stated that the enhanced 
allocation in BE 2026-27 has been made on account of salaries and capital assets. 
8.41 Regarding the manner in which the Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) 
contribute to the formulation and implementation of India’s foreign policy, the MEA 
stated that through sustained conferences, bilateral dialogues, high-level lectures, 
and policy discussions, conducted in close coordination with the MEA, ICWA has 
contributed meaningfully to India’s foreign policy discourse. The Council’s activities 
have: Facilitated structured exchange of ideas between policymakers, diplomats, 
scholars, and international partners; Generated policy-relevant recommendations 
shared with the MEA; Strengthened India’s discourse on multilateral reform, regional 
connectivity, global South cooperation, maritime security, and emerging technologies; 
Enhanced public diplomacy and intellectual outreach. 
8.42 The details of expenditure incurred by ICWA during last three years are given 
below: 
 

169 | P a g e 
 
Year Total Expenditure  
2022-23 Rs.13.91 crore 
2023-24 Rs.13.68 crore 
2024-25 Rs.14.52 crore 
 
8.43 A sum of Rs.22.36 crore has been allocated in RE 2025-26. Increase in RE 
2025-26 over the budgetary allocation of last financial year 2024-25 is necessitated 
by several factors such as increased number of activities undertaken by ICWA, 
translation of Pre-Departure Orientation Training (PDOT) Manual and PDOT Country 
Brief from English to Hindi and six other Indian languages for the use of Indian 
nationals visiting abroad for the purpose of employment, modernization of ICWA 
Library with necessary IT equipment, digitization of old newspaper clippings, 
proposed technical upgradation of website, replacement of old and unserviceable 
office furniture and equipment such as Computers, Printers, ACs and Audio System 
of Research Wing Conference Room, enhancement of emoluments of research 
faculty members, and redressal of long pending grievances of ICWA employees for 
grant of financial upgradation under MACP Scheme and extension of medical facility 
to retired employees of ICWA.   
8.44 When asked whether in ICWA been able to attain its stated objective, the 
Ministry replied that the Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) has been able to 
attain its stated objectives. As outlined in Section 13 of the ICWA Act, 2001, the 
Council’s mandate is to promote the study of Indian foreign policy and international 
affairs with a view to developing an informed body of opinion on global issues, and to 
promote India’s relations with other countries through discussions and exchange of 
ideas with institutions engaged in similar activities. It is also mandated to serve as a 
clearing house of information and knowledge on world affairs, and to publish books, 
periodicals, reviews, papers, and monographs on issues of topical interest.  In 
pursuance of these objectives, ICWA has significantly strengthened its policy 
research initiatives to enhance understanding of international affairs from an Indian 
perspective and to serve as a repository of informed analysis on foreign policy 
matters. The Council has expanded its publication output, bringing out research 
papers in the form of commentaries, special reports, guest columns, and thematic 

170 | P a g e 
 
publications, in addition to books on a wide range of international issues. Its flagship 
journal, India Quarterly, continues to be published regularly and has been well 
received by stakeholders. 
8.45 In the year 2025, ICWA published 180 research papers by in-house experts, 12 
special publications featuring contributions from eminent scholars, and 9 books. The 
journal India Quarterly was brought out regularly during the year. The Council organized 
over 100 intellectual activities, including lectures, conferences, panel discussions, 
dialogues, and interactions with foreign dignitaries on themes of contemporary 
relevance and importance in international affairs. In addition, ICWA collaborated in and 
supported 45 outreach conferences organized by universities and research institutes. 
8.46 The Committee sought details about the conferences organized by ICWA and 
the tangible policy outcomes that have emerged out of them that were beneficial in 
framing foreign political agenda. The Ministry stated that in the past three years, the 
Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) has organized a wide range of international 
and national conferences, bilateral dialogues (Track II and Track 1.5), lectures, panel 
discussions, and round tables on issues of contemporary global significance. These 
engagements have contributed to informed policy discourse and have supported the 
framing and refinement of India’s foreign policy agenda. 
International Conferences and Seminars: Over the past three years, ICWA has 
convened several major international conferences and seminars addressing strategic, 
geopolitical, developmental, and governance-related themes. Key conferences 
included: 
i. International Seminar on “Celebrating 75 Years of Indian Foreign Policy”, 
which reviewed India’s foreign policy trajectory since independence and 
deliberated on future priorities, particularly India’s engagement with the Global 
South and its vision of becoming a developed nation by 2047. 
ii. International Conference on “Shanghai Cooperation Organization: 
Reconnect ~ Rejuvenate”, organized in association with the Ministry of 
External Affairs (MEA) during India’s SCO Chairmanship. The conference 
emphasized regional connectivity (including through INSTC), counter-terrorism 

171 | P a g e 
 
cooperation, combating radicalism, drug trafficking, and misuse of cyberspace, 
and strengthening multilateral engagement within the SCO framework. 
iii. Conference on “India and the Reformed Multi-Polar World Order”, which 
examined India’s evolving role in shaping a reformed and increasingly 
multipolar global order. Discussions highlighted the need to balance ideology 
and pragmatism, values and interests, nationalism and internationalism, while 
effectively deploying soft power. 
iv. Curtain-Raiser Seminar on the UN Summit of the Future, focusing on 
sustainable development, reform of global governance, the Pact of the Future, 
and exploring mechanisms such as Article 109 of the UN Charter for 
institutional reform. 
v. International Conference on “Women Across Geographies: Issues and 
Perspectives”, which explored themes like the purdah system, prostitution, 
polygamy, pornography, widow remarriage, age of consent and women in 
peace and security.  It explored gender dynamics in global politics, women’s 
role in peace and security, and the importance of women’s leadership in 
addressing violence, radicalism, and social transformation.  
These conferences witnessed wide participation from scholars, diplomats, think 
tanks, and experts from India and abroad. The key recommendations and policy-
relevant insights emerging from the deliberations were shared with the Ministry of 
External Affairs. Conference proceedings were subsequently published, contributing 
to academic scholarship and policy discourse globally. The discussions supported 
India’s engagement on themes such as Global South solidarity, multilateral reform, 
regional connectivity, gender and peacebuilding, and the shaping of a multipolar 
world order. 
Bilateral Dialogues (Track II and Track 1.5): ICWA organized several bilateral 
Track II dialogues with its MoU partners in Russia, Uzbekistan, China, Poland, South 
Korea, Hungary, Spain, New Zealand, Vietnam, Qatar, and Peru. Track 1.5 dialogues 
were held with Germany and the United Kingdom, and interactions were also 
conducted with experts from leading U.S. think tanks. These dialogues facilitated 
structured exchanges on global, regional, and bilateral issues of mutual concern. 
They brought together Indian analysts and scholars from universities and research 
institutions to engage with counterpart delegations. Heads of Indian Missions and 

172 | P a g e 
 
foreign Heads of Missions in New Delhi regularly participated, enhancing diplomatic 
engagement. The dialogues contributed to greater mutual understanding, 
identification of convergences, and nuanced appreciation of policy sensitivities, 
thereby indirectly strengthening India’s bilateral engagements and supporting foreign 
policy formulation. 
Sapru House Lectures: Under the Sapru House Lecture Series, several distinguished 
global leaders and dignitaries addressed informed Indian audiences. These included: 
i. Prime Minister of Fiji, H.E. S.L. Rabuka 
ii. President of Chile, H.E. Gabriel Boric Font 
iii. Prime Minister of Malaysia, H.E. Dato’ Seri Anwar bin Ibrahim 
iv. Prime Minister of Viet Nam, H.E. Pham Minh Chinh 
v. Foreign Ministers of Armenia, Cyprus, Maldives, and North Macedonia 
vi. Secretary-General of ASEAN 
vii. Secretary-General of the Shanghai Cooperation Organization 
viii. President of the 78th UN General Assembly 
ix. Vice-President of the Dominican Republic 
x. Vice-Minister of Foreign Affairs of Guatemala 
These lectures provided high-level perspectives on bilateral ties, regional 
developments, and global governance, enriching public diplomacy and strengthening 
India’s diplomatic outreach. 
Panel Discussions and Round Tables: ICWA organized numerous panel discussions 
and round tables on themes of contemporary relevance, including: 
i. North East Asia’s evolving strategic landscape 
ii. Developments in the South Caucasus 
iii. Sahelian politics 
iv. Securing the Indian Ocean Region 
v. India’s role in global governance and multilateralism 
vi. India and the Global South 
vii. India’s neighbourhood and maritime vision 
viii. India’s Eurasia outlook and the Indo-Pacific 
ix. US–China strategic competition and multipolarity 

173 | P a g e 
 
x. Civilizational states and migration trends 
xi. Technological developments in international relations, including Artificial 
Intelligence 
xii. Ongoing conflicts such as Ukraine and Gaza 
These discussions generated informed analysis and policy-oriented insights that 
were shared with relevant stakeholders. They supported strategic thinking on 
emerging geopolitical trends, multilateral reform, regional security, maritime strategy, 
and technological transformation in global affairs Collectively, these initiatives 
have supported informed policy formulation and enriched the broader strategic and 
foreign policy ecosystem of India. 
8.47 Specifying the research and academic activities undertaken by ICWA during 
the last three years, the Ministry stated that during the last three years, the Indian 
Council of World Affairs (ICWA) has undertaken extensive research and academic 
activities in support of informed policy discourse and India’s foreign policy objectives. 
The Council’s work spans policy-oriented research, publications, commissioned 
studies, book projects, journal publications, and translations. 
Research Publications: ICWA’s Research Faculty conducted in-depth studies on 
major political, security, and economic developments across Asia, Africa, Europe, the 
United States, Latin America, and the wider global strategic environment, analysing 
their regional and global implications for India.In the past three years, the Council has 
published: 
 250 Issue Briefs 
 206 Viewpoints 
 53 Special Reports 
These publications address topical issues of relevance to Indian foreign policy and 
are regularly disseminated through the Council’s website. 
Sapru House Papers: The Council published 12 Sapru House Papers, which are 
comprehensive, future-oriented policy research studies taking a long-term 
perspective on key issues in international affairs. These papers undergo external 
peer review to ensure academic rigour and quality.The themes covered include 
China’s evolving role (Indo-Pacific, Arab world, Japan, Russia, Nepal, Party 
Congress), neighbourhood dynamics (Sri Lanka, Afghanistan), engagement with 

174 | P a g e 
 
small states (including SICA countries), developments in West Asia, Central Asia and 
Africa, connectivity issues in Southeast Asia and the Arctic, and bilateral relations 
with countries such as South Korea and Australia. 
Special Publications: ICWA commissioned several articles by external experts viz., 
academics and, former diplomats on range of important subjects for its Special 
Publications. In the past three years it has come out with 31 Special Publications by 
distinguished external experts, including academics and former diplomats. These 
addressed contemporary themes such as.  Maritime Domain Awareness, India’s 
Space and Satellite Diplomacy, Ideology in Latin America, Rising Significance of 
Critical Minerals in Africa, Migration and Mobility, Foreign Policy in the Amrit Kaal, the 
United Nations @ 80, SAGAR vision and its Realization, Downfall of the State of 
Pakistan, India-Bhutan relations, 60 years of India-Malta diplomatic relations and 
Geo-Political Churn in South Asia ,Women – led Development: India’s G20 
Presidency; US-China Strategic Contestation, Impact of Foreign Policy on Daily Lives 
in India; Contribution of the Indian Presidency to the G20 Process; African Common 
Position on UN Reform and its Limitations; Thirty Years of the 1994 Moscow 
Declaration; Iran - US Talks. It also published Guest Columns by experts which 
focused on issues related to the Indus Water Treaty, Myanmar Peace Process, Iran-
US talks, UN General Assembly and National Ceasefire Agreement.   
Book Publications and Research Grants: Over the last three years, ICWA has 
published 31 books, comprising: 
23 single-authored and edited volumes 
8 conference proceedings 
Recent titles cover themes such as Afghanistan under the Taliban, India’s maritime 
power, India–EU FTA prospects, Indian Parliament Shaping Foreign Policy, Regional 
economic integration and security, International law, BBIN cooperation, France in the 
Indian Ocean, and ICWA Global Review 2023 and ICWA Global Review 2024. 
Books authored by outside experts are commissioned by the Council through its 
Book Research Grant scheme to eminent experts. Currently28 book projects are at 
various stages of completion. These projects span area studies and thematic issues, 
including Indian foreign policy, India’s neighbourhood, Indians in South Africa, Indo-

175 | P a g e 
 
Pacific security, Agriculture and national security, think tanks and international affairs, 
and India’s engagement in the SCO. 
India Quarterly (Flagship Journal): ICWA continues to publish its peer-reviewed 
journal, India Quarterly (since 1945), which is indexed in reputed international 
databases including SCOPUS and International Political Science Abstracts. The 
journal features contributions from Indian and international scholars. Hindi edition is 
also published to broaden outreach and academic engagement. 
Translations and Outreach: To expand accessibility, ICWA has translated its Issue 
Briefs, Viewpoints, Sapru House Papers, and Special Reports into Hindi for 
publication on its Hindi website. In the last three years, the Council has published four 
translated books (two in Hindi, one in Bangla, and one in Urdu). 
ICWA has been at the forefront among Indian think tanks in publishing Hindi content 
on international affairs, which has been widely appreciated by academic institutions, 
particularly in the Hindi-speaking regions. Through sustained research, high-quality 
publications, commissioned studies, academic journal contributions, and expanded 
linguistic outreach, ICWA has strengthened its role as a leading policy-oriented think 
tank supporting India’s foreign policy discourse and engagement. 
8.48 Regarding the manner in which ICWA helps in promoting India's interests in 
the international community, the Ministry stated that the Indian Council of World 
Affairs (ICWA) plays an important role in advancing India’s interests in the 
international community by serving as a premier institution for policy-relevant 
research, strategic dialogue, and intellectual exchange on global affairs. Through 
structured dialogues, high-level lectures, conferences, and research publications, 
ICWA articulates and projects well-informed Indian perspectives on regional, global, 
and bilateral issues. Its engagements facilitate candid discussions on emerging 
geopolitical trends, identify areas of cooperation, and contribute to long-term strategic 
understanding with partner countries. 
8.49 The following are the specific programmes and initiatives undertaken by ICWA 
to engage with global think tanks, academic institutions, and foreign government. 

176 | P a g e 
 
International Conferences and Dialogues: ICWA regularly organizes international 
conferences, bilateral dialogues, and expert consultations on critical themes in 
international relations. These platforms enable substantive exchanges of views, 
foster mutual understanding, and strengthen policy-oriented engagement. 
Track II Dialogues and Think Tank Forums:ICWA conducts bilateral Track II 
dialogues with its MoU partners across a wide range of countries, including Russia, 
China, Poland, South Korea, Hungary, New Zealand, Vietnam, Peru, Colombia, 
Mexico, Brazil, Morocco, the UAE, Saudi Arabia, and Iran.ICWA serves as the lead 
Indian think tank for several major institutional mechanisms, including the India–
China Think Tank Forum and the India–Russia Think Tank Forum. It also participates 
in and contributes to multilateral platforms such as the SCO Think Tank Forum and 
the Conference on Interaction and Confidence Building Measures in Asia (CICA) 
Think Tank Forum. Additionally, ICWA serves as the Secretariat for the Council for 
Security Cooperation in the Asia Pacific (CSCAP)-India and actively participates in its 
expert and Study Group meetings. These Track II mechanisms facilitate informal yet 
substantive engagement between experts and practitioners, contributing to trust-
building, policy inputs, and sustained strategic dialogue. 
Track 1.5 Dialogues: ICWA organizes Track 1.5 dialogues with partners in Germany 
and the United Kingdom and is planning similar engagements with Japan and 
Vietnam in a trilateral format. It has also organized the Indian Ocean Dialogue under 
the framework of the Indian Ocean Rim Association (IORA). Track 1.5 dialogues 
bring together government officials and non-official experts, thereby bridging the gap 
between policy and academia while enabling discussions on forward-looking and 
sensitive issues in a constructive setting.ICWA regularly organizes international 
conferences, bilateral dialogues, and expert consultations on critical themes in 
international relations. These platforms enable substantive exchanges of views, 
foster mutual understanding, and strengthen policy-oriented engagement. 
Sapru House Lectures: ICWA hosts Sapru House Lectures (SHLs) by distinguished 
foreign dignitaries visiting India. These lectures provide a platform for Heads of State, 
Foreign Ministers, senior UN officials, and other high-level representatives to share 
their perspectives on issues of mutual interest and engage with an informed Indian 
audience. 

177 | P a g e 
 
In addition, ICWA organizes interactions with visiting foreign ministers, senior 
officials, ambassadors, and experts from international think tanks. These 
engagements complement formal diplomatic channels, enhance bilateral 
understanding, and provide opportunities for in-depth policy discussions.

C. RESEARCH AND INFORMATION SYSTEM FOR DEVELOPING 
COUNTRIES (RIS8 
8.51 The Research and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS) is an 
autonomous policy research institution established in 1983 under the Ministry of 
External Affairs, with a mandate to provide research and analytical support on issues 
relating to international economic development, trade policy, investment, technology, 
and South-South cooperation. An outlay of Rs. 24.87 crore has been earmarked 
under this Head during BE 2026-27.  
8.52  On being asked about details of activities proposed to be taken up by RIS 
under the enhanced allocation during 2026-27, the Ministry informed that RIS shall 
utilize the enhanced allocation during the financial year 2026-27 to substantially 
deepen, widen and scale up its evidence-based policy research, publications and 
high-level policy dialogue initiatives, with particular focus on issues confronting the 
Global South in general and India in particular. While RIS has continued to 
strengthen its established verticals in trade, development cooperation, connectivity, 
multilateral engagement and sustainable development, the forthcoming year marks a 
significant expansion in both thematic scope and institutional responsibilities. The 
enhanced allocation will enable RIS to consolidate existing workstreams while 
supporting a substantial increase in research outputs, international conferences, 
capacity-building programmes and multilateral engagements aligned with India’s 
growing global role.  
8.53  The areas of focused attention during FY 2026-27 would include strengthening 
ASEAN-India partnership under the Act East Policy; advancing India’s priorities 
during its BRICS Presidency 2026; reinforcing India’s leadership in WTO reform 
processes; strengthening Indo-Pacific maritime cooperation; promoting SDGs and 
LiFE; advancing Blue Economy frameworks; and expanding South–South and 
Triangular Cooperation through DAKSHIN. However, these are not merely 

179 | P a g e 
 
continuations of earlier initiatives; they are being significantly scaled up in depth and 
volume through structured publications, international conferences and multi-country 
engagements. 
8.54  In the context of India’s BRICS Presidency 2026, RIS’s research and 
convening responsibilities have expanded. As the designated academic institution for 
convening the BRICS Civil Forum and the BRICS Academic Forum, and as an active 
participant in the BRICS Think Tank Network for Finance, RIS will contribute 
analytical studies, background papers and policy discussions that support informed 
deliberations under the BRICS framework. Multiple publications are proposed under 
this thematic area to examine issues relating to development cooperation, finance, 
technology governance and Global South coordination. Dedicated workshops, 
consultations and outreach activities are also being planned to facilitate structured 
dialogue among scholars, policymakers and international experts. A significant 
intensification is also visible in the Science, Technology and Innovation (STI) vertical. 
RIS proposes to organise roundtables and panel discussions on AI governance, safe 
and trusted AI, synthetic biology governance and India-Africa STI cooperation, along 
with consultations involving Indian Missions and ScienceCounsellors/Attachés. These 
initiatives are aimed at generating evidence-based analytical inputs and fostering 
dialogue on emerging technology governance issues that are increasingly relevant to 
India’s external engagement and development partnerships. 
8.55  The maritime and connectivity agenda is similarly being strengthened through 
the Centre for Maritime Economy and Connectivity (CMEC) and related research 
initiatives. A Maritime Knowledge Lecture Series, along with thematic engagements 
such as a BRICS Maritime Workshop, IORA Maritime Conference and BIMSTEC-
focused discussions are proposed to facilitate research-based dialogue on maritime 
cooperation and regional connectivity. Analytical publications on shipbuilding, vessel 
tokenisation and financing shipbuilding, alongside studies on maritime corridors and 
port partnerships in Africa and Southeast Asia, are proposed to contribute to ongoing 
policy discussions relating to maritime economy and Indo-Pacific connectivity. Under 
the ASEAN–India Centre (AIC), RIS will expand its analytical outputs on ASEAN–
India relations, including the publication of the Third ASEAN–India Development and 
Cooperation Report, policy briefs on the ASEAN Community Vision 2045 and its 
relevance for India, and research on green hydrogen cooperation and tourism 

180 | P a g e 
 
linkages. Planned high-level events such as ASEAN-India Workshop on Green 
Hydrogen, the Fourth International Conference on ASEAN–India Cultural and 
Civilizational Links, the Fifth ASEAN-India Workshop on Blue Economy, the Seventh 
East Asia Summit (EAS) Conference on Maritime Security Cooperation and a 
monthly seminar series to commemorate ASEAN-India Year of Maritime Cooperation 
will further strengthen RIS’s research and convening footprint under the Act East 
framework and contribute to deepening ASEAN-India engagement across economic, 
connectivity and maritime domains. 
8.56  RIS will also continue to scale up its work in Trade, WTO and Sustainability 
domains through the preparation of the World Trade and Development Report 2026 
and related analytical publications emerging from structured brainstorming sessions 
on WTO reform and dispute settlement. Studies on EU-CBAM and its WTO 
compatibility, high-technology trade and industrial policies, intellectual property 
developments in FTAs, trade finance and supply chain, and sovereign credit ratings 
are proposed to deepen RIS’s engagement with evolving global trade and financial 
architecture.  
8.57  A major new development in FY 2026–27 is the formal introduction of Natural 
Resources as a dedicated vertical within RIS’s research programme. In view of global 
supply chain realignments, energy transition imperatives and critical mineral security 
concerns, RIS proposes to undertake focused analytical work on Critical Minerals 
and Energy Security. This addition reflects the growing strategic relevance of 
resource security within broader trade, industrial and geopolitical frameworks. The 
establishment of this vertical represents an expansion of RIS’s research scope 
beyond its traditional trade and development themes into emerging domains that 
require specialized analytical capacity and interdisciplinary expertise. 
8.58  In overall terms, RIS would continue to work across its established verticals of 
Global Governance and Development Cooperation, Trade and WTO-related issues, 
Connectivity and Maritime Economy, Science and Technology, Sustainability and 
Finance, while introducing Natural Resources as a new and dedicated research 
vertical. FY 2026-27 represents a significant scaling up in thematic coverage, 
publication outputs and international engagement, necessitating enhanced 
institutional capacity and analytical depth. The enhanced allocation is therefore 

181 | P a g e 
 
essential to sustain and expand RIS’s role as an autonomous research institution 
providing high-quality, evidence-based analytical support aligned with national 
priorities and global development objectives.

Conferences/Dialogues and Special Plenary sessions conducted during these years.
