8.60 About the role of RIS in providing intellectual support to the Policy Planning 
and Research Division, the Ministry stated that the concept of RIS was mooted at the 
NAM Ministerial Conference way back in 1975 and the decision to establish it was 
taken during its Colombo Summit in 1976. The concept floated by the Ministry was for 
RIS to work as a useful mechanism with independent back up facilities to assist the 
developing countries in their dialogue with the developed countries on trade issues to 
defend their interests. RIS was established on 30 May 1983. The founding Chairman 
was Ambassador G. Parthasarathi. 
8.61 RIS is organically connected with the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA).  The 
main thrust areas of work of RIS are Global Economic Issues and South-South 
Cooperation, Trade, Investment and Economic Cooperation, Connectivity and 
Regional Cooperation, and New Technologies and Development Issues. Over the 
years, RIS has developed the expertise to promote multi-stakeholder consultations 
on vital issues concerning developing countries. In this context, RIS has a 
comprehensive programme of research collaboration with international organizations 
and academic bodies of other countries. The outcome of such collaboration is usually 
in the form of academic research, which is published, in various index academic 
journals including the quarterly RIS Diary which is regularly shared with the PP&R 
Division and gets reflected in its monthly Compendium for wider internal circulation 
among the policy makers. The studies of RIS on trade and economic issues, 
including food and energy security, strongly back India’s diplomatic strategic 

182 | P a g e 
 
imperative that catalyzes a globalised India focused on linking the local to the global 
and on becoming a nerve center of global supply chains and a manufacturing hub.   
8.62  These important research outcomes are utilized by the Division in its bilateral 
and multilateral policy planning dialogues with other foreign offices and international 
agencies, preparation of notes such as research papers, policy briefs and situation 
reports pertaining to global developments and international affairs in general and 
India’s foreign policy in particular. These also contribute to the preparation of the 
Annual Report of the MEA.  
8.63  The primary spatial orientation of India’s foreign policy remains its immediate 
neighbourhood, under the ‘Neighbourhood First Policy’ combined with our 
revitalised ‘Act East Policy’ and ‘Think West Policy’ that seeks to enhance our 
engagement in our extended neighbourhood.  In its research dialogues and studies 
RIS reinforces this focus among think tanks and policy shapers of the region and 
effectively puts across India’s standpoint on these issues as an autonomous 
institution. Delegations from IORA and BIMSTEC have regularly visited RIS for 
brainstorming. RIS organized a mega conference in the form of Bay of Bengal 
Economic Dialogue (BoBED).  
8.64  RIS continues to share with the Division the intellectual capital generated 
through its various  in-house platforms  such as ASEAN-India Centre (AIC),  
DAKSHIN- Global South Centre of Excellence (GSCE),  Forum for Indian Science 
Diplomacy (FISD), Blue Economy Forum (BEF), Forum on Indian Traditional 
Medicine (FITM), Science Technology and Innovation Policy (STIP) Forum, Centre 
for Maritime Economy and Connectivity (CMEC), Forum for Indian Development 
Cooperation (FIDC), South Asia Centre for Policy Studies (SACEPS),  BRICS 
Academic Forum (BAF), BIMSTEC Network of Policy Think Tanks (BNPTT), ASEAN-
India Network of Think Tanks (AINTT), IBSA Academic Forum (IAF), Network of 
Southern Think Tanks (NeST), etc.  
8.65  In connection with India’s Presidency of BRICS, RIS is constantly in touch with 
the PP&R Division and has established a dedicated team of research scholars to 
provide research support to the MEA under the guidance of PP&R Division on areas 
of key importance to India  for the advancement of its foreign policy objectives 
including shaping of reformed or rebalanced  multilateralism and globalization, 

183 | P a g e 
 
enhance convergence among BRICS members for collective efforts in key areas to 
propel post pandemic collective recovery, development of new strategies and 
approaches to assist developing countries,  climate change, financial inclusion, 
effective  management of global institutions and engagement groups guided by our 
civilizational ethos and our heritage that places humanity at the centre of global 
prosperity and cooperation and of a fair, inclusive and  responsible international world 
order. 
8.67 As regards collaboration of RIS with research institutions, bodies and 
organizations working, the Ministry stated that RIS currently collaborates with 
approximately 351 institutions worldwide, comprising 79 India-based institutions and 
272 international institutions, reflecting a wide and diversified institutional network 
spanning academic, governmental, multilateral and policy research ecosystems. 
The 272 international collaborations are geographically distributed across all major 
regions: Asia (approximately 108 institutions) remains the largest footprint, followed 
by Africa (64 institutions) and Latin America and the Caribbean (43 institutions). 
Engagement in Europe accounts for about 25 institutions, while North America 
comprises around 14 institutions, Oceania about 8 institutions, and Russia/Eurasia 
10 institutions. This distribution demonstrates a strong Global South orientation 
while maintaining structured engagement with OECD countries and Indo-Pacific 
partners.  
8.68 On the Committee’s further query about contribution of to the intellectual 
discourse on global development and economic governance, the Ministry stated that 
RIS has been conducting a range of research studies in the field of development 
and economic governance to strengthen India’s economic diplomacy narratives, 
thereby identifying priorities in terms of leveraging the external sector for economic 
growth and consolidating economic cooperation mechanisms adequately informed 
of the means to safeguard national interest at all points. In this context, the evolving 
scenarios are being studied carefully to understand the impact of rising 
fragmentation in the global order and use of protectionist means by certain 
countries. In addition, the emerging developments and disruptions in trade and 
technology have been a key concern and efforts are being made to conceptually 
and empirically capture the implications for India and the global south. As 
mandated, RIS has been connecting deeply with issues of common concern in the 

184 | P a g e 
 
global south and in the extended region through its specific centres and initiatives 
viz. DAKSHIN, ASEAN-India Centre (AIC), Centre for Maritime Economy and 
Connectivity (CMEC) and the Forum for Indian Traditional Medicine (FITM).  
8.69 As part of its ongoing work programme on development cooperation, RIS has 
conducted a number of important interactions with subject experts and stakeholders. 
RIS is organizing a series of brainstorming sessions with experts and negotiators for 
the upcoming 14th WTO Ministerial Conference which would lead to the RIS flagship 
report – World Trade and Development Report (WTDR). On international trade issues 
RIS has organized a number of seminars on themes viz. Global Uncertainties and the 
Role of FTAs: An Indo-Pacific Perspective; Reshaping Value Chains: The Case for 
Deeper Asia-Pacific Trade Integration; Decoding Quality Control Orders (QCOs); 
evolving intersections between sustainability, protectionism, and global agricultural 
trade; growing protectionism and international trade: fork in the road; Globalization of 
Traditional Medicines: Harnessing AI and Digital Technology for Quality and 
Standards; India and the Framework Agreement on Facilitation of Cross-Border 
Paperless Trade in Asia and the Pacific (CPTA); Global Trade and Chemical Sector 
among others. As RIS is preparing to substantially contribute to India’s Chairship of 
BRICS in 2026, RIS organized the handover ceremony of BRICS Think Tank 
Network for Finance (BTTNF). 
8.70 RIS has also brought out key reports, studies, discussion papers and policy 
briefs on Trump’s Trade Policies; India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
(IMEC); Global Development Compact and South-South and Triangular Cooperation; 
Emerging Contours of Triangular Cooperation and Global South; A Decade of Act 
East Policy; Digital Public Infrastructure; Framework Agreement on Facilitation of 
Cross-Border Paperless Trade in Asia and the Pacific (CPTA);  Quality Control 
Orders; Agricultural Trade: Perspectives from the Global South; Paperless Trade 
Facilitation; insolvency laws and international trade; India-Germany Triangular 
Cooperation; productivity of major ports; international taxation and AI ethics. 
Commentaries on Act East Policy; ASEAN-India Regional Value Chains; ASEAN-
India partnership etc. have been published.  
8.71 RIS has developed core competence and established practice of translating all 
research findings and stakeholder consultations into useable policy inputs primarily to 

185 | P a g e 
 
strengthen India’s diplomatic strategy, nurture South-South partnerships, consolidate 
development cooperation initiatives, facilitate economic diplomacy and negotiations, 
and support policy makers on new issues, perspectives, empirical evidence and 
framework of engagement. This is particularly relevant in such times when the world 
is witnessing unprecedented flux and disruptions, even as India scales new heights of 
economic progress further strengthening its faith in meaningful engagement with all 
partners in the spirit of ‘Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam’ towards human centric 
development globally. RIS has been providing inputs based on its expertise in 
international trade, investment, connectivity, finance and technology from the 
perspective multilateral, regional and bilateral engagements.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

186 | P a g e 
 
PART II 
OBSERVATIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS BY THE COMMITTEE 
Overall Budgetary Allocation for 2026-27 
1. The Committee note that the Ministry of External Affairs has been 
allocated Rs. 22,118.97 crore in Budget Estimates (BE) 2026-27. This 
represents an increase of 7.81% over BE 2025-26 of Rs. 20,516.61 crore and 
1.73% over Revised Estimates (RE) 2025-26 of Rs. 21,742.74 crore. As a 
proportion of the total Union Budget 2026-27, the MEA's allocation stands at 
0.41%, which is the same as in 2025-26. The Committee observe that while the 
absolute allocation has increased in nominal terms, the proportional share has 
remained relatively low, even as India's global ambitions, diplomatic footprint, 
and development partnership commitments have expanded substantially. 
The Committee are concerned to note that the budgetary allocation for 
the Ministry of External Affairs as a percentage of the total Union Budget has 
declined consistently from 0.69% in 2017-18 to 0.41% in 2026-27. In absolute 
terms, India allocates approximately 0.06% of its GDP to foreign affairs, which 
compares unfavourably with countries such as the United States 
(approximately 0.15-0.20% of GDP) and United Kingdom (approximately 0.25-
0.30% of GDP). The Committee further note that MEA is ranked 23rd among 
Union Ministries in terms of budgetary allocation at Rs. 22,118.97 crore in BE 
2026-27, despite being entrusted with managing India's entire external 
engagement at a time when India occupies an increasingly prominent position 
in global affairs. 
In its Fifth Report on DFG 2025-26, the Committee had specifically 
recommended a budgetary enhancement of at least 20% in allocation to MEA. 
The Ministry has conveyed that this recommendation was formally 
communicated to the Ministry of Finance by the Foreign Secretary ahead of the 
pre-budget consultations. Despite this, the eventual increase has been limited 
to 7.81% - well short of what the Committee had recommended. The Committee 
are constrained to observe that this level of increase is inadequate to keep 
pace with India's expanding international engagements and the rising costs of 

187 | P a g e 
 
diplomacy. The Committee therefore, strongly recommend that due cognizance 
should to be given to the strategic imperatives driving MEA's expenditure and 
accord the Ministry a budgetary allocation commensurate with India's 
aspirations as a leading global power. The Committee reiterate their earlier 
recommendation for a budgetary enhancement of at least 20% and urge that 
the MEA's allocation be progressively raised to a level closer to the global 
benchmark for comparable major economies. The concern of the Committee 
may be conveyed to the Ministry of the Finance for further necessary action. 
 
Utilizing the RIS Report for a Stronger Case before the Ministry of Finance 
2. The Committee note that MEA currently ranks 23rd among all Ministries 
and Departments in terms of budgetary allocation, at 0.41% of the Union 
Budget. Ministries such as Finance (35.93%), Defence (14.29%), Road 
Transport and Highways (5.64%), and Railways (5.12%) understandably 
command larger shares given their domestic infrastructure mandates. 
However, the Committee are of the view that MEA's allocation must be 
compared not merely in terms of rank but in terms of the strategic multiplier it 
provides: effective diplomatic engagement secures trade, investment, 
Diaspora welfare, neighbourhood stability, and India's standing in multilateral 
institutions - dividends that accrue across sectors and are not fully captured in 
any single Ministry's budget. 
The Committee had persistently recommended that the Ministry should 
commission an independent empirical study to assess MEA's budgetary trends 
in a comparative framework. Pursuant to this recommendation, the Research 
and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS) has prepared a Report 
titled 'Analysis of Trends of MEA Budgetary Allocation', examining MEA's 
budget both through an inter-Ministerial comparison within the Government of 
India and a cross-country comparison with major economies.  
The Committee recommend that MEA should utilize the RIS Report and 
update it annually with the latest figures on both inter-Ministerial allocation 
trends and cross-country comparisons. The updated Report should be 

188 | P a g e 
 
formally annexed to MEA's budget demand submission to the Ministry of 
Finance and should be shared with the Committee alongside the Demands for 
Grants. The Committee are of the view that a data-driven, rigorously 
benchmarked case before the Ministry of Finance will be far more persuasive 
than incremental administrative representations, and the RIS Report provides 
exactly the kind of credible evidence base that is required. 
 
Non-inclusion of Line of Credit Provisioning 
3. The Committee had consistently held that Line of Credit provisioning, 
which represents a notional expenditure and an adjustment of accounts 
between different branches of Government, charged to the Guaranteed 
Redemption Fund, gives a disproportionate and misleading picture of MEA's 
actual expenditure. The Committee are satisfied to note that BE 2026-27 
allocations do not include any provisioning for Lines of Credit (LoC) through 
the EXIM Bank. The Foreign Secretary, during evidence confirmed that the 
Ministry remains engaged with the Reserve Bank of India and the Ministry of 
Finance on this structural issue.  
The Committee reiterate that LoC provisioning must not be included in 
MEA's regular budget in future years. The Committee suggest that this position 
be formalized through a permanent institutional arrangement, whereby such 
provisioning is charged directly to an appropriate dedicated instrument 
managed by the Ministry of Finance and the Reserve Bank of India, entirely 
outside the MEA's Demand for Grants. The Ministry should formally propose 
this structural arrangement to the concerned authorities and report back to the 
Committee on the outcome in the next year. 
 
Gap between Budgetary demand and allocation 
4. The Committee observe that there has been a persistent gap between 
the MEA’s budgetary demands and the actual allocations. In 2025-26, the 
Ministry demanded Rs. 30,871.82 crore but received only Rs. 20,516.61 crore, a 

189 | P a g e 
 
shortfall of over Rs. 10,355 crore, representing a cut of nearly 33.5% of the 
demand. In 2026-27, the Ministry demanded Rs. 25,997.56 crore and received 
Rs. 22,118.97 crore, a shortfall of Rs. 3,878.59 crore. While the Committee 
acknowledge that the gap has narrowed this year, the concern remains that 
structural underfunding needs to be addressed for continued effective 
diplomacy.  The Committee acknowledge the Ministry’s effort to rationalize its 
demands to make them more realistic and need-based. However, it is equally 
clear that even rationalized demands have been substantially cut.  
The Committee wish to make it unambiguously clear that the recurring 
demand-allocation gap is not merely an administrative inconvenience but 
represents, cumulatively, a significant underinvestment in India's diplomatic 
capacity. As India aspires to play a leading role in global governance, 
multilateral institutions, regional connectivity, and the Global South, the 
financial resources made available to the Ministry of External Affairs must 
reflect that ambition. The Committee recommend that the Ministry should 
accompany each Budget demand with a detailed, head-wise justification note, 
supported by empirical data and outcome metrics, to enable the Ministry of 
Finance to make a fully informed appraisal. The RIS Report and cross-country 
benchmarks should be integral parts of this justification. The Committee 
further recommend that the Ministry should institutionalize a structured pre-
budget dialogue with the Ministry of Finance before the preparation of the 
Union Budget. The Committee also urge the Ministry of Finance to undertake a 
more rigorous and sympathetic appraisal of MEA's budgetary demands, which 
are driven by national interests, externally determined factors such as foreign 
exchange fluctuations, opening of new Missions and Posts, and India's 
expanding development assistance portfolio etc.  
 
Gender Budgeting 
5. The Committee note that in response to its persistent recommendation 
on incorporating gender budgeting in MEA’s budgetary processes, the Ministry 
has taken several positive steps. A Gender Budget Cell has been constituted; 
gender-disaggregated data has been obtained for ITEC trainees; India has 

190 | P a g e 
 
trained nearly 30,000 women from the Global South under ITEC since 2015, 
with women accounting for nearly 39% of all ITEC trainees annually; and 
approximately 60 gender-specific or women-only courses have been offered 
since 2015. Officers have also been provided training on gender budgeting at 
IIM Ahmedabad. The Committee further note that the Ministry had 
commissioned a study on gender budgeting practices, covering best practices 
across 17 countries and international institutions. However, the Ministry has 
stated that it is ‘currently examining the Report’ without specifying the name of 
the think tank as well as any timeline for implementing its recommendations. 
The Committee observe that while the Ministry has made commendable 
progress on the softer dimensions of gender mainstreaming such as training, 
data collection, health camps, and sports events for women employees; the 
work on substantive and structural dimension of gender budgeting, namely the 
earmarking of allocations, systematic gender impact assessment of projects, 
and reporting of gender-responsive expenditure outcomes, is in progress. The 
Committee therefore, recommend that the Ministry should complete its 
examination of the think tank report on gender budgeting practices in 17 
countries and international institutions in a time bound manner, and place 
before the Committee a summary of the key findings, including the specific 
recommendations that the Ministry proposes to adopt. 
 
Classification of MEA Budget 
6. The Committee note that MEA’s budget is classified along two axes: 
Revenue and Capital on one hand, and Scheme and Non-Scheme on the other. 
The Revenue section for BE 2026-27 stands at Rs. 20,706.20 crore, while the 
Capital section stands at Rs. 1,412.77 crore. It is observed that the Capital 
section, which funds acquisition of Chancery buildings, construction of 
residential complexes, and Regional Passport Office infrastructure, has seen a 
sharp decline from Rs. 1,675.18 crore in BE 2024-25 to Rs. 996.88 crore in BE 
2025-26, recovering only partially to Rs. 1,412.77 crore in BE 2026-27. The 
Committee are concerned that such steep year-on-year volatility in Capital 
allocation impairs long-term infrastructure planning for India’s diplomatic 

191 | P a g e 
 
network. The Committee are of the view that the Capital budget of MEA has 
strategic, long-gestation implications that cannot be managed efficiently under 
annual cycles prone to sharp revision. The Ministry should, therefore, develop 
a Capital investment plan for a more predictable and purposeful capital 
provisioning. 
 
Insulating the Scheme Section from Budgetary Cuts 
7. Under the Scheme/Non-Scheme classification, ‘Scheme’ expenditure, 
comprising India’s development assistance and technical and economic 
cooperation programmes, stands at Rs. 6,997.56 crore in BE 2026-27, while 
‘Non-Scheme’ expenditure, covering establishment and operational costs, 
stands at Rs. 15,121.41 crore. The Committee note with concern that the 
Scheme section of MEA’s budget has been subject to reduction in BE 2026-27 
compared to RE 2025-26. The Ministry has attributed this reduction primarily to 
cuts in aid to Bangladesh and Myanmar owing to the internal situation in those 
countries. The Committee are informed that the Scheme/Non-Scheme 
classification is not MEA-specific but has been determined by the Ministry of 
Finance, with aid and development partnership heads treated as ‘Scheme’ 
expenditure given their socio-economic outcomes in partner countries. 
The Committee emphasize that development assistance is not merely a 
budgetary outlay but is the most visible and consequential instrument of 
India’s bilateral diplomacy, particularly in the neighbourhood, the Global 
South, Africa, and the Indo-Pacific. Reductions in scheme allocations, even if 
driven by legitimate bilateral considerations, risk signaling a withdrawal of 
India’s developmental engagement to partner countries and can erode 
goodwill built over years of sustained investment. The Committee are also 
concerned to note that there is no budgetary mechanism to protect the 
Scheme section from spillover pressures arising from the expanding Non-
Scheme section. 
The Committee therefore, strongly recommend that the Scheme section 
of MEA's budget should be treated as a protected and priority category and 

192 | P a g e 
 
that any reduction in Scheme allocation be justified. Reductions attributable to 
political situation in a particular country, such as Bangladesh or Myanmar, the 
funds thus released should not be surrendered/transferred to the Non-Scheme 
section, but should be retained within the Scheme section and utilized for 
development assistance to other partner countries or for new development 
initiatives. 
 
Budget Utilization pattern 
8.  The Committee take note of MEA’s consistent efforts over the years to 
optimize the utilization of its allocated budgetary resources. The Ministry 
achieved near-complete utilization of its revised allocation in 2022-23 (98.3%), 
2023-24 (99.6%), and 2024-25 (99.84%), with unspent balances of only Rs. 
281.17 crore, Rs. 66.68 crore, and Rs. 79.50 crore, respectively. However, the 
Committee observe that the utilization rate, as of December 2025, stands at 
67.83% of RE 2025-26, which, while broadly on track with Quarterly Cash 
Management Guidelines, points to a significant increase in expenditure 
towards the close of the financial year. The Foreign Secretary has expressed 
confidence that full utilization will be achieved by end of March 2026. The 
Committee further note that IMAS 2.0 has been integrated with PFMS since 
July 2025, with 214 out of 221 Missions now on the centralized platform, 
enabling real-time expenditure monitoring. The Committee view this as a 
critical enabler that must be leveraged more proactively to prevent last-minute 
expenditure rush. 
 The Committee commend the Ministry for the marked improvement in 
overall budget utilization over 2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25, and take note of 
the Foreign Secretary’s assurance of full utilization by end of FY 2025-26. The 
Committee encourage the Ministry to sustain this trajectory and to leverage the 
IMAS 2.0 PFMS integration to move towards a regime of not merely full 
utilization but high-quality, evenly-paced expenditure. 
 
 

193 | P a g e 
 
Budgetary Allocation for Diaspora Welfare and Engagement 
9. The Committee have examined with considerable care the budgetary 
allocations and institutional framework governing India’s engagement with its 
diaspora. India’s diaspora of over 32 million persons, spread across more than 
180 countries, remits over US$ 100 billion annually to the country and 
constitutes India’s most effective instrument of cultural diplomacy and soft 
power. The Committee note that the budgetary allocations for diaspora welfare 
and engagement are spread across multiple sub-heads such ‘Welfare of 
Overseas Indians’, ‘Engagement with Indian Diaspora’, ‘Scheme for Welfare of 
Indian Women Abroad’, and ‘Protector of Emigrants’. Individual scheme 
allocations tell their own story. The allocation for repatriation of Indian 
destitutes has remained frozen at Rs. one lakh rupees for six consecutive 
years. Legal assistance to women facing problems in NRI marriages has been 
frozen at 5 lakh for four consecutive years. The Awareness Campaign and 
Media Plan has been slashed by over 90%, from Rs. 11 crore in 2020-21 to Rs. 
1 crore in 2025-26, precisely when recruitment fraud, illegal migration, and 
trafficking are on the rise. The Pre-Departure Orientation Training programme, 
designed to protect the most vulnerable emigrant workers before they depart, 
recorded nil utilization in 2022-23 and expenditure of barely Rs. 0.29 crore in 
2024-25 against a grant of Rs. 1 crore. From the above observation, the 
Committee note that the budgetary allocations for their welfare and 
engagement are not reflective of their strategic significance in our foreign 
policy objectives.  
The Committee strongly recommend that the Ministry should undertake 
a fundamental review of the budgetary framework for diaspora welfare and 
engagement with the explicit objective of aligning allocations with the strategic 
significance of our diaspora. The Committee also recommend that the 
Government should examine, as a matter of urgency, the case for establishing 
a dedicated Department of Diaspora Overseas India Affairs within MEA, headed 
by a Secretary-level officer with an exclusive and undivided mandate for 
diaspora engagement and welfare. 
 

194 | P a g e 
 
Pre-Departure Orientation Training (PDOT) programme 
10. The Committee note that the Pre-Departure Orientation Training (PDOT) 
programme, aimed at enhancing the soft skills, destination-country awareness, 
and legal literacy of Indian emigrant workers, particularly those heading to 
ECR countries, has consistently recorded low utilization. Actual expenditure 
was nil in 2022-23, Rs. 0.79 crore against a grant of Rs. 1.20 crore in 2023-24, 
and only Rs. 0.29 crore against a grant of Rs. 1.00 crore in 2024-25. The 
allocation for 2026-27 stands at Rs. 2.5 crore. The Committee find this pattern 
of persistent underutilization a matter of concern as the PDOT programme 
directly addresses the vulnerability of low-skilled emigrant workers who are 
most at risk of exploitation, trafficking, and distress abroad. The Committee 
recommend that the Ministry should undertake an immediate diagnostic review 
of the PDOT programme to identify the specific reasons for persistent 
underutilization, including delays in fund disbursement to State government 
agencies, inadequate reach to prospective migrants, and gaps in programme 
delivery. The Committee also desire that the Ministry should strengthen 
coordination with the State Governments to ensure wider outreach.  
 
Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF) 
11. The Committee note that the Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF), 
established in 2009 and revised in 2017, has provided assistance to 3,62,882 
Indians in distress in foreign countries from 2012 to September 2025. The 
ICWF is self-sustained through a standard charge of three US dollars levied on 
each consular, passport, and visa service transaction at Indian Missions and 
Posts abroad, and is not reflected in MEA’s Union Budget allocation. The 
Committee note that the Foreign Secretary has confirmed that distressed 
nationals are invariably repatriated using ICWF funds, and that the allocation 
of Rs. 0.05 crore (five lakh rupees) for ‘Legal Assistance to Women facing 
problems in NRI Marriages’ is supplemented in practice by ICWF. 
While the Committee acknowledge the operational effectiveness of the 
ICWF model and the Foreign Secretary’s assurance that no deserving case 
goes unassisted, they note that the complete absence of ICWF data from the 

195 | P a g e 
 
Union Budget makes meaningful parliamentary oversight of this fund 
effectively impossible. The Committee recommend that the Ministry should 
urgently place before the Committee, a comprehensive statement on the ICWF 
covering total corpus of the Fund, Mission-wise collection, aggregate 
utilization, number of beneficiaries assisted, and assessment of adequacy of 
the three-dollar levy in light of evolving consular service volumes and welfare 
demands. 
 
MADAD Portal 
12. The Committee note that the MADAD portal, since its launch in February 
2015, has received over 1 lakh grievances, of which approximately 97% have 
been resolved as of February 2026. The Committee welcome the launch of 
MADAD Version 2.0 in December 2025, with an updated interface and 
enhanced real-time notifications, as well as the Ministry’s proposal to integrate 
the portal with BHASHINI for multilingual access. The Committee recommend 
that the Ministry should expedite the integration of the MADAD portal with the 
BHASHINI language module to make it available in all major Indian languages. 
 
Videsh Sampark Programme 
13. The Committee note that the Videsh Sampark Programme, launched in 
2017, has held twelve outreach events across nine States over eight years, 
with the most recent edition in Andhra Pradesh in October 2025.  The 
Committee welcome the Programme’s outcomes in terms of spreading 
awareness of safe and legal migration, strengthening State-Centre 
coordination on emigration matters, and enhancing delivery of welfare 
services. The Ministry has stated that the Programme aims for approximately 
2-3 editions per year. However, the Committee note that several high-
emigration States such as Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan, Odisha, West Bengal, and 
Tamil Nadu have not yet been covered under the Programme. The Committee 
recommend that the Ministry should prepare a time-bound coverage plan for 
the Videsh Sampark Programme that ensures all major source States of 

196 | P a g e 
 
emigration, particularly those not yet covered, are reached within the next two 
years. Also, all efforts should be made to ensure that all states are covered 
annually under this Programme. 
 
Protector of Emigrants 
14. The Committee note that the Protector General of Emigrants (PGoE) and 
the network of 16 Protector of Emigrants offices (Mumbai, Chennai, Delhi, 
Kolkata, Chandigarh, Hyderabad, Kochi, Thiruvananthapuram, Jaipur, 
Lucknow, Patna, Bengaluru, Guwahati, Ranchi, Bhubaneswar and Agartala) are 
responsible for enforcing the Emigration Act, 1983, protecting Indian workers 
emigrating to 19 notified ECR countries, regulating recruiting agents, and 
operating the eMigrate portal. The Committee acknowledges that the PoE 
network plays a critical role in protecting millions of Indian workers, 
particularly low-skilled and semi-skilled workers in Gulf countries and 
Southeast Asia from exploitation, trafficking, and illegal recruitment. However, 
the Committee are concerned that the total budget for the PGE is entirely 
disproportionate to the scale and complexity of the responsibilities involved. 
The Committee are particularly concerned that the budget for public 
awareness campaigns to inform emigrants of their rights stands at a mere Rs. 
73 lakh in 2025-26. The Committee also note that with only 16 PoE offices in a 
country of 28 states and 8 Union Territories, there is a need to establish PoE 
offices in other parts of our country, including major emigration source states, 
that have no local PoE presence. 
The Committee recommend that the Ministry should undertake an urgent 
and comprehensive review of the budgetary requirements of the PGoE and the 
PoE network, and make enhanced allocations to levels commensurate with the 
scale and sensitivity of the responsibilities involved. The Committee further 
recommend that the Ministry should prepare a phased PoE Network Expansion 
Plan, identifying States with the highest ECR emigrant populations but no PoE 
presence, estimating the cost of establishing new offices in these States, and 
setting a time-bound schedule for expansion, beginning with the completion of 
pending offices in Vijayawada (Andhra Pradesh) and the approved one in 
Madhya Pradesh. It is also essential that these offices are located in accessible 

197 | P a g e 
 
places as the one in Chandigarh is located in an industrial area. The 
Committee would like to be kept informed about the progress made in this 
regard. 
 
Combating Illegal Recruitment 
15. The Committee note that despite the eMigrate portal, the mPassport 
Police App, and the network of PoE offices, illegal recruitment agencies and 
unauthorized intermediaries continue to operate, exploiting vulnerable Indian 
workers seeking employment abroad. The Committee observe that the Ministry 
has acknowledged the challenge of combating fraud in the recruitment 
process and the need for coordination with local police and cyber-crime units, 
but has not provided the Committee with specific data on the scale of illegal 
recruitment activity, the number of agents prosecuted, or the outcomes of 
enforcement actions. The Committee are of the firm view that the protection of 
Indian workers from exploitation, trafficking, and fraudulent recruitment is a 
fundamental obligation of the State, and that the current enforcement 
architecture - characterized by a small PoE network, limited financial 
resources, and dependence on State police coordination - appear insufficient 
to meet this obligation at the required scale. 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that MEA should develop and 
implement, in coordination with concerned Ministries (such as Home Affairs 
and Labour and Employment) and state governments, a comprehensive 
strategy to counter illegal recruitment, encompassing a strengthened 
regulatory framework for recruiting agents, enhanced digital monitoring of 
online recruitment advertising, coordination with social media platforms to 
detect and remove fraudulent job advertisements, and a dedicated rapid 
response mechanism for distress cases involving illegal recruitment. The 
Committee further recommend that the Ministry institute a publicly accessible, 
real-time blacklisted illegal and deregistered recruiting agents, accessible to 
potential emigrants and their families through the eMigrate portal, MADAD, and 
other citizen-centric platforms and also work with State governments to ensure 

198 | P a g e 
 
that local police are aware of and act against the blacklisted agents operating 
in their jurisdictions. 
New Emigration Bill 
16. The emigration framework in India is governed by the Emigration Act, 
1983, administered by MEA through the Protector General of Emigrants. In the 
four decades since its enactment, India’s outward labour migration has grown 
exponentially in scale, complexity, and vulnerability. The Committee have in 
previous reports persistently called for the introduction of a comprehensive 
new Emigration Bill. The Ministry has in successive responses acknowledged 
the preparation of the draft Bill and stated that it is under ‘active 
consideration.’ In pursuance of the Committee’s recommendation contained in 
Sixth Report, the Ministry provided the draft Overseas Mobility (Facilitation and 
Welfare) Bill, 2025 to the Committee and sought feedback from Committee 
Members. The feedback was sent to the Ministry in December 2025. The 
Committee note that the introduction of the new Bill has remained pending 
despite the completion of the Committee’s feedback, and reiterate that the 
matter deserves to be treated as a legislative priority. The Committee strongly 
recommend that the Government treat the introduction of the Overseas 
Mobility (Facilitation and Welfare) Bill, 2025 as a legislative priority and 
introduce it in Parliament at the earliest opportunity. The Committee are 
hopeful that once introduced, the Bill will be referred to it for detailed 
examination, in view of its significance and wider implications. 
 
Budgetary Allocation to Technical and Economic Cooperation 
17. The Committee note that the budget head “Technical and Economic 
Cooperation with other countries” caters to India’s aid and assistance 
programmes not only in India immediate and extended neighbourhood, but 
also countries in South East Asia, Central Asia, Africa, Latin America and the 
Caribbean, and the Indo-Pacific Region.  It also caters to Aid for Disaster Relief 
and contributions to various regional funds.  The Committee note that during 
2025-26, an amount of Rs.6375 crore had been allocated at BE under this head, 

199 | P a g e 
 
which was subsequently revised upwards to Rs.6772.40 crore at RE.  The 
actual utilization up to January 2026 stood at Rs.4047.17 crore, i.e. 59.75 
percent of the RE allocation.   
The Committee are concerned to note underutilization of funds under 
several sub heads, such as Aid to Bangladesh, Aid to Myanmar, other 
Developing countries, SAARC Programme, African countries, Multilateral 
Economic Relations Programme, Investment Promotion & Publicity, Eurasian 
countries, Afghanistan, Indo Pacific Cooperation, Seychelles, Quad 
Cooperation, etc.  Considering that the primary objectives of India’s financial 
aid and assistance programmes are the furtherance of bilateral relations and 
the strengthening of bonds of friendship, respect and mutual trust between 
India and partner countries, the Committee urge the Ministry to take all 
necessary measures to ensure full and purposeful utilization of funds allocated 
at RE 2025-26 under this head before the close of the financial year.  The 
Committee also desire that the Ministry conduct a thorough review of 
persistently underperforming sub-heads and put in place targeted remedial 
measures so that budgetary provisions under each head are utilized in a time-
bound and result-oriented manner. 
 
Publicity of Aid and Assistance Projects 
18. The Committee note that an amount of Rs. 6,477.64 crore, constituting 
29.28 percent of the total budget of the Ministry of External Affairs, has been 
allocated at BE 2026-27 under the head ‘Technical and Economic Cooperation 
with other countries.’  The Committee are pleased to learn that India’s 
development partnership model has earned a high degree of acceptance 
among partner countries, owing to its demand-driven and beneficiary-centric 
character that keeps the requirements and priorities of the partner country 
.The Committee desire that the Ministry take all necessary measures to ensure 
optimum utilization of the funds allocated during 2026-27, which will serve the 
twin objectives of establishing India’s image as a trusted and reliable 
development partner and of creating economic and cultural opportunities for 
India to deepen its engagement with host countries.  The Committee also 

200 | P a g e 
 
recommend that the Ministry should adopt a proactive and structured 
approach to publicizing India’s international development projects and their 
tangible impact, both in the host countries and within India, through all 
available media platforms, high-level diplomatic engagements, and dedicated 
outreach initiatives 
 
Challenges faced in project implementation abroad 
19. The Committee have been apprised by the Ministry of the various 
challenges that impact the timely utilization of funds and have led to decreased 
allocations under certain sub-heads.  These include difficulties in applying 
Indian procurement rules in foreign commercial and legal ecosystems, non-
availability of encumbrance-free sites, delays in land acquisition, Right of Way 
issues, contractual disputes, and delays in environmental impact assessments 
that are necessarily conducted by the host country.  The Committee also take 
note of the significant adverse impact of changing political, economic and 
security situations in partner countries, most notably in Afghanistan, 
Bangladesh, and Myanmar on India’s ability to execute and complete projects 
within stipulated timeframes and budgets. Notwithstanding these challenges, 
the Committee are pleased to note that the utilization of funds under the head 
‘Technical and Economic Cooperation with other countries’ with reference to 
RE was 102.98 percent in 2023-24 and 103.59 percent in 2024-25, reflecting 
commendable performance in those years. 
While fully appreciating the measures taken by the Ministry, including 
the establishment of an in-house Project Management Unit to strengthen 
project appraisal and monitoring mechanisms, the Committee are of the view 
that these institutional efforts must be complemented by closer, more 
structured engagement with host governments at all stages of the project 
cycle.  The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry should work 
proactively with host governments to identify potential obstacles at the project 
inception stage itself, establish joint review mechanisms, and ensure that 
challenges are addressed in a timely manner so as to facilitate smooth, 
efficient, and result-oriented implementation of India’s development assistance 
projects abroad. 

201 | P a g e 
 
Aid to Bangladesh 
20.  Under the sub-head ‘Aid to Bangladesh, an amount of Rs.120 crore was 
allocated at BE during 2025-26, which was reduced to Rs.34.48 crore at RE.  
The actual utilization till January 2026 was only Rs.20.44 crore. For the year 
2026-27, an amount of Rs.60 crore only has been allocated under the head at 
BE. The Ministry has informed the Committee that due to political and security 
disturbances in Bangladesh post July-August 2024, the execution of ongoing 
grant projects has been delayed and no new project commitment were made in 
financial year 2025-26.  The delays, lack of new commitments, and completion 
of the Akhaura-Agartala Rail line project in financial year 2025-26 has 
necessitated a reduced a reduced allocation of Rs.60 crore at BE 2026-27.  The 
rail line has been formally handed over to Bangladesh Railways on 20th July 
2025.  The Committee are glad to note that following recent election, new 
Government has come to power and the Ministry of External Affairs remains 
committed not only complete the ongoing development projects, but also to    
discuss the possibility of new projects with the new Government. The 
Committee also note that Bangladesh occupies a pivotal position in India’s 
foreign policy architecture and appreciate that high-level engagements have 
begun with the newly elected government. Although recent political 
developments in Bangladesh have introduced new complexities into the 
bilateral relationship, the Committee desire that the Ministry of External Affairs 
continue to engage with the new Government of Bangladesh so as to identify 
and implement projects which will promote stability, prosperity and mutually 
benefit the two countries.  The Committee further desire that any additional 
requirements of funds, may be raised with the Ministry of Finance at RE stage 
for proper implementation of various projects in Bangladesh. The Ministry may 
keep the Committee apprised of all the progress made in the implementation of 
various projects. 
 
Aid to Bhutan 
21. The Committee note that the largest share of India’s aid portfolio 
constituting 35.33 percent of the Ministry’s development assistance budget 
has been extended to Bhutan during 2026-27, i.e. Rs.2288.56 crore out of 

202 | P a g e 
 
Rs.6477.64.  It includes Rs.1768.64 crore as Grant and Rs.519.92 crore as Loan 
to Bhutan.  The funds under the Grants head are utilized for various 
development projects, subsidy payments, scholarships to Bhutanese students 
studying in various institutions in India, etc.  The Committee are happy to note 
that due to India’s development assistance and technical support in the last 
five decades, Bhutan had graduated from Least Developed Country (LDC) to 
Middle Income Country status in December 2023. The Government of India has 
also allocated Rs.10,000 crore which is 19.5 percent of the Royal Government 
of Bhutan’s Budgetary Outlay for 13th Five Year Plan of Bhutan (2024-29), 
which has commenced from July 2024.  The Committee further note that till 
date, 572 High Impact Community Development Projects (HICDP), and 71 
Project Tied Assistance (PTA) have been approved by both the Governments 
and have commenced implementation.  The Government of India (GOI) has 
released an amount of Rs. 175 crore for Project Grant (PG), and Rs.1000 crore 
for Economic Stimulus Programme (ESP) to Bhutan.  The Committee commend 
the GOI for the above measures, which has greatly helped in maintaining 
mutual trust and understanding, an generate unmatched goodwill at all level of 
Bhutanese society. The Committee urge the Ministry to take all necessary 
measures to ensure proper and timely utilization of funds.  Since Bhutan has 
been traditionally the largest recipient of India Aid assistance, the Committee 
also desire that a proper Impact Assessment study and review of the 
implementation of various projects may also be undertaken and the report 
furnish to the Committee. However, due attention must be paid to 
environmental impact of any project. 
 
22. The Committee note that hydro-electricity is central to India-Bhutan        
economic cooperation. Support in the form of grant and loan has been 
provided by India for construction of hydro-power projects in Bhutan.  Till 
date, India and Bhutan have jointly constructed four major Hydro Electric 
Projects (HEPs) having a total installed capacity of 2136 MW, and 2 HEPs with 
a total capacity of 2220 MW are ongoing. The Committee have been informed 
by the Ministry that from a strategic and economic point of view, the 
implementation of hydro-electric projects under the Governmental Agreements 
is a win-win partnership for both India and Bhutan, and the surplus power from 

203 | P a g e 
 
these projects are exported to India.  The Committee note that Punatsangchhu-
II HEP (1020MW) has been inaugurated in November 2025.  It will be handed 
over to Bhutan before August 2027 after addressing various issues, such as 
defect liability and testing.  All geological studies have also been completed 
for Punatsangchhu-I HEP (1200MW) and full-scale dam construction work will 
start from March 2026.  While noting the progress made for the implementation 
of these projects, the Committee urge the Ministry to ensure timely handover 
of Punatsangchhu-II HEP, and also ensure proper and timely implementation of 
Punatsangchhu-I HEP. The Committee note that while for Bhutan power 
exports are a primary source of revenue, it will provide clean and renewable 
electricity to India, reducing India’s dependence on fossil fuels and ensuring 
long-term power purchases at predictable prices.

Cross-border Rail links, supply of defence stores, capacity buildings and 
scholarships, humanitarian assistance, etc.  Nepal, besides being a landlocked

and timely implementation of various projects in Nepal.  The Committee desire

challenges being faced by the people in Nepal, and promote mutual goodwill

‘Neighbourhood First’ policy, it is important that India’s footprint in the Island 
nation’s economy, people’s welfare and development continue to grow.  In this 
regard, the Committee urge the Ministry to ensure that progress of 
implementations of various projects are not affected due to shortage of funds.  
The Committee, therefore, recommend that additional requirements of funds, if 
any, may be sought from the Ministry of Finance at RE stage.

capacity building programmes.  The Committee note that India provides the 
largest number of training opportunities for Maldivian National Defence Force 
(MNDF), meeting around 70 percent of their defence training requirements.

Community Development Projects, education & capacity building programmes 
etc. While commending the Ministry for various types of assistances extended 

205 | P a g e 
 
to Maldives which hold a significant strategic importance for India, the

projects in the Island country. 
 
Aid to Myanmar 
26. The Committee note that India has been providing assistance to 
Myanmar in development cooperation, capacity building, disaster relief, etc.  
As per information provided by the Ministry, India’s development assistance 
portfolio in Myanmar is now over US $ 1.75 billion, bulk of which is grant 
funded.  With regard to utilization of fund, the Committee note that during 
2025-26, an amount of Rs.350 crore had been allocated BE, which was reduced 
to Rs.200 crore at RE.  The actual utilization up to January 2026 was Rs. 175.83 
crore.  For the year 2026-27, an amount of Rs.300 crore has been allocated at 
BE.  The Committee note that political turmoil and the security situation in 
Myanmar have impacted the pace of progress of key projects and the scope of 
newer projects has also witnessed a declining trend.  One of the main ongoing 
projects which has been affected by the present security and political situation 
in Myanmar is the timely completion of the road component of Kaladan Multi 
Modal Transit Transport (KMMTT), 109.2 km being built by IRCON.  The 
Committee are, however, glad to note that ground level difficulties are being 
addressed and work is continuing in the segments where access is available.  
Considering that Myanmar is the only land bridge between India and the South 
East Asian countries which holds significant importance for the success of 
India’s Act East Policy, the Committee encourage the Ministry to continue to 
engage with the new Government in Myanmar so as to identify their priorities 
and also ensure timely completion of the various ongoing projects, such as the 
road component of KMMTT which will greatly improve connectivity and 
promote economic growth in the North Eastern States.

Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation 
 
27. Under the head ‘Indian Technical and Economic Cooperation’, MEA has 
been allocating funds to meet expenditure for foreign candidates in defence and 
civilian training programmes, deputation of Indian experts to other countries, 
and technical assistance to developing countries.  During 2025-26, an amount of 
Rs.180 crore was allocated at BE, which was increased to Rs. 220 crore at RE.  
Out of the amount allocated at RE, an amount of Rs.141.03 crore had been 
utilized by January 2026.  For the year 2026-27, an amount of Rs.220 crore has 
been allocated at BE, Rs.182 crore for civilian ITEC courses and Rs. 38 crore for 
ITEC Defence courses. The Committee further note that the primary 
beneficiaries of the ITEC programmes are our neighbouring countries, as well 
as partner countries in the Global South.  The Committee are glad to note that 
during 2025-26 alone, close to 12,000 and 3,800 beneficiaries have been offered 
slots under ITEC at eminent civil and defence training institutions. The 
Committee compliment the Ministry for successfully implementing various 
courses under ITEC which has been well received by countries of the Global 
South and the extremely positive assessments received at various forums. The 
Committee urge the Ministry to increase the capacity to provide more slots in 
the coming years, depending on the number of requests received for 
customized courses each year.  
 
28. The Committee observe that ITEC is one of the world’s longest running 
structured capacity building programme, started in 1964, and is now an 
important strand of India’s Development Partnership.  The Committee are of the 
view that there is a need for conducting comprehensive preview of this 
programme to make it more relevant and useful for the neighbouring countries 
and countries of the Global South.  Since a comprehensive review of the ITEC 
programme was last conducted from November 2017 to March 2018 resulting in 
various outcomes, such as increasing the total number of institutes, updating 
ITEC portal database, better alumni engagement, improving inputs process, etc., 
the Committee recommend the Ministry to have a mechanism for regular 
conduct of a comprehensive review of the various programmes offered by ITEC.  
The Committee further desire that various customized courses in the field of 

207 | P a g e 
 
agriculture, health, education, environment and emerging innovations, such as 
AI may also be included in the training courses.  The Committee hope that the 
Ministry will take all necessary measures to ensure that various training courses 
being implemented under ITEC will continue to address the various 
developmental challenges of our partner countries.

implementations, in consultation with the Afghan government, that will benefit 
the people of Afghanistan.  The Committee would also like to impress upon the 
Ministry that safety concerns of Indian personnel involved in developmental and 
humanitarian work may be given adequate priority.

209 | P a g e 
 
welfare of women in Afghanistan and remain mindful of the need of Afghan

during the Second India Telecom Summit held in Georgetown in November
