investment, and strategic partnerships.  India has also consistently engaged 
with key regional grouping in Central America, including CARICOM (Caribbean 
Community) and SICA (Central American Integration System).  The Committee 
are glad to note that the increase in budgetary allocation is in alignment with 
the region’s growing importance in India’s foreign policy.  The Committee hope 
that the increased allocation would be fruitfully utilized for implementation of 
various projects, which will elevate India’s position and promote cooperation 
between India and Latin American Countries in various international forums 
and regional groupings. 
 

210 | P a g e 
 
34. The Committee note that factors such as geographical distance, 
logistical barriers, political instability, etc. are acting as barriers to realize full 
trade potential between India and Latin American countries.  The Committee 
recommend that the Government may take various measures to increase 
bilateral trade with Latin American countries, such as early completion of a 
Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement with Chile, expansion of 
Preferential Trade Agreement into Free Trade Agreement with Peru, expansion 
of India’s PTA with MERCUSOR countries, which includes Argentina, Bolivia, 
Brazil, Paraguay and Uruguay. The Ministry may also keep the Committee 
apprised of all the progress made in this regard. 
 
35. The Committee also note that Government of India is encouraging public 
and private Indian Companies to enter into critical minerals and rare earth 
material sector of south American countries, through direct license, or through 
JV with a local company, and invest through capital and/or technology.  The 
Committee are given to understand the Latin America is key to the global rare 
earth race.  However, despite having healthy resources, the production has 
been minimum due to lack of capital and technology.  The Committee are of 
the considered view that Government of India should actively promote Indian 
industries, both public and private, by giving them proper policy support for 
their expeditious participation in the critical / rare earth mineral sector so as to 
meet the growing demand for rare earth minerals in the country.  
 
Chabahar Port 
 
36. The Committee find that for the development of Chabahar Port, an 
amount of Rs.100 crore was allocated at BE 2025-26, which was increased to 
Rs.400 crore at RE.  The allocated amount had been fully utilized by January 
2026.  The Ministry has informed the Committee that no amount has been 
allocated under the head during 2026-27 as India has already fulfilled its 
commitment of contributing USD 120 million for the procurement of port 
equipment, as per the main contract signed in 2024.  The Committee are also 
aware of the fact that on 16 September 2025, the US State Department, in a 
press statement, revoked the sanctions exception issued in 2018 under the 

211 | P a g e 
 
Iran Freedom and Counter Proliferation Act 2012 for Afghanistan’s 
reconstruction and economic development, effective 29 September 2025.  
Pursuant to discussions with the US side, the latter issued guidance extending 
the conditional sanctions waiver until 26 April 2026.  Besides, the recent 
development has cast a shadow on the future of Chabahar Port.  The 
Committee are of the view that Chabahar is a vital and strategic port for India, 
as it provides access to Afghanistan and Central Asia.  It is also a key port for 
connecting with International North South Transport Corridor (INSTC).  The 
Committee welcome that the Government of India remains engaged with all 
concerned parties in order to address the implications of these developments. 
The Committee desire that the Ministry may keep the Committee apprised of all 
the plans and progress in this regard. 
 
Budgetary Allocation to Passport and Emigration 
37. The Committee note that the budgetary allocation under the sub-head 
‘Passport and Emigration’ has risen sharply from Rs. 970.66 crore at BE 2024-
25 to Rs. 1,913.48 crore at BE 2025-26, and further to Rs. 2,110.13 crore at BE 
2026-27. The principal drivers of this increase are the rollout of the upgraded 
Passport Seva Programme Version 2.0 (PSP V2.0) and the Global Passport 
Seva Programme Version 2.0 (GPSP V2.0), the substantially higher cost per 
application processed under the new vendor arrangement, the increased cost 
of e-Passport booklets, the significantly expanded volume of passport 
applications, and the increased allocation to States and Union Territories for 
police verification. The Committee acknowledge the significance of the PSP 
V2.0 rollout as a major milestone in the digital transformation of passport 
services. The pan-India rollout was completed on 26 May 2025 and GPSP V2.0 
across 37 Regional Passport Offices and 203 Indian Missions and Posts 
abroad by 28 October 2025. The Committee note with satisfaction that average 
passport issuance time, excluding police verification, currently stands at 7-10 
working days for normal passports and 1-3 working days for Tatkal passports. 
The Committee also note that PSP V2.0 is being integrated with Aadhaar, PAN, 

212 | P a g e 
 
and Voter ID databases at source level for real-time document authentication 
for reducing processing times and eliminating fraudulent applications. 
The Committee are of the view that the transition to PSP V2.0 represents 
a generational upgrade in India’s passport infrastructure and welcome the 
Ministry’s commitment towards continuous improvement in this citizen-centric 
service. The Committee welcome the successful rollout of e-Passports across 
all Passport Offices in India and all Indian Missions and Posts abroad, and 
note with satisfaction that approximately 1.02 crore e-Passports have been 
issued as on 31 January 2026. The Committee further note the Foreign 
Secretary’s submission that outstanding payments for passport production are 
carried forward to subsequent years, which can create budgetary pressure.  
The Committee recommend that the Ministry should settle all liabilities to the 
service provider, the Department of Posts, and State and Union Territory 
governments within the year in which they arise. The Ministry should also 
apprise the Committee of the current outstanding payment position in a timely 
manner. 
 
Expansion of Passport Network 
38. The Committee note that the passport network now covers 512 Lok 
Sabha constituencies through 93 Passport Seva Kendras and 452 Post Office 
Passport Seva Kendras under 37 Regional Passport Offices. The Committee 
note that 24.50% of all passport applications are now handled through 
POPSKs, demonstrating their importance as access points for citizens away 
from PSKs. 8 new POPSKs (Rajnagar, Bakhra, Kalpetta, Khargone, Bhind, 
Mandla, Kushinagar, and Pollachi) were added between March and December 
2025. The Committee appreciate the successful expansion of passport 
services through the Post Office Passport Seva Kendras (POPSKs). The 
Committee, however, note that 31 Lok Sabha constituencies remain without a 
PSK or POPSK, and that the Ministry plans to establish new centres in at least 
seven of these constituencies during 2026. Some of the passport offices are in 
need of repair, renovation and expansion such as Berhampur (Odisha), Raipur 
(Chhattisgarh) and Meerut (Uttar Pradesh). The Committee note the Ministry’s 

213 | P a g e 
 
plan to cover at least seven such constituencies during 2026 and urge that this 
target be treated as a minimum rather than a ceiling. The Committee also 
recommend that the Ministry should expedite the establishment of PSKs and 
POPSKs in the 31 remaining unserved Lok Sabha constituencies.  
 
 
mPassport Police App 
39. The Committee note that the mPassport Police App, which enables 
paperless, end-to-end digital submission of Police Verification Reports directly 
from police stations, has been launched in 25 States and Union Territories, 
covering more than 9,425 police stations. The Ministry has informed that Police 
Verification Reports are now being received within 2 to 21 days in States where 
the App has been deployed, excluding the North-East and Jammu and 
Kashmir. Twelve States and Union Territories, including Arunachal Pradesh, 
Manipur, Meghalaya, Mizoram, Nagaland, Jammu and Kashmir, Ladakh, and 
others, are yet to launch the App, with rollout contingent on local IT 
infrastructure readiness.  
The Committee note that police verification remains the principal 
determinant of the total time taken for normal passport issuance and that the 
Ministry has no direct administrative control over State police establishments. 
The Committee recommend that the Ministry should prioritise the deployment 
of the mPassport Police App in the twelve remaining States and Union 
Territories where it has not yet been launched. The Ministry, in consultation 
with each of the twelve remaining States and Union Territories, should also 
prepare a roadmap for App deployment including identification of IT 
infrastructure gaps, a timeline for their resolution, and a training plan for police 
personnel. The Committee would like to be apprised about the progress made 
in this regard. 
 
 
 

214 | P a g e 
 
Human Capital in the Central Passport Organization 
40. The Committee note that in their previous Report, the Committee had 
expressed serious concern at the Central Passport Organization functioning 
with a working strength of 1,737 against a sanctioned strength of 2,737, and 
had specifically recommended expediting recruitment, particularly for 
Assistant Passport Officers and Verifying Officers. As on 31 January 2026, the 
working strength stands at 1,793 against a sanctioned strength of 2,737, an 
improvement of only 56 posts in a year, leaving a vacancy of 944 posts, a 
shortfall of 34.5%. The position in critical operational grades is particularly 
alarming: the Superintendent cadre (Pay Level 7) has only 9 officers working 
against a sanctioned strength of 245 (over 96% vacant); the Assistant 
Superintendent cadre stands at 45% vacant; and the Junior Passport Assistant 
cadre at 42% vacant.  
While acknowledging the interim measures taken (such as contractual 
DEOs and MTS, deputation of Section Officers, Young Professionals, and SSC 
indents), the Committee are of the firm view that these temporary 
arrangements cannot be a substitute for a fully staffed permanent 
establishment in a statutory, sovereign function. The Committee reiterate their 
recommendation that the Ministry should prioritize filling vacancies in CPO, 
particularly in the grades of Assistant Passport Officer, Granting Officer, and 
Verifying Officer, which are central to the integrity of passport adjudication. 
The Ministry should also review whether the sanctioned strength of CPO 
requires upward revision given the significant expansion of passport services, 
including the rollout of PSP V2.0, the e-Passport transition, and the expanding 
POPSK network.  
 
 MEA Organizational Structure 
41. The Committee note that the Ministry of External Affairs operates through a 
headquarters consisting of 59 Divisions, Wings, and Cells in New Delhi, 
supported by a network of 216 Missions and Posts abroad. As against the 
sanctioned strength of 8697, the actual working strength of MEA is 6035. The 

215 | P a g e 
 
Ministry has attributed this gap to recruitment cycles, cadre management, 
retirements, and the time lag between creation of posts and their filling through 
Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) and Staff Selection Commission (SSC). 
The Ministry further informed that the Fifth Cadre review of the Indian Foreign 
Service, approved by the Union Cabinet in October 2023, is currently under 
implementation and is expected to be completed by 2027. 
The Committee observe that over the past decade, the Ministry’s functional 
mandate has grown significantly, not merely in terms of the number of Missions 
and Posts, but in the substantive complexity of its work. The Committee have, 
over successive reports, expressed concern about the persistent gap between 
the sanctioned and actual strength of the Ministry across various cadres. As 
India's diplomatic network and international responsibilities continue to expand, 
any sustained gap between sanctioned and actual strength directly impairs the 
Ministry's ability to fulfil its mandate effectively. 
Given India’s expanding diplomatic footprint, the Committee urge the 
Ministry to work closely with the Union Public Service Commission (UPSC) and 
Staff Selection Commission (SSC) to streamline recruitment cycles and reduce 
the time lag between creation of posts and their fulfilment. Further, the Ministry 
should continue to create cadre/induct expertise from other Ministries, think 
tanks, academia and private sector, as needed, to supplement its human 
resource with relevant domain expertise.  
 
Induction of Specialized Domain Expertise 
42. The Committee note that modern diplomacy increasingly demands 
specialized expertise in areas such as climate change, health security, cyber 
security, artificial intelligence, international trade, and digital public 
infrastructure - areas that go well beyond the traditional generalist profile of 
the Indian Foreign Service. When asked about the possibility of lateral entry of 
domain experts, the Ministry stated that it addresses this requirement through: 
IFS recruitment via the Civil Services Examination; deputation of specialized 
officers from other Ministries and Departments; and engagement of 
consultants in areas including counter terrorism, project management, 
international law, consular work, and disarmament. The Ministry has also 

216 | P a g e 
 
submitted that it has placed a request with DoPT for filling one post at the 
DS/Director level for a legal expert through the lateral entry route. 
The Committee note that the Ministry engages consultants for several 
key functions. While this is a useful short-term arrangement, the Committee 
are concerned that consultancy should not become a permanent substitute for 
building in-house institutional expertise. The Committee recommend that the 
Ministry furnish to it a statement on the number of consultants engaged, the 
areas in which they are deployed, their terms of engagement, and the specific 
outputs delivered. The Committee recommend that the Ministry should explore 
the possibility of establishing dedicated specialist cadres/ pools within the 
MEA for specific high-priority domains, drawing on the models of other foreign 
ministries that have created specialized tracks within their diplomatic services 
for technology, environment, and economic diplomacy.  
 
Gender representation 
43. The Committee note that the current representation of women within the 
IFS stands at 27.78%. Over the past decade, women officers entering the IFS 
have constituted approximately 36% of each incoming batch (36.58% in 2016 
and 36.53% in 2025 and has been as high as 42.8% in 2021). The Committee are 
hopeful that with this sustained and encouraging entry-level inflow, the overall 
representation of women within the IFS will improve progressively in the years 
ahead. The Committee note, however, that the gap between the entry-level 
proportion of approximately 36% and the overall representation of 27.78% is 
significant. This concern is reinforced by the fact that only 13 of India's 216 
Heads of Mission are women. The Committee note that the Ministry has 
acknowledged the work-life balance challenges inherent in a service requiring 
frequent transfers and overseas postings, and has put in place supporting 
measures including crèche facilities, a structured mentorship programme, 
maternity and child care leave, and an Internal Complaints Committee under 
the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and 
Redressal) Act, 2013 Act. 

217 | P a g e 
 
The Committee are of the view that in a service that engages daily with 
gender issues on the world stage, India's own diplomatic corps must reflect 
the values of equity and equal opportunity that the country advocates 
internationally. While the UPSC examination process is gender-neutral and the 
entry-level data is encouraging, the Ministry must take proactive steps to 
ensure that women officers are supported, retained, and enabled to rise 
through the ranks at every stage of their careers. The Committee recommend 
that the Ministry take proactive steps to support the recruitment and retention 
of women across all cadres of MEA, not only in the IFS. Targeted awareness 
programmes, outreach to women candidates in non-metropolitan areas 
through SAMEEP (Student & MEA Engagement Programme), and career 
development initiatives within the Ministry should be explored and 
implemented in a structured manner. 
 
Training 
44. The Committee note that the Sushma Swaraj Institute of Foreign Service 
(SSIFS) is the primary institution for in-service training of Indian Foreign 
Service officers and other MEA personnel, training of foreign diplomats, and 
professional development programmes. The budgetary allocation for SSIFS 
has remained largely unchanged over the past four years, moving from Rs. 
21.19 crore in BE 2023-24 to Rs. 24.37 crore in BE 2026-27. The actual 
expenditure during this period has similarly remained constant at Rs. 24.38 
crore in 2022-23, Rs. 24.81 crore in 2023-24, and Rs. 20.41 crore in 2024-25. The 
Committee further observe that SSIFS currently has only 10-12 in-house 
faculty members, has MoUs with 103 countries and four plurilateral groupings, 
and has recently launched a Learning Management Software (LMS) platform in 
January 2025 to digitize and modernize its training delivery. 
The Committee are concerned to note that the budgetary allocation for 
SSIFS, which is the sole dedicated training institution for India’s entire foreign 
service cadre, has remained largely unchanged even as the demands of 
modern diplomacy have grown considerably in scope and complexity. With 
India’s diplomatic network expanding to 216 Missions and Posts, and with 

218 | P a g e 
 
diplomats increasingly required to navigate different areas such as geo-
technology, digital diplomacy, international trade negotiations, climate policy, 
and health security alongside traditional foreign policy functions, an annual 
training budget of Rs. 24.37 crore and a permanent faculty strength of 10-12 is, 
in the Committee’s view, clearly inadequate for SSIFS, which is an institution 
of national importance. The Committee also note that the Institute draws 
visiting faculties from retired Indian Ambassadors, reputed domain experts, 
senior officials of MEA and other Ministries.  
The Committee welcome the launch of the LMS platform in January 2025 
and the positive results noted in terms of improved digital proficiency and time 
management among officer trainees. The Committee recommend that the 
budgetary allocation for SSIFS be substantially enhanced in the coming years 
to reflect the growing demands on India’s diplomatic service. The Committee 
are of the view that investment in training India’s diplomats is an investment in 
India’s foreign policy capacity itself, and must be treated as a priority 
expenditure. 
 
Strengthening the Policy Planning and Research Division 
45. The Committee note that the Policy Planning and Research (PP&R) 
Division serves as the nodal Division of the Ministry for medium-term policy 
planning, research, strategic outreach, coordination of India's Neighbourhood 
First Policy, and the Voice of Global South Summit initiative. In addition to 
organising four flagship Track 1.5 initiatives (the Raisina Dialogue, Global 
Technology Summit, Asia Economic Dialogue, and the Indian Ocean 
Conference), the Division also conducts Track 1.0 policy planning dialogues 
with the Foreign Offices of more than 20 countries, manages the Strategic 
Outreach Fund, oversees the MEA Niti Shakti Initiative, coordinates the work of 
ICWA and RIS, and is responsible for the preparation and publication of the 
Ministry's Annual Report. The Committee further note that the Division has 
commendably expanded its outreach beyond New Delhi, with strategic 
dialogues and conferences now being organised across cities such as 
Bengaluru, Mumbai, Kochi, Bhubaneswar, Jaipur, Kolkata, and 

219 | P a g e 
 
Thiruvananthapuram, engaging universities, think tanks, and state 
governments in the foreign policy discourse. 
The Committee are of the view that the PP&R Division carries an 
exceptionally wide and diverse mandate. While the Committee welcome the 
expanding footprint of the Division's activities, it is concerned to note that the 
breadth of administrative, coordination, and event management 
responsibilities placed upon the Division may be drawing attention and 
capacity away from its primary and most critical function, namely substantive 
policy research, strategic analysis, and medium-term foreign policy planning. 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry should undertake a 
structured review of the PP&R Division's mandate, activities, and resource 
allocation to ensure that its core intellectual and policy planning functions 
receive the requisite focus and priority. The Committee also urge that the 
Ministry to bring out a brief statement on the key research outputs, policy 
briefs, and strategic assessments produced by the PP&R Division during the 
year, along with an indication of how these have fed into the Ministry's policy 
deliberations and decisions. 
46.  The Committee note that officers posted to the PP&R Division are 
required to track developments across the full spectrum of global affairs,  
geopolitics, geo-economics, and geo-technology  and produce a range of 
outputs including the Daily Report, connecting the dots, Arth Niti, Raksha Niti, 
Tech Niti, Monthly Reports, situation reports, and policy briefs, which are 
disseminated to the Ministry, Missions abroad, and agencies including the 
PMO, Ministry of Defence, and the National Security Council Secretariat. The 
Committee also note that the Ministry has taken positive steps to 
institutionalize external expertise through the MEA Niti Shakti Initiative, which 
enables divisions to commission research from think tanks and universities, 
through the Strategic Outreach Fund. 
The Committee are of the considered view that given the rapidly 
evolving and complex global landscape, the quality and depth of India's 
foreign policy planning is critically dependent on the availability of adequate 
human resources with specialized domain expertise within the PP&R Division, 

220 | P a g e 
 
supplemented by a robust and structured engagement with India's universities 
and think tank community. The Committee are concerned to note that the 
current model of engagement with external institutions, while wide in its reach 
remains largely event-driven and project-specific, and does not appear to be 
anchored in a systematic, long-term framework for knowledge partnership. 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry should 
undertake a comprehensive assessment of the human resource requirements 
of the PP&R Division, having regard to the full scope of its mandate, and 
ensure that the Division is adequately staffed with officers with requisite 
domain expertise in specialized areas such as geo-technology, international 
economics, climate diplomacy, and regional studies. The Committee further 
urge the Ministry to move beyond event-based collaboration with think tanks 
and universities towards more enduring, structured knowledge partnerships. 
 
Grand Strategy / Foreign Policy Document 
47. The Committee note that with regard to formulating a grand strategy or a 
comprehensive foreign policy document that clearly articulates India's foreign 
policy objectives, goals, and long-term strategic approach, the Ministry 
submitted that no single overarching and formalised strategic document 
currently exists, and that India's diplomatic principles and priorities are 
expressed through various platforms. The Committee has been furnished with 
a list of 36 countries, including major powers such as the United States, United 
Kingdom, Germany, Japan, and Russia, as well as smaller nations such as 
Estonia, New Zealand, Slovenia, and Bhutan, that have published formal 
foreign policy or national security strategy documents laying out their 
international objectives, priorities, and approaches in a single accessible 
document. 
The Committee are of the considered view that the absence of a formal, 
publicly available foreign policy document is a significant gap for a country of 
India's global stature, ambitions, and expanding international engagement. The 
Ministry's Annual Report, while comprehensive and valuable as a record of the 

221 | P a g e 
 
previous year's diplomatic activities, is inherently a retrospective document. It 
is not a forward-looking strategic document that articulates where India seeks 
to go in the world, what its priorities are, and how it intends to pursue them 
over the medium to long term. The Committee also note that the Ministry has 
acknowledged that there are no obstacles to preparing such a document. In 
this context, the Committee would like to understand why such a document 
has not been prepared thus far, particularly given India's growing global profile 
and its active engagement across bilateral, regional, and multilateral platforms. 
The Committee, therefore, strongly recommend that the Ministry, 
through the PP&R Division, take up on priority the preparation of a formal 
Indian Foreign Policy Document - a publicly available, medium-term strategic 
document that clearly articulates India's foreign policy objectives, regional and 
global priorities, key bilateral and multilateral engagements, and the guiding 
principles of India's approach to the world. The Committee urge the Ministry to 
inform them of the timeline within which this exercise will be completed. 
 
Budgetary allocation to ‘Embassies and Missions’ 
48. The allocation under the sub-head ‘Embassies and Mission’ has been 
progressively enhanced, from Rs. 3,528.85 crore in BE 2023-24 to Rs. 4,206.22 
crore in BE 2025-26 and Rs. 5,059.30 crore in BE 2026-27, reflecting the 
growing scale and complexity of India’s diplomatic engagements. The 
Committee observe that the RE stage has consistently seen significant 
augmentation. In 2025-26, the RE allocation of Rs. 4,632.27 crore, which was 
Rs. 426.05 crore above the BE. The Committee note with concern that in each 
of the past four years, actual expenditure has broadly been in line with the RE 
allocations, underlining that the BE stage consistently underestimates the 
Ministry’s genuine resource requirements for its Missions abroad. 
The Ministry has explained that the enhanced BE 2026-27 allocation 
includes provisioning for five new Missions and Posts operationalized in 2026-
27 and that the Ministry starts ‘from a lower base’ at BE with augmentation 
sought at RE. The Committee are of the view that this may lead to mid-year 
disruptions in Mission’s functioning and uncertainty in fiscal planning. The 

222 | P a g e 
 
Committee strongly recommend that the Ministry should move away from the 
practice of under-provisioning for the ‘Embassies and Missions’ head at BE 
stage and routinely seeking correction at RE. The practice of beginning ‘from a 
lower base’ may distort the budgetary picture, weakens the Ministry’s case 
before the Ministry of Finance, and create operational uncertainty for 
Missions/Posts. The Committee also recommend that the Ministry should also 
develop and maintain a consolidated database of per-Mission expenditure 
across all heads, updated on a quarterly basis, to enable more precise 
budgetary planning. 
 
Geographical Spread of Embassies and Posts abroad 
49. The Committee note that India currently maintains 219 Missions and 
Posts across the world, making it one of the most expansive diplomatic 
networks among developing nations. In the last financial year, one new 
Mission in Quito (Ecuador) and four new Posts in Boston (USA), Los Angeles 
(USA), Kazan (Russia) and Yekaterinburg (Russia) have been operationalized. 
The Committee welcome India’s expanding diplomatic footprint and note that 
this expansion has been driven by a considered assessment of bilateral 
potential, trade and investment priorities, diaspora presence, and India’s 
growing multilateral leadership.  
The Committee note, however, that as of date, India does not have 
resident Missions in 40 UN member countries. While many of these are small 
island states where concurrent accreditation may be sufficient, the list also 
includes countries of diplomatic and strategic significance such as Andorra, 
Bosnia and Herzegovina, Costa Rica, El Salvador, Honduras, Montenegro, 
North Macedonia, Moldova, Nicaragua, and Uruguay. The Ministry has 
acknowledged that the scope and depth of bilateral relations with countries 
lacking resident Indian Missions is regularly reviewed. The Committee are of 
the view that this review must be formalized and its outcomes made more 
transparent. The Ministry should carry out a comprehensive assessment of the 
40 countries where India lacks resident Missions, identifying priority cases and 
roadmap for early Mission establishment.  

223 | P a g e 
 
Security of Indian Missions and Posts Abroad 
50. The Committee note with concern that the Ministry does not have a 
dedicated budget head for the security of Indian Missions and Posts abroad, 
and that security expenditure is met from establishment heads “as per 
requirement.” The Committee are of the view that this arrangement may be 
fundamentally inadequate in a period of heightened geopolitical tensions, 
multiple active conflict zones, and increased targeting of diplomatic premises 
globally. The Committee note that the funding of expenditure for security of 
Indian diplomats, mission staff, and consular facilities from general 
establishment heads may not be adequate to address the systematic threat 
environment that Indian Missions face. The Committee strongly recommend 
that the Ministry create a dedicated budget sub-head for Mission Security, with 
a specific annual allocation based on a comprehensive threat assessment and 
security audit of all Indian Missions and Posts abroad. The Committee further 
recommend that the Ministry should conduct an immediate comprehensive 
security audit of all Indian Missions, prioritizing high-risk locations including 
conflict zones, countries with strained bilateral relations, and locations with 
known security vulnerabilities, along with a time-bound security enhancement 
plan and its budgetary implications. 
 
Monitoring Mechanism for Embassies and Posts abroad 
51. Recognizing the importance of an effective monitoring mechanism for 
ensuring the optimal performance of Indian Missions and Posts abroad, the 
Committee note the establishment of the Performance Evaluation and 
Monitoring System (PEMS) by the Ministry in 2016. The Ministry is currently 
evaluating the performance of 203 Missions/Posts for FY 2024-25 under PEMS 
6.0. The Committee note that PEMS evaluation scores feed into a physical 
inspection cycle conducted by the Directorate General of Inspections (DGI), 
with inspection findings followed up through Action Taken Reports until 
compliance is achieved. The Committee also note the Ministry’s deployment of 
multiple technology platforms for Mission operations, including IMAS 2.0 for 
integrated mission accounting, MEADOWS for HR management, the MADAD 

224 | P a g e 
 
portal for consular grievance redressal, eMigrate for emigration processing, 
and the 3Ts (Trade, Tourism and Technology) dashboard for economic 
diplomacy tracking.  
Acknowledging the efforts of the Ministry towards making the PEMS 
interface more user friendly, the Committee recommend that periodic feedback 
surveys from the Missions and Posts should be regularly conducted to identify 
scope for further improvements. The Committee desire to be apprised of the 
current status of the performance evaluation being done for the 203 
Missions/Posts abroad under PMS 6.0 and therefore Ministry may furnish a 
report on the performance of India’s Mission network, drawing on PEMS data 
and DGI inspection findings in its Action Taken Replies.  
 
Human capital at Mission and Posts abroad 
52. The Committee note that as of 1 January 2026, the sanctioned strength 
of the Ministry stands at 6,035, of which 54.08% are located in Missions and 
Posts abroad, with an additional local cadre strength of 3,179 at 
Missions/Posts. The Ministry has stated that vacancies are regularly reviewed 
and officers posted against vacancies. However, the Committee observe that 
the Ministry has not provided the Committee with the vacancy position, that is, 
how many of the 6,035 sanctioned posts are actually filled, which cadres have 
the highest vacancy rates, and what is the Ministry’s specific recruitment and 
filling timeline. The Committee recommend that the Ministry should furnish a 
complete cadre-wise statement of sanctioned strength, filled positions, 
vacancies and expected timeline for filling vacancies for each cadre within the 
Ministry, both at Headquarters and at Missions/Posts abroad. 
 
Nalanda University 
53. The Committee acknowledge the steadfast commitment of the 
Government of India in reviving the ancient seat of learning through Nalanda 
University, established under the Nalanda University Act, 2010 as an 

225 | P a g e 
 
international institution under the East Asia Summit (EAS) framework with 17 
member countries as founding partners. The Committee note with satisfaction 
that a state-of-the-art Net-Zero sustainable campus spanning approximately 
455 acres was formally inaugurated by the Prime Minister in June 2024. 
Notwithstanding this milestone, the Committee observe that construction of 
the Central Library and the 2,000-seater auditorium remain to be completed. 
The Committee also note that the project faced significant time and cost 
overruns on account of the remote location of the campus, the logistical 
challenges of mobilizing skilled manpower, and the severe disruptions caused 
by the COVID-19 pandemic between 2020 and 2022. 
The Committee express their concern that despite its founding premise 
as a collectively supported international institution, financial contributions 
from EAS member countries have been both minimal and largely confined to 
the University’s early years. Twelve of the seventeen founding partner 
countries have made no contribution. The burden of sustaining the University 
has fallen almost entirely on the Government of India. The Committee welcome 
the constitution of an Endowment Committee to develop a fundraising strategy 
covering philanthropic donations, corporate sponsorships, and support from 
global foundations. 
The Committee reiterate their concern, expressed in previous reports, 
that the financial engagement of EAS founding partner countries with Nalanda 
University has remained inadequate. The Committee recommend that the 
Ministry should move beyond general diplomatic encouragement and design a 
structured, time-bound initiative to secure meaningful financial commitments 
from EAS member countries, with specific bilateral engagement at an 
appropriately senior level. The Committee would like to be apprised of the 
outcome of these efforts.  
54. The Committee note that Nalanda University has made significant 
academic progress since commencing operations in 2014. The University now 
operates six Schools: Historical Studies, Ecology and Environment Studies, 
Buddhist Studies, Philosophy and Comparative Religions, Languages and 
Literature, Management Studies, and International Relations and Peace 

226 | P a g e 
 
Studies, and offers over 30 academic programmes. Total student enrolment 
stands at 1,925 in 2025-26 from an initial intake of 12 students in 2014-15. The 
Committee further note that Nalanda University is the nodal institution of the 
ASEAN-India University Network (AINU) and has established bilateral MoUs 
with institutions across Asia, Europe, and North America, including partners in 
Japan, Republic of Korea, Indonesia, Vietnam, Sri Lanka, Cambodia, Nepal, 
Mongolia, Russia, Spain, the United States, and the Czech Republic. The 
Committee were informed by MEA that AINU-related collaborations are fully 
funded by the ASEAN Secretariat, ensuring that they do not place a financial 
burden on the University. The Committee, however, note that these 
collaborations, while wide in geographical reach, have yet to translate into a 
globally recognized research output or academic reputation. 
In the considered opinion of the Committee, with the substantial 
operationalization of the permanent campus, the University must transition 
decisively from a phase of infrastructure development to one of academic 
consolidation and excellence. The Committee reiterate that the University’s 
long-term credibility as an instrument of India’s educational diplomacy will 
depend on the quality and global standing of its academic and research 
output. The Committee recommend that the Ministry, in consultation with 
Nalanda University’s Governing Board, should prepare an academic 
development plan covering projected enrolment targets for each programme 
stream, proposed new academic offerings, faculty recruitment timelines, and 
research output goals. 
 
South Asian University 
55. In spite of its regional significance as a flagship initiative under SAARC, 
the tardy progress in the establishment and operationalization of the South 
Asian University remains a matter of concern to the Committee. The 
Committee note that the capital budget of SAU is sourced entirely from India, 
with the Union Cabinet in 2009 approving a contribution of USD 198 million 
towards capital expenditure, of which USD 171.461 million has been disbursed 
to date. Construction of the permanent campus has been undertaken in 

227 | P a g e 
 
packages: Package I (boundary wall) and Package II (five buildings) are 
complete; Package III (seven buildings, including the Library, Convention 
Centre, and Administration building) is 98% complete; and the scope of 
Package IV is under consideration. The Committee note that the capital works 
have been significantly delayed on account of Gram Sabha land issues, forest 
land encumbrances, geo-morphological complications, the COVID-19 
pandemic, and frequent construction bans imposed by the National Green 
Tribunal. 
The Committee note that SAU’s operational budget is contributed by all 
eight SAARC member states, with India’s share standing at 59.8%. The 
Committee reiterate their concern, expressed in successive reports, that 
several SAARC member states continue to default on their operational budget 
contributions. The Ministry has itself acknowledged that these pending dues 
affect day-to-day operations, constrain SAU’s ability to project itself as a well-
managed institution, and limit its academic collaborations with global partners. 
The Committee are disheartened to note that despite repeated follow-ups at 
SAU Governing Board meetings and SAARC Programming Committee 
meetings, the problem of default payments by member state has remained 
unresolved year after year. 
The Committee reiterate their concern that India bears 100% of the 
capital cost and 59.8% of the operational cost of SAU, a burden wholly 
inconsistent with the foundational premise of the University as a collectively 
owned and jointly managed regional institution. The Committee strongly 
recommend that the Government of India should take up the matter of pending 
dues with defaulting member states at the highest diplomatic level, going well 
beyond Governing Board and SAARC Programming Committee meetings, and 
engaging through bilateral channels at an appropriate senior level. The 
Committee further urge that Package III be completed without any further 
delay, that the scope and timeline for Package IV be finalized, and that a 
revised cost statement against the approved USD 198 million outlay be 
furnished. 

228 | P a g e 
 
56. The Committee note that South Asian University has made tangible 
academic progress since its establishment, growing from an initial intake of 50 
students in 2010-11 to a current intake of 1,105 in 2025-26, with total enrolment 
standing at 1,756. The University now offers 5 Bachelor’s, 12 Master’s, and 9 
Ph.D. programmes across disciplines including Computer Science, 
Economics, Biotechnology, International Relations, Sociology, Law, and 
Business Administration. The University has set an ambitious target of 4,000 
students by 2029-30. 
While welcoming this trajectory, the Committee, are concerned to note 
that this expansion in student numbers is occurring against a backdrop of 
chronic financial stress caused by member state defaults, which constrains 
faculty recruitment, scholarship offerings, and academic collaborations with 
global institutions. The Committee recommend that the Ministry should ensure 
that SAU’s expansion towards the 4,000-student target is matched by 
commensurate growth in faculty strength, academic infrastructure, residential 
capacity, and financial resources. A university that is growing rapidly in 
student numbers while facing operational funding uncertainty is particularly 
vulnerable to deterioration in the quality of its academic offering, which would 
defeat the very purpose for which SAU was established. The Committee 
recommend that the Ministry should identify and pursue specific steps, within 
the existing governance framework, to strengthen SAU’s academic profile and 
international visibility, including by facilitating research partnerships with 
Indian universities and research institutions, extending SAARC Silver Jubilee 
Scholarships to a wider cohort of students, and supporting SAU’s participation 
in regional academic networks and knowledge-exchange platforms. SAU’s 
potential as a symbol of India’s commitment to regional integration and as a 
platform for people-to-people connectivity within South Asia can only be 
realized if the institution is demonstrably well-resourced, well-managed, and 
academically credible. 
 
 
 

229 | P a g e 
 
Assistance to autonomous bodies and institutions 
57. The Committee note that the Ministry of External Affairs provides 
Grants-in-Aid to autonomous bodies and institutions, principally the Indian 
Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR), the Indian Council of World Affairs 
(ICWA), and the Research and Information System for Developing Countries 
(RIS).  These institutions are not administrative extensions of the Ministry but 
are the intellectual and cultural infrastructure through which India’s diplomacy 
acquires depth, reach, and the capacity to engage the world beyond the formal. 
The total allocation to these institutions has grown from Rs. 369.85 crore in BE 
2024-25 to Rs. 451.19 crore in BE 2026-27. The Committee note that the 
Ministry has put in place an established accountability framework for these 
institutions.  
The Committee observe that while the budgetary allocation for this head 
has grown incrementally, the Committee are of the view that the increase is 
modest relative to the scale of India’s foreign policy objectives. Further, there 
is a lack of structured and outcome-oriented framework that links the research 
mandates of these institutions to the Ministry’s identified foreign policy 
priorities. The Committee urge the Ministry to establish a formal, structured 
mechanism for integrating the research outputs of ICWA and RIS into MEA’s 
foreign policy formulation process. The Committee further recommend that the 
Ministry, in coordination with ICCR, ICWA, and RIS, should develop a 
coordinated global outreach strategy that identifies countries of priority 
strategic importance, where the combined intellectual and cultural presence of 
India’s autonomous bodies will be systematically strengthened. 
 
Budgetary Allocation to Indian Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR) 
58.  The Committee acknowledge that Indian Council for Cultural Relations 
(ICCR) plays a pivotal role in projecting India’s soft power and advancing its 
cultural diplomacy through a wide spectrum of activities such as scholarships, 
cultural delegations, Indian Cultural Centres abroad, promotion of Indian 
languages, performing arts, and the International Day of Yoga. The Committee 

230 | P a g e 
 
note that the budgetary allocation for ICCR has been enhanced from Rs. 351.25 
crore in BE 2025-26 to Rs. 391.50 crore in BE 2026-27. However, ICCR had 
sought Rs. 410.21 crore for BE 2026-27, leaving a gap of Rs. 18.71 crore 
between the institution’s assessed requirement and the allocation made. The 
Ministry has stated that this enhanced allocation will support an expanded 
scholarship intake, a larger number of outgoing and incoming cultural 
delegations, the International Day of Yoga 2026, additional ICCR Chairs, and 
increased activities at Indian Cultural Centres.  The Committee further note 
that ICCR has virtually no independent revenue base. Its own earnings from 
auditorium and art gallery rentals amounted to only Rs. 95.43 lakh in 2024-25, 
making it almost wholly dependent on the Ministry’s grant for all its activities. 
The Ministry has itself acknowledged, in its reply to this Committee, that ICCR 
would need more budgetary allocations and resources to discharge its 
mandate fully. 
Keeping in view the broad and expanding mandate of ICCR as a frontline 
instrument of India’s soft power, the Committee are of the considered opinion 
that the current level of budgetary support needs to be suitably enhanced. The 
Committee have previously observed that ICCR’s allocation has historically 
remained in the range of Rs. 300-350 crore annually, and while the 
enhancement to Rs. 391.50 crore in BE 2026-27 is a welcome step, the 
Committee contend that such funding levels remain insufficient relative to the 
scale, ambition, and expanding scope of ICCR’s activities. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly reiterate their recommendation that ICCR’s budgetary 
allocation be raised to Rs. 500-600 crore in the forthcoming years, in a phased 
and planned manner, to enable ICCR to discharge its pivotal mandate of 
cultural outreach, educational diplomacy, and promotion of India’s soft power 
with the scale and quality that India’s rising global profile demands. In the 
immediate term, the Committee recommend that the Ministry address the 
unfunded gap of Rs. 18.71 crore at the Revised Estimates stage of 2026-27 so 
that ICCR’s expanded programme commitments are not curtailed. 
 
 
 

231 | P a g e 
 
ICCR Scholarships 
59. The Committee note that the ICCR scholarship programme, with 3,649 
students availing scholarships in 2025-26 under 21 schemes, is one of India’s 
most enduring and cost-effective soft power instruments. The current Prime 
Minister of Sri Lanka and the High Commissioner of Sri Lanka to India are both 
ICCR alumni, an illustration of the long-term diplomatic returns that the 
programme generates. The Committee endorse the Ministry’s stated intent to 
enhance total scholarship slots in academic year 2026-27 and urge that this 
expansion be accompanied by a strategic review of slot allocation across 
countries, with deliberate effort to expand reach into the Indo-Pacific, West 
Asia, Central Asia, and Latin America, where India’s strategic footprint is 
growing but ICCR’s scholarship presence remains limited. The Committee also 
recommend that ICCR strengthen its alumni engagement programme and 
maintain active networks with former scholarship holders, given the 
demonstrable diplomatic returns that well-connected alumni deliver. 
ICCR Cultural Centres 
 
60. The Committee are aware that the 38 Indian Cultural Centres (ICCs) 
serve as vital instruments in projecting India’s rich and diverse cultural 
heritage through a wide array of activities, including dance, music, yoga, 
languages, food, festivals, history, and contemporary cultural narratives. The 
Committee note that ICCR and MEA are in the process of reviewing the 
locations of India’s 38 Indian Cultural Centres abroad, with a view to opening 
new Centres in strategically important countries and ensuring that existing 
Centres are located where they can have the greatest impact.  
The previous DFG reports of the Committee had called for expedited 
operationalization of the Indian Cultural Centres in Washington DC and Paris. 
The Committee welcome the completion of renovation at the Swami 
Vivekananda Cultural Centre in Paris. The Committee are, however, deeply 
concerned that the Washington DC property, located in the capital of one of 
India’s most strategically significant bilateral partner, is now being 
recommended for sale on the basis of a 2024 Property Management Team 
assessment, without any clear alternative arrangement. The Committee place 
on record that USD 4,41,581.91 has already been expended on consultancy 
services for this property, that the United States hosts one of the world’s 
largest and most influential Indian diaspora communities, and that the absence 
of a dedicated ICCR Cultural Centre in Washington DC may represent a 
significant and conspicuous gap in India’s cultural diplomacy infrastructure. 
The Committee urge the Ministry to take necessary steps for providing 
institutional presence in Washington, United States and inform the Committee 
about it. 
Indian Council of World Affairs (ICWA) 

232 | P a g e 
 
61. The Committee note that the Indian Council of World Affairs, established 
in 1943 and accorded statutory status under the Indian Council of World 
Affairs Act, 2001, is one of India’s oldest and most distinguished institutions 
for research and dialogue on foreign policy. While the budgetary allocation of 
ICWA has witnessed an upward trend from Rs. 15.48 crore in BE 2025-26 to Rs. 
28.88 crore in BE 2026-27, the Committee observe the increase is primarily 
attributed to salaries ad capital expenditure. The Committee note that ICWA 
has been highly productive in recent years. in 2025 alone, the Council 
published 180 research papers, 12 special publications by eminent external 
scholars, and 9 books.  The Committee observe that ICWA’s schedule of 
bilateral and multilateral dialogues for 2026-27 is extensive, covering 17 
bilateral and multilateral engagements across Asia, Europe, Latin America, and 
the Gulf. The Committee observe that this level of convening activity 
represents a significant and strategically valuable contribution to India’s 
foreign policy ecosystem, and recommend that the Ministry ensure that 
ICWA’s programme commitments under its 2026-27 calendar are fully funded 
and that any supplementary programme requirements arising from ICWA’s 
chairship responsibilities are anticipated and provided for at the RE stage. At 
the same time, the Committee express grave concern at the failure to convene 
even a single Governing Council meeting, which raise great concern about its 
accountability.  
Research and Information System for Developing Countries (RIS) 
62. The Committee note that the Research and Information System for 
Developing Countries, established in 1983 under the Ministry of External 
Affairs, is a policy research institution of considerable standing whose work 
on trade, development cooperation, connectivity, South-South cooperation, 
and science diplomacy strengthen India’s positions at the WTO, BRICS, 
BIMSTEC, ASEAN, and other multilateral forums. The Committee note that 
RIS’s allocation has been increased from Rs. 19.51 crore in BE 2025-26 to Rs. 
24.87 crore in BE 2026-27, an increase of Rs. 5.36 crore, driven by India’s 
Chairship of BRICS and IORA and associated expanded programmatic 
commitments. RIS currently collaborates with approximately 351 institutions 
worldwide, 79 within India and 272 internationally. RIS has also established a 

233 | P a g e 
 
significant institutional infrastructure: DAKSHIN  (the Global South Centre of 
Excellence) was created at RIS with Government support; the ASEAN-India 
Centre supports Act East policy research; the Centre for Maritime Economy 
and Connectivity (CMEC) was established with Indian Ports Association 
support; and a new Natural Resources vertical is being introduced in FY 2026-
27, reflecting the growing strategic importance of critical minerals and energy 
security within India’s trade and geopolitical framework. 
The Committee are of the view that RIS’s eight thematic centres and its 
wide-ranging activities represent a formidable institutional architecture for 
multilateral engagement that is substantially underfunded relative to its 
potential. The Committee, therefore, advise the Ministry to conduct a 
systematic review of the resource requirements of RIS, with a view to 
providing dedicated and predictable funding. 
 
 
NEW DELHI                                        Dr. Shashi Tharoor                  
12  March, 2026                                                                                      Chairperson,                                
21 Phalguna, 1947 (Saka)                                          Committee on External Affairs

Key Cultural programmes & achievements in recent years by ICCR 
Cultural Programmes during India’s SCO Presidency: ICCR anchored in 
organizing the Gala Cultural programmes organized on various occasions during 
India’s SCO presidency like Cultural programmes on the sidelines of SCO-RATS 
Summit on 14 October 2022 in New Delhi, SCO Foreign Minister’s Meet on 4 May 
2023 in Goa showcasing the rich cultural heritage of India through performing art on 
each occasions.  
Festival of Russian Culture in India : A major bilateral Cultural exchange 
programme held in New Delhi, Kolkata and Mumbai in the month of November 2022 
featuring performances by renowned Russian artists, folk ensembles, and ballet 
troupe showcasing Russian music, dance and traditional art forms strengthening 
bilateral ties. 
Year of Connecting the World through Ramayana 2024: A year-long initiative 
involving international performances, workshops, and exhibitions on Ramayana 
strengthening Cultural connections with countries across the globe through shared 
cultural heritage.   
1st BIMSTEC Traditional Music Festival: In fulfillment of the commitment made by 
the Hon’ble Prime Minister during the BIMSTEC Summit held in Bangkok in April 
2025, ICCR organized the 1st BIMSTEC Traditional Music Festival in Kolkata & New 
Delhi during 31st July to 4th August 2025 featuring Joint Musical performance by 
artists from 7 BIMSTEC countries entitled ‘Saptasur, Seven Nations, One Melody’ 
– aimed to enhance people-to-people connections in the region through cultural and 
artistic collaboration. 
18th Pravasi Bharatiya Diwas : ICCR anchored in organizing the Cultural 
components of 18th PBD 2025 including the Gala Cultural show entitled 
“Sindhurarunam” at the PBD Convention Centre, Bhubaneswar on 9 January 2025. 
The specially curated Cultural performance visually showcased how the large Indian 
global diaspora reflects India’s outward-looking embrace of the world in a spirit of 
‘Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam’ beginning with the Sindhu-Saraswati Civilization till 
modern times. The story of India going out to the world and the world coming to India 
was presented in the form of a stirring audio-visual spectacle by 130 artists depicting 

272 | P a g e 
 
various Indian dance forms including 8 Indian classical dances and 9 folk dances 
also incorporating Puppetry and Aerial art forms. 
International Festival in Maha Kumbh Mela: In major achievements in the history 
of its Annual Festivals, ICCR organized the 10th edition of the India International 
Dance & Music Festival during 22-24 February 2025 at the Maha Kumbh Mela 
ground in Prayagraj featuring curated performance of 127 artists from 11 countries 
adding a vibrant cultural dimension to the grand spiritual gathering. This was the first 
time; the ICCR brought such a large international cultural festival to the Maha 
Kumbh.Mela. The Festival brought together diverse cultures through the universal 
language of dance and music fostering cultural dialogue, strengthens international 
ties and enriches India's artistic landscape. 
India International Folk Festival: ICCR organized the 9th edition of the annual 
India International Folk Festival in New Delhi & 11 other Indian cities during 30 
September to 12 October 2025 featuring cultural performance of 200 artists from 11 
countries showcasing the spirit of cultural unity and traditional artistry while fostering 
international understanding & cultural ties.  
India International Dance & Music Festival: ICCR organized the 11th India 
International Dance & Music Festival in New Delhi & 6 other Indian cities from 15-25 
January 2026 featuring Cultural performances by 149 artists from 8 countries. The 
festival aimed to promote cultural exchange and global unity through the language of 
music and dance bringing together artists from countries across the globe to 
celebrate diverse cultures, continuing the ICCR's mission of cultural diplomacy.

2. At the outset, the Chairperson extended a warm welcome to the Members of the 
Committee and the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs to the sitting of 
the Committee convened to take oral evidence of the representatives of the Ministry 
on Demands for Grants of the Ministry for the year 2026-27. He also drew attention of 

275 | P a g e 
 
the representatives of the Ministry to Direction 55 (1) of Directions by the Speaker, Lok 
Sabha.  
3. In his opening remarks, the Chairperson provided a detailed analysis of the 
overall budgetary allocations for the Ministry of External Affairs, drawing comparisons 
between the Budget Estimates (BE) for 2026-27 and the Revised Estimates (RE) for 
2025-26. He highlighted that the budgetary allocation of Rs. 22,118.97 crore for the 
financial year 2026-27 represents an increase of only 7.81% over BE 2025-26 - a 
allocation that was less than the 20 % mark which the Committee has been 
recommending over the years. He noted that the Ministry's share in the overall 
Government of India budget stands at a mere 0.41%, and that the Ministry ranks 23rd 
among all Central Government Ministries in terms of budgetary allocation for the 
current fiscal year. The Chairperson drew particular attention to the persistent decline 
in MEA's proportional share of the Union Budget - from 0.69% in 2017-18 to 0.41% in 
2026-27 and to the fact that India allocates approximately 0.06% of its GDP to foreign 
affairs, which compares unfavourably with comparable major economies. In light of 
India's growing global influence, its expanding diplomatic footprint, and the widening 
scope of the Ministry's development partnership, cultural diplomacy, and consular 
responsibilities, the Chairperson expressed serious concern regarding the adequacy 
of the budgetary provision in enabling the Ministry to effectively pursue the country's 
foreign policy objectives. 
4. The Foreign Secretary, after expressing his gratitude for the Committee's 
continued interest in and guidance to the Ministry, provided a comprehensive 
overview of the Ministry's budget for 2026-27. He elaborated on the Ministry's key 
achievements during the ongoing fiscal year, the challenges encountered in 
implementing projects abroad, particularly in neighbourhood countries affected by 
political and security disturbances and the actual expenditure trends of the previous 
year. He highlighted the near-complete utilization of the revised allocation in recent 
years and the integration of IMAS 2.0 with PFMS across Missions/Posts abroad as a 
significant step towards real-time expenditure monitoring. He also drew attention to 
the Ministry's development partnership portfolio, the progress of flagship bilateral 
projects in Bhutan, Nepal, Sri Lanka, Maldives, and other partner countries, and the 
steps being taken to address project implementation delays.  

276 | P a g e 
 
5. Thereafter, the Members raised pertinent queries covering a wide range of 
issues relating to the Ministry's mandate and budgetary priorities. The issues raised 
included, gap between MEA's budgetary demands and actual allocations; Cross-
country comparative assessment of foreign affairs budgets;  Line of Credit (LoC) 
provisioning through EXIM Bank within MEA's regular budget; demand-allocation 
differential; allocations and actual utilization under development cooperation sub-
heads; status of operationalization and timelines for the pan-India rollout of e-
Passports; expansion of Passport Seva Kendras (PSKs) and Post Office Passport 
Seva Kendras (POPSKs) to the remaining unserved Lok Sabha constituencies; 
deployment of the mPassport Police App in the remaining 12 States and Union 
Territories where it has not yet been launched; adequacy in budgetary allocations for 
diaspora-related functions of the Ministry; allocations for repatriation of destitutes, 
legal assistance to women in NRI marriages, and the Awareness Campaign budget; 
understaffing across vital departments at MEA Headquarters and at Missions and 
Posts abroad; funding for cultural exchanges and scholarship programmes conducted 
through the Indian Council for Cultural Relations (ICCR); status of construction, 
academic progress, financial sustainability, and contribution of partner countries to 
Nalanda University and South Asian University; Chabahar Port and so on.  
6.  In response to the queries raised by Members, the Foreign Secretary and the 
accompanying officials provided detailed clarifications on a range of issues. These 
included: the rationale for rationalization of expenditure under certain sub-heads; 
steps being taken to enhance coordination with the Ministry of Finance ahead of the 
budget preparation cycle; distinction between the Scheme and Non-Scheme 
classification of MEA's budget; monitoring mechanisms in place for implementation of 
international development projects,; lessons learnt from the implementation 
experience of the Passport Seva Programme; revenue earnings from passport and 
visa services; current status of the establishment of new Protector of Emigrants (PoE) 
offices; representation of women in senior positions within the Indian Foreign Service; 
and the range of welfare schemes and programmes being administered by the 
Ministry for the benefit of the Indian Diaspora abroad, including the functioning of the 
Indian Community Welfare Fund (ICWF).  

277 | P a g e 
 
7. The Chairperson, in his concluding remarks, underscored the Committee's 
conviction that India's foreign policy ambitions must be matched by commensurate 
financial resources, and expressed the hope that the Ministry would make a 
compelling, evidence-based case before the Ministry of Finance for a substantially 
enhanced allocation. He also directed that the Ministry should furnish written replies to 
all the points raised by Members during the course of the discussion to the Secretariat 
at the earliest. 
The witnesses then withdrew. 
The Committee then adjourned. 
A verbatim record of the proceedings has been kept.
